View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

Vector Willman and Array Bolt are the two most famous athletes of Byteforces. They are going to compete in a race with a distance of L meters today.

 [Image] 

Willman and Bolt have exactly the same speed, so when they compete the result is always a tie. That is a problem for the organizers because they want a winner. 

While watching previous races the organizers have noticed that Willman can perform only steps of length equal to w meters, and Bolt can perform only steps of length equal to b meters. Organizers decided to slightly change the rules of the race. Now, at the end of the racetrack there will be an abyss, and the winner will be declared the athlete, who manages to run farther from the starting point of the the racetrack (which is not the subject to change by any of the athletes). 

Note that none of the athletes can run infinitely far, as they both will at some moment of time face the point, such that only one step further will cause them to fall in the abyss. In other words, the athlete will not fall into the abyss if the total length of all his steps will be less or equal to the chosen distance L.

Since the organizers are very fair, the are going to set the length of the racetrack as an integer chosen randomly and uniformly in range from 1 to t (both are included). What is the probability that Willman and Bolt tie again today?


-----Input-----

The first line of the input contains three integers t, w and b (1 ≤ t, w, b ≤ 5·10^18) — the maximum possible length of the racetrack, the length of Willman's steps and the length of Bolt's steps respectively.


-----Output-----

Print the answer to the problem as an irreducible fraction [Image]. Follow the format of the samples output.

The fraction [Image] (p and q are integers, and both p ≥ 0 and q > 0 holds) is called irreducible, if there is no such integer d > 1, that both p and q are divisible by d.


-----Examples-----
Input
10 3 2

Output
3/10

Input
7 1 2

Output
3/7



-----Note-----

In the first sample Willman and Bolt will tie in case 1, 6 or 7 are chosen as the length of the racetrack.

The input will be stdin and you should print your solution to stdout


Now solve the problem and return the code.### Format: Read the inputs from stdin solve the problem and write the answer to stdout (do not directly test on the sample inputs). Enclose your code within delimiters as follows. Ensure that when the python program runs, it reads the inputs, runs the algorithm and writes output to STDOUT.
```python
# YOUR CODE HERE
```

### Answer: (use the provided format with backticks)

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have a race track of length L meters (chosen randomly from 1 to t)
2. Willman takes steps of length w meters
3. Bolt takes steps of length b meters
4. The winner is the one who can run farther without falling into an abyss
5. An athlete can only take steps where the total distance ≤ L
6. We need to find the probability that Willman and Bolt tie

## Key Insights

For a given track length L:
- Willman can run exactly k×w meters where k×w ≤ L, so the maximum distance he can reach is floor(L/w) × w
- Bolt can run exactly m×b meters where m×b ≤ L, so the maximum distance he can reach is floor(L/b) × b

They tie when: floor(L/w) × w = floor(L/b) × b

This means both athletes reach the same distance.

## Finding When They Tie

For Willman and Bolt to tie at distance d:
- d must be a multiple of both w and b, so d = lcm(w,b) × k for some integer k
- For a given k, the maximum distance they can reach is lcm(w,b) × k
- This distance must be ≤ L
- So for a fixed k, they tie when L ≥ lcm(w,b) × k, but L < lcm(w,b) × (k+1)

For each valid k, the values of L that make them tie are in the range [lcm(w,b) × k, lcm(w,b) × (k+1) - 1]

## Counting Valid L Values

We need to count how many values of L in [1, t] make Willman and Bolt tie.

For k ≥ 0, the tied values are in [lcm(w,b) × k, min(lcm(w,b) × (k+1) - 1, t)]

Let's say LCM = lcm(w,b)

The valid ranges are:
- [0, LCM-1]: no tie (both have distance 0)
- [LCM, 2×LCM-1]: tie at distance LCM
- [2×LCM, 3×LCM-1]: tie at distance 2×LCM
- And so on...

For d=6: L in [6,7) → L=6

Count = 3. Correct.

So the condition is that d must be a multiple of lcm(w,b), and for each such d, the number of L in [d, min(d+min(w,b), t+1)) that satisfy the floor conditions.

But since d is a multiple of w and b, the floor conditions do give exactly d in the intervals [d, d+w) and [d, d+b), so in [d, d+min(w,b)).

So the count is as before, but only for d = k * LCM where k is integer ≥0.

And for each such d, the count is max(0, min(d + min(w,b), t+1) - d)

So total_count = sum_{k=0}^{infinity} max(0, min(k*LCM + min(w,b), t+1) - k*LCM) for k*LCM ≤ t

But only when k*LCM ≤ t.

And the term is min(min(w,b), t+1 - k*LCM)

So total_count = sum_{k=0}^{floor(t/LCM)} min(min(w,b), t+1 - k*LCM)

For the example: t=7, w=1, b=2, LCM=2, min(w,b)=1

k=0: min(1, 8-0) = 1 → but d=0, L in [0,1) → no values from L≥1
k=1: d=2, min(1,8-2)=1 → L in [2,3) → L=2
k=2: d=4, min(1,8-4)=1 → L in [4,5) → L=4
k=3: d=6, min(1,8-6)=1 → L in [6,7) → L=6
k=4: d=8>7, stop

Total_count = 1+1+1 = 3. Correct.

For the first example: t=10, w=3, b=2, LCM=6, min(w,b)=2

k=0: min(2,11-0)=2 → L in [0,2) → L=1 (since L≥1) → count=1
k=1: d=6, min(2,11-6)=2 → L in [6,8) → L=6,7 → count=2
k=2: d=12>10, stop

Total_count = 1+2 = 3. Correct.

Perfect!

So algorithm:

1. Compute LCM of w and b
2. Compute m = min(w, b)
3. total_count = 0
4. k = 0
5. while k * LCM <= t:
6.     add min(m, t+1 - k*LCM) to total_count
7.     k += 1
8. total_count is the number of L in [1,t] where they tie
9. probability = total_count / t
10. Reduce the fraction to irreducible form.

But the values can be up to 5e18, so we cannot loop from k=0 to t/LCM which might be huge.

We need to compute the sum efficiently.

Total_count = sum_{k=0}^{k_max} min(m, t+1 - k*LCM) where k_max = floor(t / LCM)

This sum can be split at the point where t+1 - k*LCM = m.

That is, when k*LCM > t+1 - m, or k > (t+1 - m) / LCM

Let threshold = (t+1 - m) / LCM

For k from 0 to floor(threshold), min(m, t+1-k*LCM) = m
For k from floor(threshold)+1 to k_max, min(m, t+1-k*LCM) = t+1-k*LCM

But only if floor(threshold) >= 0.

Let k_max = t // LCM

Let k1 = floor((t+1 - m) / LCM)

Then:
- If k1 < 0: then for all k in [0, k_max], min(m, t+1-k*LCM) = t+1-k*LCM
- Else: 
  - For k in [0, k1]: add m
  - For k in [k1+1, k_max]: add (t+1 - k*LCM)

But k1 = floor((t+1 - m) / LCM)

The sum is:
- For k=0 to k1: contribution = m * (k1 + 1)
- For k=k1+1 to k_max: contribution = sum_{k=k1+1}^{k_max} (t+1 - k*LCM)

The second sum is: sum_{k=k1+1}^{k_max} (t+1 - k*LCM) = (k_max - k1) * (t+1) - LCM * sum_{k=k1+1}^{k_max} k

Sum of k from k1+1 to k_max = (k_max - k1) * (k1 + k_max + 1) // 2

But need to be careful with integer division.

Actually:
sum_{k=a}^{b} k = (b-a+1) * (a+b) // 2

So sum_{k=k1+1}^{k_max} k = (k_max - k1) * (k1 + k_max + 1) // 2

Therefore:
second_sum = (k_max - k1) * (t+1) - LCM * (k_max - k1) * (k1 + k_max + 1) // 2

But this is integer arithmetic.

We need to ensure no overflow.

Since t, w, b up to 5e18, LCM might be huge, so k_max might be small.

In worst case, if w and b are large, LCM is large, so k_max is small.

If w and b are small, LCM might be small.

But in any case, k_max = t // LCM, and since LCM >= max(w,b) >= 1, k_max <= t, but in practice, if LCM is large, k_max is small.

However, if LCM is 1, and t is 5e18, then k_max is 5e18, which is too large to loop.

So we need the formula.

Let k_max = t // LCM

Let k1 = (t+1 - m) // LCM  # floor division

Then:
- If k1 < 0: then k1 = -1, and we use only the second part.
- If k1 >= k_max: then k1+1 > k_max, so only first part.

Actually, k1 = floor((t+1 - m) / LCM)

We only sum for k from 0 to k_max.

So:
- If k1 < 0: then for all k in [0, k_max], min(m, t+1-k*LCM) = t+1-k*LCM
- Else if k1 >= k_max: then for all k in [0, k_max], min(m, t+1-k*LCM) = m
- Else: first part for k=0 to k1, second part for k=k1+1 to k_max

Then total_count = first_part + second_part

first_part = m * (k1 + 1)

second_part = sum_{k=k1+1}^{k_max} (t+1 - k*LCM)

= (k_max - k1) * (t+1) - LCM * sum_{k=k1+1}^{k_max} k
= (k_max - k1) * (t+1) - LCM * (k_max - k1) * (k1 + k_max + 1) // 2

This is integer, and the division by 2 is exact because (k1+k_max+1) and (k_max-k1) might be same parity.

Actually, the sum of k from a to b is (b-a+1)(a+b)//2, which is integer.

So we can compute it.

In Python, we can use math.gcd.

- This happens when k*LCM > t - m + 1

- For k=0: count_0 = max(0, min(m, t+1) - 1)
- For k=1 to k1: count_k = m
- For k=k1+1 to k_max: count_k = t - k*LCM + 1

But only if k1 >= 1.

So:

Let k_max = t // LCM

Let k1 = (t - m + 1) // LCM   # floor division

Then:
- count_0 = max(0, min(m, t+1) - 1)
- if k1 < 0: then no k>=1
- else:
  - for k=1 to k1: each contributes m
  - for k=k1+1 to k_max: each contributes (t - k*LCM + 1)

The sum for k=1 to k1 is: m * k1 (since k from 1 to k1, inclusive)

The sum for k=k1+1 to k_max is: sum_{k=k1+1}^{k_max} (t - k*LCM + 1)
= (k_max - k1) * (t+1) - LCM * sum_{k=k1+1}^{k_max} k
= (k_max - k1) * (t+1) - LCM * (k_max - k1) * (k1 + k_max + 1) // 2

With the same formula.

But we must ensure k1 >= 0 and k1 < k_max.

Also, if k1 >= k_max, then the second sum is empty.

Now implement with care for large numbers and integer division.

Steps:

1. Read t, w, b
2. Compute g = gcd(w,b)
3. LCM = w * b // g
4. m = min(w, b)
5. k_max = t // LCM
6. count_0 = max(0, min(m, t+1) - 1)
7. k1 = (t - m + 1) // LCM   # floor division
8. total_count = count_0
9. if k1 >= 1:
10.   total_count += m * k1
11. if k1 < k_max:
12.   # k from k1+1 to k_max
13.   k2 = k1 + 1
14.   k3 = k_max
15.   count_second = (k3 - k2 + 1) * (t + 1) - LCM * (k3 - k2 + 1) * (k2 + k3 + 1) // 2
16.   total_count += count_second
17. if total_count == 0:
18.   print("0/1")
19. else:
20.   g2 = gcd(total_count, t)
21.   num = total_count // g2
22.   den = t // g2
23.   print(f"{num}/{den}")

We must use integer arithmetic and be careful with division.

Note that in step 15, (k3 - k2 + 1) * (t+1) - LCM * (k3 - k2 + 1) * (k2 + k3 + 1) // 2
= (k3 - k2 + 1) * ( (t+1) - LCM * (k2 + k3 + 1) // 2 )

But the division by 2 is exact because (k2 + k3 + 1) might be even or odd.

Actually, the sum of integers from a to b is (b-a+1)(a+b)//2, which is integer.

So we can compute it as:
n = k3 - k2 + 1
sum_k = n * (k2 + k3 + 1) // 2
count_second = n * (t+1) - LCM * sum_k

This is an integer.

Now implement.

We need gcd function.

Note: when t is 0, but t>=1, so no issue.

Also, if LCM is very large, k_max might be 0.

Let me write the code.

We must be cautious about overflow with numbers up to 5e18.

In the expression (k3 - k2 + 1) * (t+1), if k3-k2+1 is large, t+1 is 5e18, product might be 2.5e36, which is within Python int range (Python ints are arbitrary precision), so it's fine.

But in worst case, if LCM is 1, then k_max=t, which is 5e18, and k1 = (t-m+1)//1 = t-m+1, which is about 5e18, so we are not looping, just doing arithmetic.

The only potential issue is that (k2 + k3 + 1) might be large, but k2, k3 up to 5e18, so (k2+k3+1) up to 1e19, and multiplied by (k3-k2+1) which is up to 5e18, so product up to 5e36, which is acceptable in Python.

So we proceed.

One more edge: if m=0, but m=min(w,b) and w,b>=1, so m>=1.

Also, when t is small.

Let me code accordingly.

```python
import math

def gcd(a, b):
    while b:
        a, b = b, a % b
    return a

t, w, b = map(int, input().split())

# Compute LCM of w and b
g = gcd(w, b)
lcm_val = w * b // g

m = min(w, b)
k_max = t // lcm_val

# Count for k=0
count_0 = max(0, min(m, t + 1) - 1)

total_count = count_0

# For k from 1 to k1 (where k1 is the largest k such that k*lcm_val + m - 1 <= t)
k1 = (t - m + 1) // lcm_val

if k1 >= 1:
    total_count += m * k1

# For k from k1+1 to k_max (if any)
if k1 < k_max:
    n = k_max - k1  # number of terms
    # Sum of k from k1+1 to k_max
    # Sum = n * (first + last) / 2
    # first = k1+1, last = k_max
    sum_k = n * (k1 + 1 + k_max) // 2
    # For each k, count = t - k*lcm_val + 1
    # Sum = sum_{k=k1+1}^{k_max} (t - k*lcm_val + 1)
    # = n*(t+1) - lcm_val * sum_k
    count_second = n * (t + 1) - lcm_val * sum_k
    total_count += count_second

# Reduce fraction
if total_count == 0:
    print("0/1")
else:
    g2 = gcd(total_count, t)
    num = total_count // g2
    den = t // g2
    print(f"{num}/{den}")
```

I think this should work.

Now I'll write the final code.

Note: we need to import math for gcd.

But since we are using math.gcd, and it's available.

```python
import math

t, w, b = map(int, input().split())

# Compute LCM of w and b
g = math.gcd(w, b)
lcm_val = w * b // g

m = min(w, b)
k_max = t // lcm_val

# Count for k=0: values of L in [1, min(m, t+1)-1]
count_0 = max(0, min(m, t + 1) - 1)

total_count = count_0

# For k from 1 to k1 (k1 is the largest k such that k*lcm_val + m - 1 <= t)
k1 = (t - m + 1) // lcm_val

if k1 >= 1:
    total_count += m * k1

# For k from k1+1 to k_max (if any)
if k1 < k_max:
    n = k_max - k1
    # Sum of k from k1+1 to k_max = n * (first + last) // 2
    # where first = k1+1, last = k_max
    sum_k = n * (k1 + 1 + k_max) // 2
    # The sum of (t - k*lcm_val + 1) for k in [k1+1, k_max]
    count_second = n * (t + 1) - lcm_val * sum_k
    total_count += count_second

# Output the reduced fraction
if total_count == 0:
    print("0/1")
else:
    g2 = math.gcd(total_count, t)
    num = total_count // g2
    den = t // g2
    print(f"{num}/{den}")
```

Submitted Code

View Extracted Code 
import math

t, w, b = map(int, input().split())

# Compute LCM of w and b
g = math.gcd(w, b)
lcm_val = w * b // g

m = min(w, b)
k_max = t // lcm_val

# Count for k=0: values of L in [1, min(m, t+1)-1]
count_0 = max(0, min(m, t + 1) - 1)

total_count = count_0

# For k from 1 to k1 (k1 is the largest k such that k*lcm_val + m - 1 <= t)
k1 = (t - m + 1) // lcm_val

if k1 >= 1:
    total_count += m * k1

# For k from k1+1 to k_max (if any)
if k1 < k_max:
    n = k_max - k1
    # Sum of k from k1+1 to k_max = n * (first + last) // 2
    # where first = k1+1, last = k_max
    sum_k = n * (k1 + 1 + k_max) // 2
    # The sum of (t - k*lcm_val + 1) for k in [k1+1, k_max]
    count_second = n * (t + 1) - lcm_val * sum_k
    total_count += count_second

# Output the reduced fraction
if total_count == 0:
    print("0/1")
else:
    g2 = math.gcd(total_count, t)
    num = total_count // g2
    den = t // g2
    print(f"{num}/{den}")

Vector Willman and Array Bolt are the two most famous athletes of Byteforces. They are going to compete in a race with a distance of L meters today.

 [Image]

While watching previous races the organizers have noticed that Willman can perform only steps of length equal to w meters, and Bolt can perform only steps of length equal to b meters. Organizers decided to slightly change the rules of the race. Now, at the end of the racetrack there will be an abyss, and the winner will be declared the athlete, who manages to run farther from the starting point of the the racetrack (which is not the subject to change by any of the athletes). 

Note that none of the athletes can run infinitely far, as they both will at some moment of time face the point, such that only one step further will cause them to fall in the abyss. In other words, the athlete will not fall into the abyss if the total length of all his steps will be less or equal to the chosen distance L.

Since the organizers are very fair, the are going to set the length of the racetrack as an integer chosen randomly and uniformly in range from 1 to t (both are included). What is the probability that Willman and Bolt tie again today?


-----Input-----

The first line of the input contains three integers t, w and b (1 ≤ t, w, b ≤ 5·10^18) — the maximum possible length of the racetrack, the length of Willman's steps and the length of Bolt's steps respectively.


-----Output-----

Print the answer to the problem as an irreducible fraction [Image]. Follow the format of the samples output.

The fraction [Image] (p and q are integers, and both p ≥ 0 and q > 0 holds) is called irreducible, if there is no such integer d > 1, that both p and q are divisible by d.


-----Examples-----
Input
10 3 2

Output
3/10

Input
7 1 2

Output
3/7

## Problem Understanding

1. Two athletes compete in a race of length L meters
2. Willman takes steps of length `w` meters, Bolt takes steps of length `b` meters
3. They can only go as far as the total distance without falling into an abyss
4. The race distance L is chosen uniformly from 1 to t
5. We need to find the probability that Willman and Bolt tie (i.e., both reach the same maximum distance)

## Key Insight

For a given race distance L:
- Willman can go at most `k * w` where `k * w ≤ L`, so maximum distance = `w * floor(L/w)`
- Bolt can go at most `m * b` where `m * b ≤ L`, so maximum distance = `b * floor(L/b)`

They tie when `w * floor(L/w) = b * floor(L/b)`

This means both athletes reach the same distance, which happens when both can make exactly the same number of steps such that their total distance equals the same value.

For a given L, Willman can achieve distance `w * floor(L/w)` and Bolt can achieve `b * floor(L/b)`.

They tie when `w * floor(L/w) = b * floor(L/b)`.

But we need to count how many values of L in [1,t] satisfy this condition.

## Finding when they tie

Let's think about when `w * floor(L/w) = b * floor(L/b)`.

This happens when both athletes achieve the same distance. Notice that:
- Willman achieves distance `d` when `d` is a multiple of `w` and `d ≤ L`
- Bolt achieves distance `d` when `d` is a multiple of `b` and `d ≤ L`

For a given L, they tie if and only if the maximum reachable distance by both athletes is the same.

Now code:

Note: we need to be careful with integer division and large numbers.

We'll use math.gcd.

But note: when min_wb == 1, min(t, min_wb-1) = min(t,0) = 0.

Also, when t is small, k_max might be negative.

For example, if t - min_wb + 1 < 0, then k_max = (t - min_wb + 1) // lcm <= 0.

So our condition k_max>=1 will not trigger.

Also, if m = t // lcm = 0, then k_max < m might be false (0<0 false).

So it's safe.

But what if lcm is very large such that t // lcm = 0? Then m=0, and k_max = (t - min_wb + 1) // lcm <= (t) // lcm = 0, so k_max <= 0, so k_max < m is false.

So no issue.

Let me write the code.

Important: when min_wb == 1, count = 0, which is correct.

We need to handle the case where min_wb - 1 might be negative, but min_wb >=1, so min_wb-1 >=0.

So min(t, min_wb-1) is always >=0.

Now code:

```python
import math

def solve():
    t, w, b = map(int, input().split())
    
    # Compute gcd and lcm
    g = math.gcd(w, b)
    lcm = w * b // g
    min_wb = min(w, b)
    
    # Count for k=0 (d=0)
    count = min(t, min_wb - 1) if min_wb > 1 else 0
    
    # For k>=1
    k_max = (t - min_wb + 1) // lcm
    m = t // lcm
    
    if k_max >= 1:
        count += k_max * min_wb
    
    if k_max < m:
        n = m - k_max
        # Sum of k from k_max+1 to m
        sum_k = (m * (m + 1) // 2) - (k_max * (k_max + 1) // 2)
        count += n * (t + 1) - lcm * sum_k
    
    # Reduce fraction count/t
    gcd_val = math.gcd(count, t)
    count //= gcd_val
    t //= gcd_val
    
    print(f"{count}/{t}")

solve()
```

But wait, what about the case when w and b are both 1? We already tested.

One more edge: when min_wb = 1, count starts with 0, then for k>=1, we add terms.

But when min_wb = 1, then for any k>=1, we have d = k*1 = k, and d + min_wb - 1 = k + 1 - 1 = k
So if k <= t, then d + min_wb - 1 = k <= t, so we add min_wb = 1 for each k from 1 to k_max.

k_max = (t - 1 + 1) // 1 = t // 1 = t
m = t // 1 = t
So we add k_max * min_wb = t * 1 = t
Then k_max < m? t < t? false
So count = 0 + t = t
Fraction t/t = 1. Correct.

Another edge: when w=2, b=4, t=5
min_wb=2, lcm=4
count = min(5,1)=1
k_max = (5-2+1)//4 = 4//4=1
m = 5//4=1
k_max>=1: add 1*2=2
k_max < m? 1<1? false
count=3
gcd(3,5)=1, so 3/5

Check manually:
L=1: 2*0=0, 4*0=0 → tie
L=2: 2*1=2, 4*0=0 → not tie
L=3: 2*1=2, 4*0=0 → not
L=4: 2*2=4, 4*1=4 → tie
L=5: 2*2=4, 4*1=4 → tie

So L=1,4,5 → count=3. Correct.

Perfect.

Final code:

```python
import math

t, w, b = map(int, input().split())

# Compute gcd and lcm
g = math.gcd(w, b)
lcm = w * b // g
min_wb = min(w, b)

# Count for k=0 (d=0)
count = min(t, min_wb - 1) if min_wb > 1 else 0

# For k>=1
k_max = (t - min_wb + 1) // lcm
m = t // lcm

if k_max >= 1:
    count += k_max * min_wb

if k_max < m:
    n = m - k_max
    # Sum of k from k_max+1 to m
    sum_k = (m * (m + 1) // 2) - (k_max * (k_max + 1) // 2)
    count += n * (t + 1) - lcm * sum_k

# Reduce fraction count/t
g_val = math.gcd(count, t)
count //= g_val
t //= g_val

print(f"{count}/{t}")
```

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

Vector Willman and Array Bolt are the two most famous athletes of Byteforces. They are going to compete in a race with a distance of L meters today.

 [Image] 

Willman and Bolt have exactly the same speed, so when they compete the result is always a tie. That is a problem for the organizers because they want a winner. 

While watching previous races the organizers have noticed that Willman can perform only steps of length equal to w meters, and Bolt can perform only steps of length equal to b meters. Organizers decided to slightly change the rules of the race. Now, at the end of the racetrack there will be an abyss, and the winner will be declared the athlete, who manages to run farther from the starting point of the the racetrack (which is not the subject to change by any of the athletes). 

Note that none of the athletes can run infinitely far, as they both will at some moment of time face the point, such that only one step further will cause them to fall in the abyss. In other words, the athlete will not fall into the abyss if the total length of all his steps will be less or equal to the chosen distance L.

Since the organizers are very fair, the are going to set the length of the racetrack as an integer chosen randomly and uniformly in range from 1 to t (both are included). What is the probability that Willman and Bolt tie again today?


-----Input-----

The first line of the input contains three integers t, w and b (1 ≤ t, w, b ≤ 5·10^18) — the maximum possible length of the racetrack, the length of Willman's steps and the length of Bolt's steps respectively.


-----Output-----

Print the answer to the problem as an irreducible fraction [Image]. Follow the format of the samples output.

The fraction [Image] (p and q are integers, and both p ≥ 0 and q > 0 holds) is called irreducible, if there is no such integer d > 1, that both p and q are divisible by d.


-----Examples-----
Input
10 3 2

Output
3/10

Input
7 1 2

Output
3/7



-----Note-----

In the first sample Willman and Bolt will tie in case 1, 6 or 7 are chosen as the length of the racetrack.

The input will be stdin and you should print your solution to stdout

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. Two athletes compete in a race of length L meters
2. Willman takes steps of length w meters, Bolt takes steps of length b meters
3. An athlete can only go as far as the total step length ≤ L (they won't fall into the abyss)
4. The race length L is chosen uniformly from 1 to t
5. We need to find the probability that both athletes go the same distance (tie)

## Key Insight

For a given racetrack length L, Willman can go at most floor(L/w) * w meters, and Bolt can go at most floor(L/b) * b meters.

They tie when: floor(L/w) * w = floor(L/b) * b

Let's think about when this happens:
- Willman goes k*w where k = floor(L/w)
- Bolt goes m*b where m = floor(L/b)
- They tie when k*w = m*b

This means k*w = m*b, or k*w is a common multiple of w and b.

The least common multiple (LCM) of w and b is LCM(w,b) = (w*b)/GCD(w,b).

So for a given L, they tie when L is such that floor(L/w) * w = floor(L/b) * b = some common multiple of w and b.

Actually, let me reconsider. They tie when the distance they both cover is the same. This happens when they both reach the same point on the track.

For a given L, Willman covers the maximum distance that is a multiple of w and ≤ L.
Similarly, Bolt covers the maximum distance that is a multiple of b and ≤ L.

They tie when these two maximum distances are equal.

This happens when L ≥ k*w and L ≥ k*b for the same k, but also when k*w = k*b (which only happens if w=b).

Wait, let me think differently. They tie when the largest multiple of w that is ≤ L equals the largest multiple of b that is ≤ L.

- Bolt's distance = (L // b) * b

They tie when (L // w) * w = (L // b) * b

Let's say this common distance is D. Then D must be a multiple of both w and b, so D must be a multiple of LCM(w,b).

For a given D = LCM(w,b) * k (for k ≥ 1), the athletes will tie when D ≤ L and both can reach D.

For Willman to reach D: D ≤ L and D % w == 0
For Bolt to reach D: D ≤ L and D % b == 0

Since D is a multiple of both w and b, both conditions are satisfied when D ≤ L.

When will they not reach D? When L < D.

So for a given k, the distance D = k * LCM(w,b) is reachable by both athletes when L ≥ D.

Yes! Because if they tie at some distance D, then D must be a multiple of both w and b (since the multiples of w grow by w and multiples of b grow by b), so D must be a multiple of LCM(w,b).

- Willman stops at 6 when L ≥ 6 and L < 6+3 = 9
- Bolt stops at 6 when L ≥ 6 and L < 6+2 = 8

So both stop at 6 when L ≥ 6 and L < 8, i.e., L = 6,7.

Plus, when L < min(w,b) = 2, both stop at 0? For L=1: both stop at 0.

For L < min(w,b), both stop at 0, so they tie.

So the tie points are:
- For L < min(w,b): both stop at 0 → tie
- For each multiple M of LCM(w,b) = 6: both stop at M when L ≥ M and L < M + min(w,b)

In this case:
- M=0: L < min(3,2)=2 → L=1
- M=6: L ≥ 6 and L < 6+2=8 → L=6,7

That gives us L=1,6,7 → perfect!

So general solution:
1. Let M = LCM(w,b) = (w*b)/GCD(w,b)
2. The tie occurs when:
   - L < min(w,b): both cover 0 → tie
   - L ≥ M and L < M + min(w,b): both cover M → tie  
   - L ≥ 2M and L < 2M + min(w,b): both cover 2M → tie
   - etc.

But we need to stop when M*k > t.

So the total number of favorable L values is:
- Count of L in [1, min(w,b)-1] (when L < min(w,b))
- Plus, for each k ≥ 1 such that M*k ≤ t, count of L in [M*k, min(M*k + min(w,b) - 1, t))

The count for each k is: max(0, min(M*k + min(w,b) - 1, t) - M*k + 1)

But we need to be careful about the range.

Actually, for each k ≥ 1:
- The range is [M*k, min(M*k + min(w,b) - 1, t)]
- The length of this range is: max(0, min(M*k + min(w,b) - 1, t) - M*k + 1)

For k=0: L in [0, min(w,b)-1], but L starts from 1, so L in [1, min(w,b)-1] if min(w,b) > 1
If min(w,b) = 1, then [1, 0] is empty.

So final count:
- count = 0
- If min(w,b) > 1: count += min(w,b) - 1
- For k = 1, 2, ... while M*k ≤ t:
  - end = min(M*k + min(w,b) - 1, t)
  - if end >= M*k:
    - count += end - M*k + 1

But wait, when k=0, we have the case L < min(w,b). That's when both athletes reach 0.

For L=1,2,...,min(w,b)-1: both reach 0 → tie.

So the count is (min(w,b) - 1) if min(w,b) > 1, else 0.

Then for each k ≥ 1, we add the range [M*k, min(M*k + min(w,b) - 1, t)].

The total favorable L values is the sum.

Total possible L values is t.

Therefore, probability = favorable / t.

But we need to output as irreducible fraction.

However, t can be up to 5*10^18, so we can't iterate over k.

We need to find the sum efficiently.

Let me define:
- Let g = GCD(w,b)
- Let LCM = w*b // g  (but we need to be careful about overflow)

Actually, w*b can be huge (up to 25*10^36), so we can't compute this directly.

But Python int handles big integers, so it should be fine.

We need to compute the sum:
- base = max(0, min(w,b) - 1)
- additional = 0
- k starts from 1
- while M*k <= t:
  - start = M*k
  - end = min(M*k + min(w,b) - 1, t)
  - length = end - start + 1
  - additional += length
  - k += 1

But this loop can run until k such that M*k > t, which could be up to t/M.

If M is small (like 1), this could be t iterations, which is 5*10^18, too slow.

We need a mathematical formula.

For the additional part (k ≥ 1), we have:
- For k such that M*k + min(w,b) - 1 ≤ t, the length is (min(w,b))
- For k such that M*k + min(w,b) - 1 > t, the length is (t - M*k + 1)

So we need to find the largest k where M*k ≤ t - (min(w,b) - 1)

Let me define:
- Let m = min(w,b)
- Let M = LCM(w,b)

For k such that M*k ≤ t - m + 1:
  - The range length is m
  - So contribution is m * floor((t - m + 1) / M)

For k such that M*k > t - m + 1 and M*k ≤ t:
  - The range length is (t - M*k + 1)

The range of k for the second case is from k_max = floor((t - m + 2) / M) + 1 to k_max2 = floor(t / M)

Wait, let me be precise:

The condition for full length m is: M*k + m - 1 ≤ t
So M*k ≤ t - m + 1
So k ≤ (t - m + 1) // M

So k from 1 to k1 = (t - m + 1) // M

Then for k from k1+1 to k2 = t // M (inclusive), we have partial length.

k2 = floor(t / M)

For k from k1+1 to k2:
- start = M*k
- end = t
- length = t - M*k + 1

Sum of this arithmetic sequence:
Sum = Σ(k=k1+1 to k2) (t - M*k + 1)
= Σ(k=k1+1 to k2) (t+1 - M*k)
= (k2 - k1) * (t+1) - M * Σ(k=k1+1 to k2) k
= (k2 - k1) * (t+1) - M * (k2*(k2+1)//2 - k1*(k1+1)//2)

But we need to be careful about when k1 < k1+1.

Also, we need to handle the case where (t - m + 1) < 0.

Let me simplify:

If t < m - 1, then no k ≥ 1 has M*k ≤ t, so only the base case.

Otherwise:

k_max_full = (t - m + 1) // M if t >= m - 1, else 0
But if t < m - 1, then base = 0

Actually, the base case (L < min(w,b)) is for L in [1, min(w,b)-1], so if min(w,b) <= 1, then base = 0.

So:
base = max(0, min(w,b) - 1)

k1 = (t - min(w,b) + 1) // M if t >= min(w,b) - 1, else 0
Actually, we need M*k ≤ t - min(w,b) + 1, so k ≤ (t - min(w,b) + 1) // M

So k1 = max(0, (t - min(w,b) + 1) // M)

But if t - min(w,b) + 1 < 0, then k1 = 0.

k1 = max(0, (t - min(w,b) + 1) // M)

For k from 1 to k1: each contributes min(w,b) to the count.

For k from k1+1 to k2 (where k2 = t // M): each contributes (t - M*k + 1)

k2 = t // M

So additional = k1 * min(w,b) + sum_{k=k1+1}^{k2} (t - M*k + 1)

The sum S = Σ_{k=k1+1}^{k2} (t+1 - M*k)
= (k2 - k1) * (t+1) - M * Σ_{k=k1+1}^{k2} k
= (k2 - k1) * (t+1) - M * (k2*(k2+1)//2 - k1*(k1+1)//2)

But we need to be careful about integer division and overflow.

We also need to compute GCD to get LCM.

Python's math.gcd works for big integers.

Let me test with the examples:

Example 1: t=10, w=3, b=2
- min(w,b)=2
- g = GCD(3,2)=1
- M = 3*2//1 = 6
- base = max(0, 2-1) = 1
- k1 = max(0, (10-2+1)//6) = (9)//6 = 1
- k2 = 10//6 = 1
- additional = 1*2 + sum_{k=2}^{1} ... = 1*2 + 0 = 2
- total favorable = 1 + 2 = 3
- probability = 3/10 ✓

Example 2: t=7, w=1, b=2
- min(w,b)=1
- g = GCD(1,2)=1
- M = 1*2//1 = 2
- base = max(0, 1-1) = 0
- k1 = max(0, (7-1+1)//2) = (7)//2 = 3
- k2 = 7//2 = 3
- additional = 3*1 + sum_{k=4}^{3} ... = 3 + 0 = 3
- total favorable = 3
- probability = 3/7 ✓

Perfect!

Now for the case where k1+1 > k2, the sum is 0.

Also, we need to handle when t < min(w,b)-1.

Now we need to implement this with big integers and reduce the fraction.

Total favorable = base + k1 * min_wb + sum_{k=k1+1}^{k2} (t+1 - M*k)

Sum = (k2 - k1) * (t+1) - M * (sum of k from k1+1 to k2)

Sum of k from k1+1 to k2 = (k2*(k2+1) - k1*(k1+1)) // 2

But we need to be careful about integer division and overflow, but Python handles big integers.

Finally, we have favorable = total_count, and total = t.

We need to reduce the fraction favorable/t to irreducible form.

Use GCD to divide both by gcd(favorable, t).

But favorable could be 0, need to handle.

Edge case: if favorable == 0, then answer is 0/1.

Otherwise, g = gcd(favorable, t), then result is (favorable//g) / (t//g)

Let me code accordingly.

Note: we need to compute GCD of w and b, and be careful about the LCM calculation which might be huge but Python handles big integers.

```python
import math

def solve():
    t, w, b = map(int, input().split())
    
    min_wb = min(w, b)
    g = math.gcd(w, b)
    
    # LCM(w, b) = w * b // GCD(w, b)
    # But w*b might be huge, but Python ints handle it
    lcm = w * b // g
    
    # Base case: L < min(w,b) → both reach 0
    base = max(0, min_wb - 1)
    
    # For k >= 1: when L >= k*lcm and L < k*lcm + min_wb
    # So range [k*lcm, min(k*lcm + min_wb - 1, t)]
    # Only if k*lcm <= t
    
    k1 = (t - min_wb + 1) // lcm if t >= min_wb - 1 else 0
    # k1 is the maximum k such that k*lcm + min_wb - 1 <= t
    # i.e., k <= (t - min_wb + 1) // lcm
    k1 = max(0, k1)
    
    k2 = t // lcm  # maximum k such that k*lcm <= t
    
    # If k1 >= k2, then no k from k1+1 to k2
    # Contribution from k=1 to k1: each contributes min_wb
    additional = k1 * min_wb
    
    # Contribution from k = k1+1 to k2
    if k1 + 1 <= k2:
        # Sum of (t + 1 - lcm * k) for k from k1+1 to k2
        # = (k2 - k1) * (t + 1) - lcm * (sum of k from k1+1 to k2)
        count_terms = k2 - k1
        sum_k = (k2 * (k2 + 1) // 2) - (k1 * (k1 + 1) // 2)
        additional += (count_terms * (t + 1)) - lcm * sum_k
    
    total_favorable = base + additional
    
    # If total_favorable is 0, probability is 0/1
    if total_favorable == 0:
        print("0/1")
    else:
        # Reduce fraction total_favorable / t
        gcd_val = math.gcd(total_favorable, t)
        p = total_favorable // gcd_val
        q = t // gcd_val
        print(f"{p}/{q}")

solve()
```

import math

def solve():
    t, w, b = map(int, input().split())
    
    min_wb = min(w, b)
    g = math.gcd(w, b)
    
    # LCM(w, b) = w * b // GCD(w, b)
    # But w*b might be huge, but Python ints handle it
    lcm = w * b // g
    
    # Base case: L < min(w,b) → both reach 0
    base = max(0, min_wb - 1)
    
    # For k >= 1: when L >= k*lcm and L < k*lcm + min_wb
    # So range [k*lcm, min(k*lcm + min_wb - 1, t)]
    # Only if k*lcm <= t
    
    k1 = (t - min_wb + 1) // lcm if t >= min_wb - 1 else 0
    # k1 is the maximum k such that k*lcm + min_wb - 1 <= t
    # i.e., k <= (t - min_wb + 1) // lcm
    k1 = max(0, k1)
    
    k2 = t // lcm  # maximum k such that k*lcm <= t
    
    # If k1 >= k2, then no k from k1+1 to k2
    # Contribution from k=1 to k1: each contributes min_wb
    additional = k1 * min_wb
    
    # Contribution from k = k1+1 to k2
    if k1 + 1 <= k2:
        # Sum of (t + 1 - lcm * k) for k from k1+1 to k2
        # = (k2 - k1) * (t + 1) - lcm * (sum of k from k1+1 to k2)
        count_terms = k2 - k1
        sum_k = (k2 * (k2 + 1) // 2) - (k1 * (k1 + 1) // 2)
        additional += (count_terms * (t + 1)) - lcm * sum_k
    
    total_favorable = base + additional
    
    # If total_favorable is 0, probability is 0/1
    if total_favorable == 0:
        print("0/1")
    else:
        # Reduce fraction total_favorable / t
        gcd_val = math.gcd(total_favorable, t)
        p = total_favorable // gcd_val
        q = t // gcd_val
        print(f"{p}/{q}")

Vector Willman and Array Bolt are the two most famous athletes of Byteforces. They are going to compete in a race with a distance of L meters today.

 [Image] 

Willman and Bolt have exactly the same speed, so when they compete the result is always a tie. That is a problem for the organizers because they want a winner. 

While watching previous races the organizers have noticed that Willman can perform only steps of length equal to w meters, and Bolt can perform only steps of length equal to b meters. Organizers decided to slightly change the rules of the race. Now, at the end of the racetrack there will be an abyss, and the winner will be declared the athlete, who manages to run farther from the starting point of the the racetrack (which is not the subject to change by any of the athletes). 

Note that none of the athletes can run infinitely far, as they both will at some moment of time face the point, such that only one step further will cause them to fall in the abyss. In other words, the athlete will not fall into the abyss if the total length of all his steps will be less or equal to the chosen distance L.

Since the organizers are very fair, the are going to set the length of the racetrack as an integer chosen randomly and uniformly in range from 1 to t (both are included). What is the probability that Willman and Bolt tie again today?


-----Input-----

The first line of the input contains three integers t, w and b (1 ≤ t, w, b ≤ 5·10^18) — the maximum possible length of the racetrack, the length of Willman's steps and the length of Bolt's steps respectively.


-----Output-----

Print the answer to the problem as an irreducible fraction [Image]. Follow the format of the samples output.

The fraction [Image] (p and q are integers, and both p ≥ 0 and q > 0 holds) is called irreducible, if there is no such integer d > 1, that both p and q are divisible by d.


-----Examples-----
Input
10 3 2

Output
3/10

Input
7 1 2

Output
3/7

After analyzing the known solution pattern for such problems:

The answer is:

Actually, the standard solution for this problem is:

Let g = gcd(w,b), lcm = w*b // g

For a fixed k, we need floor(L/w) * w = lcm*k and floor(L/b) * b = lcm*k.

This means w * floor(L/w) = lcm*k and b * floor(L/b) = lcm*k.

Similarly, floor(L/b) = (lcm*k) / b = (w/g)*k

- L < w * (b/g)*k + w and L < b * (w/g)*k + b

Wait, floor(L/w) = (b/g)*k means w * (b/g)*k ≤ L < w * (b/g)*k + w

Similarly, floor(L/b) = (w/g)*k means b * (w/g)*k ≤ L < b * (w/g)*k + b

For the values to be equal, we need both conditions.

So L must be in the intersection:
max(w*(b/g)*k, b*(w/g)*k) ≤ L < min(w*(b/g)*k + w, b*(w/g)*k + b)

Note that w*(b/g)*k = (w*b)/g * k = lcm * k
Similarly, b*(w/g)*k = lcm * k

So the intersection is:
lcm*k ≤ L < min(lcm*k + w, lcm*k + b)

So L in [lcm*k, lcm*k + min(w,b) - 1]

For k=0: [0, min(w,b)-1], so L in [0, min(w,b)-1]
For k=1: [lcm, lcm + min(w,b) - 1]

But for k=0, if L in [0, min(w,b)-1], both reach 0 → tie
For k≥1, L in [lcm*k, lcm*k + min(w,b) - 1], both reach lcm*k → tie

In example w=3,b=2:
g=gcd(3,2)=1, lcm=6, min(w,b)=2
k=0: [0,1] → L=0,1
k=1: [6,7] → L=6,7
So total ties: L=1 (since L≥1), L=6,7 → L=1,6,7 → matches!

For w=1,b=2,t=7:
g=gcd(1,2)=1, lcm=2, min(w,b)=1
k=0: [0,0] → L=0
k=1: [2,2] → L=2
k=2: [4,4] → L=4
k=3: [6,6] → L=6
k=4: [8,8] → but t=7, so up to 7

L in [0,0] → L=0 (not in [1,7])
L in [2,2], [4,4], [6,6] → L=2,4,6

But expected is 3/7 → so 3 values out of 7 → should be L=2,4,6 → that's 3 values → matches!

So the tie occurs at:
- For k=0: L in [0, min(w,b)-1] ∩ [1,t]
- For k≥1: L in [lcm*k, lcm*k + min(w,b) - 1] ∩ [1,t]

But for k=0: [0, min(w,b)-1] ∩ [1,t] = [1, min(w,b)-1] ∩ [1,t]
For k≥1: [lcm*k, lcm*k + min(w,b) - 1] ∩ [1,t]

The total number of ties is the sum over valid k of the length of these intervals.

Let's implement this.

## Step-by-step Solution

1. Calculate g = gcd(w,b), lcm = w*b // g
2. min_step = min(w,b)
3. total_ties = 0
4. For k=0: interval [1, min(w,b)-1] ∩ [1,t] has length max(0, min(w,b)-1)
5. For k≥1: interval [lcm*k, lcm*k + min_step - 1] has length min_step, but capped by t
6. We need to find the maximum k such that lcm*k + min_step - 1 ≤ t

For k≥1, the interval [lcm*k, lcm*k + min_step - 1] starts at lcm*k and goes to lcm*k + min_step - 1.
This interval is valid if lcm*k ≤ t.

The number of integers in [lcm*k, lcm*k + min_step - 1] is min_step, but we take only the part within [1,t].

So for k≥1, the contribution is:
- If lcm*k + min_step - 1 ≥ t, then the length is t - lcm*k + 1 (only if lcm*k ≤ t)
- Otherwise, the length is min_step

Actually, the number of integers in [lcm*k, lcm*k + min_step - 1] ∩ [1,t] is:
- If lcm*k > t: 0
- Else: min(min_step, t - lcm*k + 1)

For k=0, the interval [1, min(w,b)-1] has length max(0, min(w,b)-1)

So total ties = max(0, min(w,b)-1) + sum_{k=1}^{k_max} min(min_step, t - lcm*k + 1)

Where k_max is the largest k such that lcm*k ≤ t.

We can compute this sum efficiently.

However, note that min_step = min(w,b), and lcm*k grows exponentially, so the sum has at most O(t/lcm) terms, which can be very large if lcm is small.

But t can be up to 5e18, so we can't iterate over all k.

We need to find the sum of min(min_step, t - lcm*k + 1) for k=1,2,... until lcm*k > t.

We can split the sum into two parts:
1. When t - lcm*k + 1 ≥ min_step, i.e., lcm*k ≤ t - min_step + 1
2. When t - lcm*k + 1 < min_step, i.e., lcm*k > t - min_step + 1

For the first part, when k ≤ (t - min_step + 1) // lcm, each term contributes min_step.
For the second part, when k > (t - min_step + 1) // lcm, each term contributes t - lcm*k + 1.

Let K1 = (t - min_step + 1) // lcm
Then:
- Sum1 = min_step * K1
- Sum2 = sum_{k=K1+1}^{k_max} (t - lcm*k + 1)

Where k_max = t // lcm

Sum2 = sum_{k=K1+1}^{k_max} (t + 1 - lcm*k)
= (k_max - K1) * (t + 1) - lcm * sum_{k=K1+1}^{k_max} k
= (k_max - K1) * (t + 1) - lcm * [k_max*(k_max+1)//2 - K1*(K1+1)//2]

But we also have to cap k_max = t // lcm.

So total ties = max(0, min(w,b)-1) + Sum1 + Sum2

However, we must be careful about the range of k.

Let me write it step by step:

1. g = gcd(w,b)
2. lcm = w * b // g  (must be computed carefully to avoid overflow)
3. min_step = min(w,b)
4. k_max = t // lcm  (largest k such that lcm*k <= t)
5. ties_from_k0 = max(0, min_step - 1)  # [1, min_step-1]
6. ties_from_k1 = 0
7. If k_max >= 1:
   - K1 = (t - min_step + 1) // lcm
   - K1 = min(K1, k_max)  # K1 is the last k where t - lcm*k + 1 >= min_step
   - ties_from_k1 = min_step * (K1)  # for k=1 to K1
   - For k = K1+1 to k_max:
     - contribution = t - lcm*k + 1
     - sum = sum_{k=K1+1}^{k_max} (t + 1 - lcm*k)
     - This is an arithmetic series

Actually, the sum of (t+1 - lcm*k) from k=K1+1 to k_max is:
= sum_{k=K1+1}^{k_max} (t+1) - lcm * sum_{k=K1+1}^{k_max} k
= (k_max - K1) * (t+1) - lcm * (sum of integers from K1+1 to k_max)

Sum of integers from a to b = b*(b+1)//2 - (a-1)*a//2

So sum = (k_max - K1) * (t+1) - lcm * (k_max*(k_max+1)//2 - K1*(K1+1)//2)

But we need to be careful about integer division.

However, notice that when min_step = 0, but min(w,b) ≥ 1, so min_step ≥ 1.

Also, when w=0 or b=0, but the constraints say w,b ≥ 1.

So the code:

```python
import math

def gcd(a, b):
    while b:
        a, b = b, a % b
    return a

# Read input
t, w, b = map(int, input().split())

# Calculate gcd and lcm
g = gcd(w, b)
# Avoid overflow in lcm calculation
# Since w*b // g might be very large, but t is up to 5e18
# We can compute it if w*b // g <= t, otherwise we can set it to a large value
lcm_val = (w // g) * b  # This avoids overflow: w*b//g = (w//g)*b

# min_step = min(w,b)
min_step = min(w, b)

# Count ties for k=0
# L in [1, min_step-1] → count = max(0, min_step - 1)
ties_k0 = max(0, min_step - 1)

# Count ties for k≥1
k_max = t // lcm_val  # largest k such that lcm_val * k ≤ t

if k_max == 0:
    total_ties = ties_k0
else:
    # For each k≥1, the interval [lcm_val*k, lcm_val*k + min_step - 1]
    # The length is min(min_step, t - lcm_val*k + 1)
    
    # Find K1 such that lcm_val*(K1+1) > t - min_step + 1
    # So when k ≤ K1, we have t - lcm_val*k + 1 ≥ min_step
    # When k > K1, we have t - lcm_val*k + 1 < min_step
    
    # K1 = floor((t - min_step + 1) / lcm_val)
    K1 = (t - min_step + 1) // lcm_val
    
    # K1 is the largest k where the length is min_step
    # But k starts from 1, so we consider k from 1 to min(K1, k_max)
    
    # Number of k with full min_step
    k1_count = min(K1, k_max)
    ties_k1_full = min_step * k1_count
    
    # For k from k1_count+1 to k_max, each contributes (t - lcm_val*k + 1)
    k1_start = k1_count + 1
    k1_end = k_max
    
    if k1_start <= k1_end:
        # Sum of (t + 1 - lcm_val * k) for k from k1_start to k1_end
        # = (k1_end - k1_start + 1) * (t + 1) - lcm_val * sum(k from k1_start to k1_end)
        
        count = k1_end - k1_start + 1
        sum_k = (k1_start + k1_end) * count // 2
        ties_k1_partial = count * (t + 1) - lcm_val * sum_k
    else:
        ties_k1_partial = 0
    
    total_ties = ties_k0 + ties_k1_full + ties_k1_partial

# Total ties / t
# We need to output as irreducible fraction

# Find gcd of total_ties and t
if total_ties == 0:
    # Fraction 0/1
    print("0/1")
else:
    g_ties = math.gcd(total_ties, t)
    print(f"{total_ties // g_ties}/{t // g_ties}")
```

Wait, I need to be very careful about the bounds.

For k≥1:
- The interval is [lcm_val*k, lcm_val*k + min_step - 1]
- We only count values ≤ t
- So for each k, the valid values are from lcm_val*k to min(t, lcm_val*k + min_step - 1)

The number of values is max(0, min(t, lcm_val*k + min_step - 1) - lcm_val*k + 1)
= max(0, min(min_step, t - lcm_val*k + 1))

Yes.

For k from 1 to k_max, each contributes max(0, min_step, t - lcm_val*k + 1)

When t - lcm_val*k + 1 ≥ min_step, it contributes min_step
When t - lcm_val*k + 1 < min_step, it contributes t - lcm_val*k + 1

We split at k such that lcm_val*k ≤ t - min_step + 1
i.e., k ≤ (t - min_step + 1) // lcm_val

So:
- K1 = (t - min_step + 1) // lcm_val
- k from 1 to min(K1, k_max): contributes min_step
- k from K1+1 to k_max: contributes t - lcm_val*k + 1

Yes.

But we need to ensure k1_start ≤ k1_end.

Also, k_max = t // lcm_val

Let me test with the examples.

Example 1: t=10, w=3, b=2
g=gcd(3,2)=1, lcm_val=(3//1)*2=6, min_step=min(3,2)=2
ties_k0 = max(0, 2-1) = 1
k_max = 10 // 6 = 1

K1 = (10 - 2 + 1) // 6 = 9 // 6 = 1
k1_count = min(1,1) = 1
ties_k1_full = 2 * 1 = 2
k1_start = 2, k1_end = 1 → no contribution
total_ties = 1 + 2 = 3 → matches!

Example 2: t=7, w=1, b=2
g=gcd(1,2)=1, lcm_val=(1//1)*2=2, min_step=min(1,2)=1
ties_k0 = max(0, 1-1) = 0
k_max = 7 // 2 = 3

K1 = (7 - 1 + 1) // 2 = 7 // 2 = 3
k1_count = min(3,3) = 3
ties_k1_full = 1 * 3 = 3
k1_start = 4, k1_end = 3 → no contribution
total_ties = 0 + 3 = 3 → matches!

Perfect!

Now handle edge cases like when lcm_val is very large (greater than t), then k_max=0.

Also, when min_step=1, ties_k0=0.

The code should work.

But note: w*b // g might overflow if we do w*b first. Instead, we do (w//g)*b.

This is safe since g divides w.

Let me write the final code.

```python
import math

t, w, b = map(int, input().split())

# Calculate gcd
g = math.gcd(w, b)

# Calculate lcm = w * b / g without overflow
# w * b / g = (w / g) * b
lcm_val = (w // g) * b

# min_step = min(w, b)
min_step = min(w, b)

# Count ties for k=0: L in [1, min_step-1]
ties_k0 = max(0, min_step - 1)

# Count ties for k >= 1
k_max = t // lcm_val

if k_max == 0:
    total_ties = ties_k0
else:
    # Find K1 such that lcm_val * (K1+1) > t - min_step + 1
    # i.e., K1 = floor((t - min_step + 1) / lcm_val)
    K1 = (t - min_step + 1) // lcm_val
    
    # Number of k from 1 to min(K1, k_max) that contribute min_step
    k1_count = min(K1, k_max)
    ties_k1_full = min_step * k1_count
    
    # For k from k1_count+1 to k_max, each contributes (t - lcm_val*k + 1)
    k1_start = k1_count + 1
    k1_end = k_max
    
    if k1_start <= k1_end:
        count = k1_end - k1_start + 1
        # Sum of k from k1_start to k1_end = count * (k1_start + k1_end) // 2
        sum_k = count * (k1_start + k1_end) // 2
        ties_k1_partial = count * (t + 1) - lcm_val * sum_k
    else:
        ties_k1_partial = 0
        
    total_ties = ties_k0 + ties_k1_full + ties_k1_partial

# Output as irreducible fraction
if total_ties == 0:
    print("0/1")
else:
    g_ties = math.gcd(total_ties, t)
    print(f"{total_ties // g_ties}/{t // g_ties}")
```

You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

Vector Willman and Array Bolt are the two most famous athletes of Byteforces. They are going to compete in a race with a distance of L meters today.

 [Image] 

Willman and Bolt have exactly the same speed, so when they compete the result is always a tie. That is a problem for the organizers because they want a winner. 

While watching previous races the organizers have noticed that Willman can perform only steps of length equal to w meters, and Bolt can perform only steps of length equal to b meters. Organizers decided to slightly change the rules of the race. Now, at the end of the racetrack there will be an abyss, and the winner will be declared the athlete, who manages to run farther from the starting point of the the racetrack (which is not the subject to change by any of the athletes). 

Note that none of the athletes can run infinitely far, as they both will at some moment of time face the point, such that only one step further will cause them to fall in the abyss. In other words, the athlete will not fall into the abyss if the total length of all his steps will be less or equal to the chosen distance L.

Since the organizers are very fair, the are going to set the length of the racetrack as an integer chosen randomly and uniformly in range from 1 to t (both are included). What is the probability that Willman and Bolt tie again today?


-----Input-----

The first line of the input contains three integers t, w and b (1 ≤ t, w, b ≤ 5·10^18) — the maximum possible length of the racetrack, the length of Willman's steps and the length of Bolt's steps respectively.


-----Output-----

Print the answer to the problem as an irreducible fraction [Image]. Follow the format of the samples output.

The fraction [Image] (p and q are integers, and both p ≥ 0 and q > 0 holds) is called irreducible, if there is no such integer d > 1, that both p and q are divisible by d.


-----Examples-----
Input
10 3 2

Output
3/10

Input
7 1 2

Output
3/7



-----Note-----

In the first sample Willman and Bolt will tie in case 1, 6 or 7 are chosen as the length of the racetrack.

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. Two athletes run a race of length L meters
2. Willman takes steps of length w meters
3. Bolt takes steps of length b meters
4. An athlete can only run as far as the total distance is ≤ L (they don't fall into abyss)
5. The race length L is chosen uniformly from 1 to t
6. We need to find the probability that both athletes run the same distance

## Key Insight

For Willman to run exactly k meters, k must be a multiple of w, and k ≤ L.
For Bolt to run exactly k meters, k must be a multiple of b, and k ≤ L.

For a given L, both athletes will tie if they can both reach the same distance. This happens when there exists some distance k such that:
- k is a multiple of both w and b (so k is a multiple of LCM(w,b))
- k ≤ L

So for a given L, the athletes tie if there exists a multiple of LCM(w,b) that is ≤ L.

This means they tie when L ≥ some multiple of LCM(w,b).

Actually, let me reconsider. If there's a common multiple of w and b (which is the LCM), then both athletes can reach that distance. The largest distance both can reach is the greatest common multiple of w and b that is ≤ L.

For both to tie, they need to reach the same distance. The smallest such distance is LCM(w,b). So they tie if and only if L ≥ LCM(w,b).

Wait, that's not right either. If L < LCM(w,b), then they can't reach a common distance. If L ≥ LCM(w,b), then they can both reach LCM(w,b) meters, so they tie.

For example, if w=3, b=2, LCM=6. If L=1,2,3,4,5: no common multiple ≤ L. If L=6,7: they can both reach 6 meters, so they tie.

So for each L from 1 to t, they tie if and only if L ≥ LCM(w,b).

Therefore, the number of favorable outcomes = t - LCM(w,b) + 1 (if LCM(w,b) ≤ t), otherwise 0.

- If LCM(w,b) > t: no ties possible
- If LCM(w,b) ≤ t: ties occur for L = LCM(w,b), LCM(w,b)+1, ..., t → total of (t - LCM(w,b) + 1) cases

Total possible cases = t

So probability = (t - LCM(w,b) + 1) / t if LCM(w,b) ≤ t, else 0/1.

- For a given L, Willman's distance = (L // w) * w
- Bolt's distance = (L // b) * b

They tie when (L // w) * w = (L // b) * b.

So d = k * LCM(w,b) for some integer k ≥ 1, and k * LCM(w,b) ≤ L.

So for a given L, if there exists k such that k * LCM(w,b) ≤ L, then d = k * LCM(w,b) is achievable by both.

2. LCM(w,b) = w * b // g

If k=0, favorable=0.

But k = t // LCM(w,b), which could be 0.

Perfect.

So algorithm:
1. Read t, w, b
2. g = gcd(w, b)
3. lcm = w * b // g
4. min_val = min(w, b)
5. k = t // lcm
6. favorable = (min_val - 1) + min_val * k
7. g_simple = gcd(favorable, t)
8. p = favorable // g_simple
9. q = t // g_simple
10. Print p/q

Note: when favorable = 0, gcd(0,t) = t, so p=0, q=1.

We need to be careful about integer overflow. Since w, b, t ≤ 5e18, w*b can be up to 2.5e37 which might be too big for Python? 

But Python integers are arbitrary precision, so it should be fine.

However, w*b might be very large. We can avoid computing w*b directly by using the fact that lcm = w*b / gcd(w,b).

But we need to compute w*b // gcd(w,b). This is the same as (w//g) * (b//g) * g? 

Actually, w*b // g = (w//g) * (b//g) * g.

But since g divides w and b, w//g and b//g are integers.

So we can compute:
g = gcd(w,b)
w1 = w // g
b1 = b // g
lcm = w1 * b1 * g  # This avoids large intermediate product

But w1 * b1 * g = (w//g) * (b//g) * g = w * b // g, same as before.

But w1 * b1 might be large (up to (5e18)^2 / g), which could be huge.

However, in worst case g=1, so w1*b1 ~ 2.5e36, which is fine in Python.

So we proceed.

Let me implement:

Edge cases:
- If w=b, then lcm = w, min_val = w
- If w=1,b=2,t=1: we have favorable = 0 + 1*0 = 0 → 0/1 ✓

```python
import math

def gcd(a, b):
    while b:
        a, b = b, a % b
    return a

t, w, b = map(int, input().split())

# Compute gcd of w and b
g = math.gcd(w, b)

# Compute LCM(w, b) = w * b / gcd(w, b)
# To avoid overflow, compute as (w // g) * (b // g) * g
w1 = w // g
b1 = b // g
lcm = w1 * b1 * g

min_val = min(w, b)

# k = floor(t / lcm)
k = t // lcm

# favorable = (min_val - 1) + min_val * k
favorable = (min_val - 1) + min_val * k

# Simplify fraction favorable / t
g_simple = math.gcd(favorable, t)
p = favorable // g_simple
q = t // g_simple

print(f"{p}/{q}")
```

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

Vector Willman and Array Bolt are the two most famous athletes of Byteforces. They are going to compete in a race with a distance of L meters today.

 [Image] 

Willman and Bolt have exactly the same speed, so when they compete the result is always a tie. That is a problem for the organizers because they want a winner. 

While watching previous races the organizers have noticed that Willman can perform only steps of length equal to w meters, and Bolt can perform only steps of length equal to b meters. Organizers decided to slightly change the rules of the race. Now, at the end of the racetrack there will be an abyss, and the winner will be declared the athlete, who manages to run farther from the starting point of the the racetrack (which is not the subject to change by any of the athletes). 

Note that none of the athletes can run infinitely far, as they both will at some moment of time face the point, such that only one step further will cause them to fall in the abyss. In other words, the athlete will not fall into the abyss if the total length of all his steps will be less or equal to the chosen distance L.

Since the organizers are very fair, the are going to set the length of the racetrack as an integer chosen randomly and uniformly in range from 1 to t (both are included). What is the probability that Willman and Bolt tie again today?


-----Input-----

The first line of the input contains three integers t, w and b (1 ≤ t, w, b ≤ 5·10^18) — the maximum possible length of the racetrack, the length of Willman's steps and the length of Bolt's steps respectively.


-----Output-----

Print the answer to the problem as an irreducible fraction [Image]. Follow the format of the samples output.

The fraction [Image] (p and q are integers, and both p ≥ 0 and q > 0 holds) is called irreducible, if there is no such integer d > 1, that both p and q are divisible by d.


-----Examples-----
Input
10 3 2

Output
3/10

Input
7 1 2

Output
3/7



-----Note-----

In the first sample Willman and Bolt will tie in case 1, 6 or 7 are chosen as the length of the racetrack.

The input will be stdin and you should print your solution to stdout

Model Response

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. Two athletes compete in a race of length L meters
2. Willman takes steps of length w meters, Bolt takes steps of length b meters
3. They can only run as far as L (they won't go beyond L)
4. The race length L is chosen uniformly from 1 to t
5. We need to find the probability that they tie (both run the same distance)

## Key Insight

For Willman and Bolt to tie, they must both reach the same point without falling into the abyss.

- Willman can reach exactly the multiples of w (w, 2w, 3w, ...)
- Bolt can reach exactly the multiples of b (b, 2b, 3b, ...)

So they tie when the race length L is a multiple of both w and b. This happens when L is a multiple of LCM(w, b).

## Step-by-Step Solution

1. Find the LCM of w and b
   - LCM(w, b) = (w * b) / GCD(w, b)

2. Count how many numbers in range [1, t] are multiples of LCM(w, b)
   - This is floor(t / LCM(w, b))

3. Total possible values of L is t

4. Probability = (number of favorable outcomes) / (total outcomes)
   - = (floor(t / LCM(w, b))) / t

5. Simplify the fraction to irreducible form

## Implementation Challenges

- w, b, t can be very large (up to 5×10^18)
- Need to compute GCD efficiently (Euclidean algorithm)
- Need to compute LCM = (w * b) / GCD(w, b)
- Need to handle overflow when computing w * b
- Need to simplify the fraction

## Edge Cases

- If GCD(w, b) = w * b, then LCM = 1 (if w = b)
- If t < LCM(w, b), then there are 0 favorable outcomes

## Code Implementation

```python
import math

def gcd(a, b):
    while b:
        a, b = b, a % b
    return a

def lcm(a, b):
    return (a * b) // gcd(a, b)

# Read input
t, w, b = map(int, input().split())

# Find LCM of w and b
# We need to be careful about overflow when computing w * b
# If w * b would overflow, we can compute it as (w // gcd(w,b)) * b or similar

g = gcd(w, b)
lcm_val = (w // g) * b  # This avoids potential overflow

# Count multiples of lcm_val in range [1, t]
# Number of multiples = t // lcm_val
if lcm_val == 0:
    # This shouldn't happen with positive inputs
    num_favorable = 0
else:
    num_favorable = t // lcm_val

# Create fraction: num_favorable / t
# Simplify this fraction
if num_favorable == 0:
    # Fraction is 0/1
    print("0/1")
else:
    # Simplify fraction num_favorable/t
    g2 = gcd(num_favorable, t)
    numerator = num_favorable // g2
    denominator = t // g2
    
    print(f"{numerator}/{denominator}")
```

So only when d is even: d=2,4,6 → values at L=2,4,6 → 3 values.

Why only when d is even? Because for L in [d, d+1), both achieve distance d only if floor(L/w) * w = d and floor(L/b) * b = d.

For w=1, b=2, floor(L/1) = L, so distance = L
 floor(L/2) = floor(L/2), distance = 2 * floor(L/2)

We need L = 2 * floor(L/2)

This happens when L is even.

For L even, floor(L/2) = L/2, so distance = 2 * (L/2) = L

For L odd, floor(L/2) = (L-1)/2, distance = 2 * (L-1)/2 = L-1

So only when L is even, both achieve distance L.

So in general, for a given d, when is L in [d, d+min(w,b)) and both achieve distance d?

For that, we need:
- floor(L/w) * w = d
- floor(L/b) * b = d

Since d is a multiple of LCM(w,b), and w and b may not share the same factors, this may only happen for specific d.

Perhaps it's better to realize that they tie when the maximum distance is the same, and this occurs when the floor functions give the same value.

After research, I recall that in such problems, the number of L in [1,t] where floor(L/w) * w = floor(L/b) * b is equal to the number of L in [1,t] such that floor(L/w) = floor(L/b), or when both are zero.

But in our examples, it's not that.

Perhaps the only tie points are when L is in [0, min(w,b)) (both achieve 0) or when L is in [k*LCM, k*LCM + min(w,b)) for each k, and at such points both achieve distance k*LCM.

For w=1,b=2: at L=2: has to be in [2,3) → achieves distance 2
 at L=4: in [4,5) → achieves distance 4
 at L=6: in [6,7) → achieves distance 6

For L=2: distance for Willman: floor(2/1)=2, so 2*1=2
 for Bolt: floor(2/2)=1, so 1*2=2 → tie
 similarly for 4 and 6.

For L=1: distance for Willman:1, for Bolt:0 → not tie

For L=3: for Willman:3, for Bolt:2 → not tie

So only when L is even.

And even numbers are exactly the ones in intervals [2,3), [4,5), [6,7), etc. for the even values.

In general, for each multiple d of LCM(w,b), the values of L in [d, d+min(w,b)) will have both achieve distance d.

And since d is a multiple of both w and b, and min(w,b) is the min, the number of such L is min(w,b) if d + min(w,b) ≤ t+1, otherwise t - d + 1.

But in the w=1,b=2 example, min(w,b)=1, so for each d, the interval is [d, d+1), so only one value.

 for d=4: L=4
 for d=6: L=6
 so 3 values.

So the values are at L=d for each d that is a positive multiple of LCM(w,b) and d ≤ t.

And at such L, both achieve distance d.

 for d=6: L=6 (since [6,8) contains 6,7, so should be 2 values: 6 and 7)
 for d=0: [0,2) → L=1
 so total 3 values: 1,6,7

Yes! so for each multiple d of LCM(w,b), the number of L in [d, d+min(w,b)) and L in [1,t] is:
- if d == 0: then number of L in [0, min(w,b)) and L≥1, so max(0, min(w,b)-1)
- if d > 0: then number of L in [d, min(d+min(w,b), t+1)) and L≤t, so max(0, min(d+min(w,b), t+1) - d)

Since the interval is [d, d+min(w,b)), the number of integers L is min(w,b) if d+min(w,b) ≤ t+1, otherwise t - d + 1.

But since the upper bound is min(d+min(w,b), t) (L≤t), the number is min( min(w,b), t - d + 1 ) if d ≤ t, else 0.

For d=0: if min(w,b) > 0, then number is min(w,b) - 1 (since L=1,2,..,min(w,b)-1)

For d>0: number is min(min(w,b), t - d + 1)

In the example w=3,b=2,t=10:

d=0: min(1,7) - 1 = 1-1=0
