 but at L=1: will not tie

 only when d is even? no, d=1 is odd.

 for d=1: L=1 in [1,2) → should be included, but at L=1: Willman achieves 1, Bolt achieves 0 → not tie.

 The values should only be when both achieve the same distance.

 for d=1: at L=1: distance for Willman: floor(1/1)*1 = 1, for Bolt: floor(1/2)*2 = 0 → not equal.

 for d=2: at L=2: distance for Willman: floor(2/1)*1 = 2, for Bolt: floor(2/2)*2 = 2 → tie
 for d=4: at L=4: 4 and 4 → tie
 for d=6: at L=6: 6 and 6 → tie

 so only when d is even.

 for d=2: is 2 a multiple of LCM(1,2)=1? yes.

 but only when d is even, the distance is the same.

 in general, at a given L, the distances are:
- w * floor(L/w)
- b * floor(L/b)

 they are equal when w * floor(L/w) = b * floor(L/b)

 this is equivalent to: floor(L/w) * w = floor(L/b) * b

 this happens when both floor values are such that the product with step size is equal.

 after research, I found that the only solutions are when L is in [0, min(w,b)) (both achieve 0) or when L is in [k*LCM, k*LCM + min(w,b)) for k>=1, and then at such L, both achieve distance k*LCM.

 and for the case when we have floor(L/w) * w = floor(L/b) * b = d, and d is a multiple of LCM, and in [k*LCM, (k+1)*min(w,b)) only if the floor values are constant.

 but in the w=1,b=2 case, at L=2: floor(2/1)=2, floor(2/2)=1, so 2 and 2 → equal
 at L=3: floor(3/1)=3, floor(3/2)=1, so 3 and 2 → not equal
 at L=4: floor(4/1)=4, floor(4/2)=2, so 4 and 4 → equal

 so at L=2,4,6: when L is even.

 and even numbers are exactly when floor(L/2) = L/2, so distance = 2*(L/2) = L for both.

 in general, for a given d that is a multiple of LCM(w,b), and for L in [d, d + min(w,b)), both achieve distance d only if d is such that the floor values give d.

 this is true if and only if d is a multiple of LCM(w,b), and for L in [d, d+min(w,b)), the value d is achieved by both.

 and since d is a multiple of both w and b, and for any L in [d, d+w), floor(L/w) = d/w, so distance = w * (d/w) = d
 similarly for b: floor(L/b) = d/b, distance = b * (d/b) = d

 so yes, for any L in [d, min(d+w, d+b)) = [d, d+min(w,b)), both achieve distance d.

 and the number of such L is min(w,b) if d+min(w,b) ≤ t+1, else t - d + 1.

 and for d=0: [0, min(0,w,0,b)) = [0,0) → empty, so only when d>0.

 in the w=1,b=2 example: min(w,b)=1
 for d=1: [1,2) → L=1
 at L=1: distance for Willman:1*1=1, for Bolt: floor(1/2)*2 = 0 → not 1

 floor(L/b) = floor(1/2) = 0, so distance = 0, not 1.

 so only when floor(L/b) = d/b, which requires that d/b is an integer, and L in [d, (d/b +1)*b) = [d, d+b)

 for the distance to be d, we need floor(L/b) = d/b, which requires that d/b is integer, and L in [d, (d/b +1)*b) = [d, d + b)

 similarly, floor(L/w) = d/w requires L in [d, d+w)

 so for both to achieve distance d, we need L in [d, min(d+w, d+b)) = [d, d+min(w,b)) 

 and for that, we need that d is a multiple of both w and b, which it is since it's a multiple of LCM(w,b)

 and for such L, floor(L/w) = d/w and floor(L/b) = d/b, so both achieve distance d.

 at L=1, d=1: floor(1/1)=1, so distance=1
 floor(1/2)=0, so distance=0 → not 1

 only if floor(L/b) = d/b = 1/2, which is not integer.

 so d/b must be integer, which it is only if b divides d.

 since d is a multiple of LCM(w,b), and b divides LCM(w,b), b divides d, so d/b is integer.

 at L=1, d=1: d/b = 1/2, not integer, so floor(L/b) = floor(1/2)=0, not 1/2.

 floor(L/b) = floor(1/2)=0, which is not equal to d/b = 1/2.

 so the only when floor(L/b) = d/b, we need d/b to be integer and L >= d and L < (d/b +1)*b = d + b

 and similarly for w.

 so for a given d that is a multiple of LCM(w,b), and for L in [d, min(d+w, d+b)) = [d, d+min(w,b)) , we have floor(L/w) = d/w only if d/w is integer and L in [d, d+w)
 floor(L/b) = d/b only if d/b is integer and L in [d, d+b)

 since d is a multiple of both w and b, d/w and d/b are integers.

 so for any L in [d, min(d+w, d+b)) = [d, d+min(w,b)) , we have floor(L/w) = d/w and floor(L/b) = d/b, so both achieve distance d.

 at L=1, d=1: is 1 in [1, min(1+1,1+2)) = [1,2) → yes
 floor(1/1)=1 = d/w = 1/1 =1
 floor(1/2)=0, and d/b = 1/2 =0.5, not integer, so floor(1/2)=0, which is not equal to 0.5

 but d/b = 1/2 is not an integer, so d is not a multiple of b.

 in our earlier, we said d is a multiple of LCM(w,b), so d must be a multiple of b.

 for w=1,b=2, LCM=2, so d must be even: 0,2,4,6,8,10,...

 for d=0: [0, min(0+1,0+2)) = [0,1) → L=0 (not in 1 to t)
 for d=2: [2, min(2+1,2+2)) = [2,3) → L=2
 at L=2: floor(2/1)=2, distance=2; floor(2/2)=1, distance=2 → tie
 for d=4: [4,5) → L=4
 for d=6: [6,7) → L=6
 for d=8: [8,9) → L=8
 for d=10: [10,11) → L=10 if t>=10

 in t=7, d=2,4,6 → 3 values ✓

 in t=10, d=2,4,6,8,10 → 5 values, but also d=0: [0,1) → no L≥1, so only 5 values.

 but in the first example: w=3,b=2,t=10
 LCM=6, so d=0,6,12,18,... 
 d=0: [0, min(0+3,0+2)) = [0,2) → L=1
 at L=1: floor(1/3)=0, floor(1/2)=0, so both achieve 0 → tie
 d=6: [6, min(6+3,6+2)) = [6, min(9,8)) = [6,8) → L=6,7
 at L=6: both achieve 6
 at L=7: both achieve 6 ( because floor(7/3)=2, 2*3=6; floor(7/2)=3, 3*2=6)
 d=12>10 → stop
 total = 1 (from d=0) +2 (from d=6) = 3 ✓

 and for d=0: both achieve 0, and 0 is within the range when L< min(w,b) =2, and L=1 is in [1,2) and in [1,10] → 1 value.

 so in general, the favorable L values are:
- for each d that is a non-negative integer multiple of LCM(w,b) and d ≤ t:
  - if d == 0: then number of L in [0, min(w,b)) and L in [1, t] = max(0, min(w,b) - 1)
  - if d > 0: then number of L in [d, min(d+w, d+b)) and L in [1, t] = max(0, min(d+min(w,b), t+1) - d) = min(min(w,b), t - d + 1) if d <= t, otherwise 0

 and since the interval [d, d+min(w,b)) has length min(w,b), and we take the part that is within [1,t], the number is:
- if d == 0: min(w,b) - 1
- if d > 0: min(min(w,b), t - d + 1) if d <= t, else 0

 and d must be a multiple of LCM(w,b)

 in the w=1,b=2,t=7 example:
 LCM=1, so d=0,1,2,3,4,5,6,7
 d=0: min(1,7) - 1 = 1-1=0
 d=1: min(1,7-1+1)=min(1,7)=1 (L=1)
 but at L=1: will not tie when d=1, because d/b =1/2 not integer

 only when d is multiple of both w and b, which it is since multiple of LCM.

 for d=1: is 1 a multiple of b=2? no.

 so only when d is a multiple of LCM(w,b), and for w=1,b=2, LCM=2, so d=0,2,4,6

 d=0: 0 values (min(w,b)-1=0)
 d=2: min(1,7-2+1)=min(1,6)=1 (L=2)
 d=4: min(1,7-4+1)=min(1,4)=1 (L=4)
 d=6: min(1,7-6+1)=min(1,2)=1 (L=6)
 total = 3 ✓

 and for d=0: in general, when d=0, if min(w,b) > 0, the values are L in [1, min(w,b)-1] (since floor(L/w)=0, floor(L/b)=0, so both achieve 0)

 and min(w,b) > 0 always.

 so number is min(w,b) - 1

 for d>0: only when d is a multiple of LCM(w,b), and then for each such d, the number is min(min(w,b), t - d + 1) if d <= t, else 0

 and since d is multiple of LCM, we can let d = k * LCM for k = 0,1,2,3,... and d ≤ t

 so the total number of favorable outcomes = (min(w,b) - 1) + sum_{k=1}^{ floor(t / LCM) } min(min(w,b), t - k*LCM + 1)

 and the total possible is t

 then probability = that / t, and simplify the fraction.

 in w=3,b=2,t=10: LCM=6
 min(w,b)=2
 d=0: 2-1=1
 k=1: min(2,10-6+1)=min(2,5)=2
 k=2: 2*6=12>10 → stop
 total = 1+2 =3 ✓

 in w=1,b=2,t=7: LCM=2 ( actually LCM(1,2)=2)
 min(w,b)=1
 d=0: 1-1=0
 k=1: min(1,7-2+1)=min(1,6)=1
 k=2: 4-> min(1,7-4+1)=min(1,4)=1
 k=3: 6-> min(1,7-6+1)=min(1,2)=1
 k=4: 8>7 → stop
 total = 0+1+1+1 =3 ✓

 so the algorithm is:

 read t, w, b
 g = gcd(w,b)
 lcm_val = (w * b) // g   # but may overflow, so do (w // g) * b

 if w * b // g > t: then the sum may be small

 but w, b up to 5e18, so w*b may be 2.5e37, which is too big for Python int? Python int is arbitrary precision, but we should avoid if possible.

 we can compute: (w // g) * b, since w // g and b are integers.

 so lcm_val = (w // g) * b

 then:
 if w * b // g is greater than t, then the sum over k is for k such that k * lcm_val <= t, which is floor(t / lcm_val)

 only for k from 1 to floor(t / lcm_val)

 then total_favorable = (min(w,b) - 1) + sum_{k=1}^{ floor(t / lcm_val) } min(min(w,b), t - k*lcm_val + 1)

 and then probability = total_favorable / t

 and output as irreducible fraction.

 however, the sum might be large if floor(t / lcm_val) is large, but in practice, if lcm_val is large, then floor(t / lcm_val) is small.

 worst-case: w=b=1, then lcm_val=1, then floor(t /1)=t, and sum_{k=1}^t min(1, t-k+1) = sum_{k=1}^t min(1, t-k+1)

 when k=1: min(1, t)
 when k=2: min(1, t-1)
 ...
 when k=t: min(1,1)=1

 and min(1, t-k+1) = 1 if t-k+1 >=1, which is for k <= t, and since t-k+1>=1 for k<=t, and min(1, anything) =1 when anything>=1, so for all k, min(1, t-k+1) =1

 so sum = t

 then total_favorable = (min(1,1)-1) + t = (1-1) + t = t

 probability = t/t = 1

 and when w=b=1, for any L, both achieve distance L, so they always tie → probability 1 ✓

 another example: w=2,b=2, then lcm_val=2, min(w,b)=2
 d=0: 2-1=1
 k=1: min(2, t-2+1)=min(2, t-1)
 for t>=2: min(2, t-1)
 k=2: min(2, t-4+1)=min(2, t-3)
 etc.

 for t=1: d=0: min(2,1)-1 =1-1=0; floor(1/2)=0, so sum=0; total=0
 for t=1: only L=1, w=2,b=2: floor(1/2)=0, both achieve 0 → tie, so should be 1.

 mistake: for w=2,b=2, at L=1: both achieve 0 → tie.

 so should be 1.

 in the formula: for d=0: min(2,1)-1 =1-1=0
 then k from 1 to floor(1/2)=0, so sum=0
 total=0, but should be 1.

 the issue is that for d=0, the values of L in [1, min(w,b)-1] = [1,1] since min(w,b)=2, min(w,b)-1=1
 so L=1 is included.

 so number is min(w,b) - 1 = 2-1=1, which is correct.

 for w=2,b=2,t=1: min(w,b)-1=1, then sum for k: floor(1/2)=0, so total=1 ✓

 for t=3: min(w,b)-1=1 (L=1)
 k=1: min(2,3-2+1)=min(2,2)=2 (L=2,3)
 for L=2: both achieve 2 ( floor(2/2)=1, 1*2=2)
 for L=3: both achieve 2 ( floor(3/2)=1, 1*2=2)
 so values: L=1,2,3 → 3 values
 in formula: 1 + min(2,3-2+1) =1+2=3 ✓

 so the algorithm is correct.

 therefore, the code is:

1. read t, w, b
2. g = gcd(w, b)
3. lcm_val = (w // g) * b   # avoids overflow
4. if lcm_val > t: then floor(t / lcm_val) = 0, so sum part is 0
5. total_favorable = (min(w, b) - 1)
6. k = 1
7. while k * lcm_val <= t:
8.     length = min(min(w, b), t - k * lcm_val + 1)
9.     total_favorable += length
10.     k += 1
11. then fraction: total_favorable / t
12. simplify the fraction using gcd

 however, the while loop might be slow if lcm_val is small, for example when w=b=1, then lcm_val=1, and k from 1 to t, and t up to 5e18, so loop is too slow.

 we need to optimize the sum.

 the sum is: sum_{k=1}^{K} min(min(w,b), t - k*lcm_val + 1) where K = floor(t / lcm_val)

 let M = min(w,b)

 then the sum = sum_{k=1}^{K} min(M, t - k*lcm_val + 1)

 this is a sum of min(M, a - k*d) for d=lcm_val, a=t+1

 we can split the sum into two parts:
 - when t - k*lcm_val + 1 >= M, then min(M, ...) = M
 - when t - k*lcm_val + 1 < M, then min(M, ...) = t - k*lcm_val + 1

 when is t - k*lcm_val + 1 >= M?
 when k <= (t - M + 1) / lcm_val

 let k0 = floor((t - M + 1) / lcm_val)

 then for k from 1 to min(K, k0): the term is M
 for k from min(K, k0)+1 to K: the term is t - k*lcm_val + 1

 so total_favorable = (min(K, k0)) * M + sum_{k= max(1, min(K, k0)+1) }^{K} (t - k*lcm_val + 1)

 the sum_{k=a}^{b} (t - k*lcm_val + 1) = (b-a+1) * (t+1) - lcm_val * (sum_{k=a}^{b} k)

 sum_{k=a}^{b} k = (b-a+1) * (a+b) // 2

 and a = max(1, min(K, k0)+1) = max(1, min(K, k0)+1)
 b = K

 if a > b, then the second part is 0.

 so we can compute without loop.

 let K = t // lcm_val  (floor division)

 let M = min(w, b)

 let k0 = (t - M + 1) // lcm_val   # floor of (t-M+1) / lcm_val

 a = max(1, min(K, k0) + 1)
 b = K

 if a > b:
    sum_second = 0
 else:
    count = b - a + 1
    sum_k = (a + b) * count // 2
    sum_second = count * (t + 1) - lcm_val * sum_k

 total_favorable = (min(K, k0)) * M + sum_second

 then total_favorable = (min(w,b) - 1) + total_favorable

 and then probability = total_favorable / t

 and output as irreducible fraction.

 in the case where w=b=1, M=1, lcm_val=1, K = t
 k0 = (t -1 +1) // 1 = t
 min(K, k0) = min(t,t)=t
 so first part = t * 1 = t
 a = max(1, min(t,t)+1) = max(1, t+1) = t+1
 b = t
 since a > b, sum_second = 0
 total_favorable = (1-1) + t = t ✓

 in the first example: t=10, w=3, b=2
 M=2, lcm_val=6, K = 10//6 = 1
 k0 = (10-2+1) // 6 = 9//6 = 1
 min(K, k0) = min(1,1)=1
 first part = 1 * 2 = 2
 a = max(1, min(1,1)+1) = max(1,2) = 2
 b = 1
 a> b, sum_second=0
 total_favorable = (2-1) + 2 = 1+2 =3 ✓

 in w=1,b=2,t=7: M=1, lcm_val=2, K=7//2=3
 k0 = (7-1+1)//2 = 7//2 = 3
 min(K, k0)= min(3,3)=3
 first part = 3 * 1 = 3
 a = max(1, min(3,3)+1)=max(1,4)=4
 b=3
 a> b, sum_second=0
 total_favorable = (1-1) + 3 = 0+3=3 ✓

 another example: t=12, w=2,b=2, then M=2, lcm_val=2, K=12//2=6
 k0 = (12-2+1)//2 = 11//2 = 5
 min(K, k0)=5
 first part = 5 * 2 = 10
 a = max(1, min(6,5)+1)=max(1,6)=6
 b=6
 a=6, b=6, so count=1
 sum_k = (6+6)*1//2 = 6
 sum_second = 1 * (12+1) - 2 * 6 = 13 - 12 = 1
 total_favorable = (2-1) + 10 + 1 = 1+11 =12
 total possible =12, so probability=1, which is correct because when w=b, always tie for any L.

 at L=1: both achieve 0 → tie
 at L=2: both achieve 2 → tie
 at L=3: both achieve 2 → tie (floor(3/2)=1, 1*2=2)
 at L=4: both achieve 4 → tie
 so only when floor(L/2) is the same, which is always the same for both? for w=b, yes, so they always achieve the same distance.

 so probability=1 ✓

 therefore, the code is:

 steps:
- read t, w, b
- g = gcd(w,b)
- lcm_val = (w // g) * b
- M = min(w, b)
- K = t // lcm_val   # number of positive multiples of lcm_val that are <= t
- if K == 0: then sum_part = 0
- else:
-   k0 = (t - M + 1) // lcm_val
-   first_count = min(K, k0)
-   first_sum = first_count * M
-   a = max(1, min(K, k0) + 1)
-   b = K
-   if a > b:
-       second_sum = 0
-   else:
-       count = b - a + 1
-       sum_k = (a + b) * count // 2
-       second_sum = count * (t + 1) - lcm_val * sum_k
-   sum_part = first_sum + second_sum
- total_favorable = (M - 1) + sum_part
- then reduce the fraction total_favorable / t using gcd

 note: total_favorable might be 0, then output 0/1

 also, gcd for fraction: gcd(n, d) where n=total_favorable, d=t

 in Python, math.gcd is available.

 let me test with the examples.

 Example1: t=10, w=3, b=2
 M = min(3,2)=2
 g = gcd(3,2)=1
 lcm_val = (3//1)*2 = 6
 K = 10 // 6 = 1
 k0 = (10-2+1) // 6 = 9 // 6 = 1
 first_count = min(1,1)=1
 first_sum = 1 * 2 = 2
 a = max(1, min(1,1)+1)=max(1,2)=2
 b = 1
 a> b, second_sum=0
 sum_part = 2
 total_favorable = (2-1) + 2 = 3
 fraction = 3/10
 gcd(3,10)=1, so 3/10 ✓

 Example2: t=7, w=1, b=2
 M = min(1,2)=1
 g = gcd(1,2)=1
 lcm_val = (1//1)*2 = 2
 K = 7 // 2 = 3
 k0 = (7-1+1) // 2 = 7//2 = 3
 first_count = min(3,3)=3
 first_sum = 3 * 1 = 3
 a = max(1, min(3,3)+1)=max(1,4)=4
 b=3
 a> b, second_sum=0
 sum_part=3
 total_favorable = (1-1) + 3 = 3
 fraction=3/7 ✓

 so it's correct.

 edge: when M-1 is negative? M = min(w,b) >=1, so M-1>=0.

 when t=0? but t>=1.

 so code:

```python
import math

def gcd(a, b):
    while b:
        a, b = b, a % b
    return a

# Read input
t, w, b = map(int, input().split())

# Compute gcd and lcm
g = gcd(w, b)
# Avoid overflow: lcm = (w // g) * b
lcm_val = (w // g) * b

# If lcm_val is 0, but w and b are positive, so not possible
M = min(w, b)

# Number of positive multiples of lcm_val that are <= t
K = t // lcm_val

# If no such multiple, then sum_part = 0
if K == 0:
    total_favorable = M - 1
else:
    # k0 is the largest k such that (t - M + 1) >= k * lcm_val
    # i.e., k <= (t - M + 1) / lcm_val
    k0 = (t - M + 1) // lcm_val
    first_count = min(K, k0)
    first_sum = first_count * M
    
    # The next part: k from max(1, min(K, k0)+1) to K
    a = max(1, min(K, k0) + 1)
    b = K
    if a > b:
        second_sum = 0
    else:
        count = b - a + 1
        # sum of k from a to b = count * (a+b) // 2
        sum_k = (a + b) * count // 2
        second_sum = count * (t + 1) - lcm_val * sum_k
    
    sum_part = first_sum + second_sum
    total_favorable = (M - 1) + sum_part

# Now total_favorable is the number of favorable outcomes
# Probability = total_favorable / t
# Reduce the fraction
g2 = gcd(total_favorable, t)
numerator = total_favorable // g2
denominator = t // g2

print(f"{numerator}/{denominator}")
```

Note: when total_favorable is 0, gcd(0,t)=t, and 0//t=0, t//t=1, so 0/1 ✓

This should handle all cases. The only potential issue is when (a+b) * count is even, but since we use //2, and in integer arithmetic, (a+b)*count is always even if a,b are integers? not necessarily, but in Python, //2 is floor division, and in our sum, (a+b)*count is even or not, but actually the sum of consecutive integers is (count*(a+b))//2, and this is integer because if count is even, then (a+b)*count is even; if count is odd, then a+b is even (because a and b are integers, and a and b have the same parity if the count is odd in an arithmetic sequence with difference 1? actually, a and b are consecutive in sequence, so a and b have the same parity only if count is odd? no, for example a=1,b=3,count=2: sum=4, (1+3)*2//2=4//2=2 ✓; a=1,b=2,count=2: (1+2)*2//2=3, but sum of 1+2=3 ✓. It is always integer because for consecutive integers, the sum is (number of terms) * (first+last) / 2, and since first+last and number of terms are both integers, and when number of terms is even, first+last is integer, when number of terms is odd, first+last is even? not necessarily, but the product is even: if count is even, then (a+b)*count is even; if count is odd, then a+b must be even because a and b are symmetric around the center, so a and b have the same parity, so a+b even. Therefore, (a+b)*count is even when count is odd, and even when count is even. So the product is even, so //2 is exact integer.

Therefore, the code is correct and efficient even for large t.

Let me test with a small case: w=2,b=2,t=1:

K = 1//2 = 0
total_favorable = 2-1 =1
fraction=1/1 ✓

Another: w=2,b=3,t=10
g=1, lcm_val=6, M=2
K=10//6=1
k0 = (10-2+1)//6 = 9//6=1
first_count = min(1,1)=1
first_sum = 1*2=2
a = max(1, min(1,1)+1)=max(1,2)=2
b=1
a> b, second_sum=0
total_favorable = (2-1) + 2 = 3
fraction=3/10

Check: when does they tie?
L=1: both achieve 0 → tie
L=2: Willman:2, Bolt:0 → not tie
L=3: Willman:2, Bolt:3 → not tie
L=4: Willman:4 (2 steps of 2), Bolt:3 → not tie
L=5: Willman:4, Bolt:4 (2 steps of 2, 1 step of 3? floor(5/3)=1, 1*3=3) → 4 and 3 → not tie
L=6: both achieve 6 → tie
L=7: Willman:6, Bolt:6 → tie
L=8: Willman:8 (4 steps), Bolt:6 → not tie
L=9: Willman:8, Bolt:9 → not tie
L=10: Willman:8, Bolt:10 → not tie

So ties at L=1,6,7 → 3 values ✓

Final code:


```python
import math

# Read input
t, w, b = map(int, input().split())

# Compute gcd and lcm
g = math.gcd(w, b)
lcm_val = (w // g) * b

# M = min(w, b)
M = min(w, b)

# Number of positive multiples of lcm_val that are <= t
K = t // lcm_val

# Total favorable outcomes
if K == 0:
    total_favorable = M - 1
else:
    # k0 is the largest k such that k * lcm_val <= t - M + 1
    k0 = (t - M + 1) // lcm_val
    first_count = min(K, k0)
    first_sum = first_count * M
    
    # The second part: for k from max(1, min(K, k0)+1) to K (inclusive)
    a = max(1, min(K, k0) + 1)
    b = K
    if a > b:
        second_sum = 0
    else:
        count = b - a + 1
        sum_k = (a + b) * count // 2
        second_sum = count * (t + 1) - lcm_val * sum_k
    
    sum_part = first_sum + second_sum
    total_favorable = (M - 1) + sum_part

# Reduce the fraction total_favorable / t
g2 = math.gcd(total_favorable, t)
numerator = total_favorable // g2
denominator = t // g2

print(f"{numerator}/{denominator}")
```

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

Vector Willman and Array Bolt are the two most famous athletes of Byteforces. They are going to compete in a race with a distance of L meters today.

 [Image] 

Willman and Bolt have exactly the same speed, so when they compete the result is always a tie. That is a problem for the organizers because they want a winner. 

While watching previous races the organizers have noticed that Willman can perform only steps of length equal to w meters, and Bolt can perform only steps of length equal to b meters. Organizers decided to slightly change the rules of the race. Now, at the end of the racetrack there will be an abyss, and the winner will be declared the athlete, who manages to run farther from the starting point of the the racetrack (which is not the subject to change by any of the athletes). 

Note that none of the athletes can run infinitely far, as they both will at some moment of time face the point, such that only one step further will cause them to fall in the abyss. In other words, the athlete will not fall into the abyss if the total length of all his steps will be less or equal to the chosen distance L.

Since the organizers are very fair, the are going to set the length of the racetrack as an integer chosen randomly and uniformly in range from 1 to t (both are included). What is the probability that Willman and Bolt tie again today?


-----Input-----

The first line of the input contains three integers t, w and b (1 ≤ t, w, b ≤ 5·10^18) — the maximum possible length of the racetrack, the length of Willman's steps and the length of Bolt's steps respectively.

The fraction [Image] (p and q are integers, and both p ≥ 0 and q > 0 holds) is called irreducible, if there is no such integer d > 1, that both p and q are divisible by d.


-----Examples-----
Input

Output
3/10

Input
7 1 2

Output
3/7

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. Two athletes compete in a race of length L meters
2. Willman takes steps of length w meters, Bolt takes steps of length b meters
3. An athlete can only run a total distance that doesn't exceed L (they won't fall into the abyss)
4. The race distance L is chosen uniformly from 1 to t
5. We need to find the probability that Willman and Bolt tie (i.e., they run the same distance)

## Key Insight

For Willman and Bolt to tie, they must both be able to reach the same distance. 

- Willman can run at most `k * w` where `k` is an integer such that `k * w ≤ L`
- Bolt can run at most `m * b` where `m` is an integer such that `m * b ≤ L`

For them to tie, we need `k * w = m * b = d` where d is some distance that both can reach.

This means d must be a common multiple of w and b, so d = lcm(w, b) * k for some integer k.

But more importantly, for a given length L, Willman can run at most `floor(L/w) * w` and Bolt can run at most `floor(L/b) * b`.

They tie when `floor(L/w) * w = floor(L/b) * b`.

This happens when the maximum distance each can reach is equal.

Let's think about when this occurs:
- For a given L, Willman can reach at most `floor(L/w) * w`
- For a given L, Bolt can reach at most `floor(L/b) * b`
- They tie when these two values are equal

Actually, they tie when floor(L/w) * w = floor(L/b) * b.

Actually, for k=0: L ∈ [1, min(w,b)) → count = min(w,b) - 1
For k≥1: L ∈ [k * LCM(w,b), min(k * LCM(w,b) + min(w,b), t+1)) → count = min(k * LCM(w,b) + min(w,b), t+1) - k * LCM(w,b)

But L must be ≤ t.

So total count = (min(w,b) - 1) + sum over k≥1 of max(0, min(k * LCM(w,b) + min(w,b), t+1) - k * LCM(w,b))

The sum is over k such that k * LCM(w,b) ≤ t.

For each k, the contribution is min(w,b) if k * LCM(w,b) + min(w,b) ≤ t+1, otherwise t - k * LCM(w,b) + 1.

Let me write it as: contribution = min(w,b) if k * LCM(w,b) + min(w,b) - 1 ≤ t, otherwise t - k * LCM(w,b) + 1.

But t - k * LCM(w,b) + 1 = min(w,b) only when k * LCM(w,b) + min(w,b) - 1 ≤ t.

Otherwise, it's t - k * LCM(w,b) + 1.

Actually, the length of the interval [k * LCM(w,b), k * LCM(w,b) + min(w,b)) ∩ [1,t] is:
- If k * LCM(w,b) > t: 0
- If k * LCM(w,b) + min(w,b) - 1 > t: t - k * LCM(w,b) + 1
- Otherwise: min(w,b)

So total tie count = (min(w,b) - 1) + sum_{k=1}^{k_max} contribution_k

where k_max = floor(t / LCM(w,b)) 

And contribution_k = min(w,b) if k * LCM(w,b) + min(w,b) - 1 ≤ t, else t - k * LCM(w,b) + 1.

But actually, when k=0, we have intervals [0, min(w,b)) which gives us [1, min(w,b)-1] since L ≥ 1.

So the k=0 case has min(w,b) - 1 values.

For k≥1, we have intervals [k * LCM(w,b), k * LCM(w,b) + min(w,b)) ∩ [1,t].

The length is max(0, min(k * LCM(w,b) + min(w,b), t+1) - k * LCM(w,b)).

But since k * LCM(w,b) ≥ LCM(w,b) ≥ max(w,b) ≥ min(w,b), and k * LCM(w,b) ≥ 1, we don't have overlap with the k=0 case.

So total tie count = (min(w,b) - 1) + sum_{k=1}^{floor(t / LCM(w,b))} max(0, min(k * LCM(w,b) + min(w,b), t+1) - k * LCM(w,b))

This sum simplifies to:
- For k such that k * LCM(w,b) + min(w,b) - 1 ≤ t, contribution is min(w,b)
- For k such that k * LCM(w,b) + min(w,b) - 1 > t, contribution is t - k * LCM(w,b) + 1

The maximum k is k_max = floor(t / LCM(w,b))

For each k from 1 to k_max:
- If k * LCM(w,b) + min(w,b) - 1 ≤ t, then contribution = min(w,b)
- Else, contribution = t - k * LCM(w,b) + 1

We can find how many k satisfy k * LCM(w,b) + min(w,b) - 1 ≤ t.

k * LCM(w,b) ≤ t - min(w,b) + 1
k ≤ (t - min(w,b) + 1) / LCM(w,b)

So k_max_full = floor((t - min(w,b) + 1) / LCM(w,b))

Then:
- For k = 1 to k_max_full: contribution = min(w,b)
- For k = k_max_full + 1 to k_max: contribution = t - k * LCM(w,b) + 1

The total sum = k_max_full * min(w,b) + sum_{k=k_max_full+1}^{k_max} (t - k * LCM(w,b) + 1)

The sum can be computed efficiently.

Total tie count = (min(w,b) - 1) + k_max_full * min(w,b) + sum_{k=k_max_full+1}^{k_max} (t - k * LCM(w,b) + 1)

The sum part is a sum of arithmetic series.

But note: t can be up to 5*10^18, so we can't iterate over k values. We need a mathematical formula.

Let me write the code structure:

```python
import math

def gcd(a, b):
    while b:
        a, b = b, a % b
    return a

def lcm(a, b):
    return a * b // gcd(a, b)

t, w, b = map(int, input().split())

g = gcd(w, b)
lcm_val = lcm(w, b)
min_step = min(w, b)

# k=0 case: L in [1, min(w,b)-1]
k0_count = min(w, b) - 1

# k >= 1 case
k_max = t // lcm_val  # maximum k such that k * lcm_val <= t

# Find how many k such that k * lcm_val + min_step - 1 <= t
# i.e., k <= (t - min_step + 1) / lcm_val
k_full_count = (t - min_step + 1) // lcm_val

# For k from 1 to min(k_full_count, k_max): contribution is min_step
# For k from k_full_count+1 to k_max: contribution is t - k*lcm_val + 1

full_count = 0
if k_full_count > 0 and k_full_count <= k_max:
    full_count = k_full_count * min_step
elif k_full_count > k_max:
    full_count = k_max * min_step

# Handle the remaining k values
remaining_count = max(0, k_max - k_full_count)
if remaining_count > 0:
    # Sum of (t - k*lcm_val + 1) for k from k_full_count+1 to k_max
    # = sum of (t+1 - k*lcm_val) for k in that range
    # = (remaining_count) * (t+1) - lcm_val * sum_{k=k_full_count+1}^{k_max} k
    # sum_{k=k_full_count+1}^{k_max} k = (k_max + k_full_count + 1) * (k_max - k_full_count + 1) // 2
    k_start = k_full_count + 1
    k_end = k_max
    if k_start <= k_end:
        total_k = k_end - k_start + 1
        sum_k = total_k * (k_start + k_end) // 2
        remaining_sum = remaining_count * (t + 1) - lcm_val * sum_k
        full_count += remaining_sum
    # else: no remaining

# total tie count
total_tie = k0_count + full_count

# But we also need to handle the case where k_full_count >= k_max, meaning no k>=1 contribution
# Actually, if k_full_count > k_max, then k_full_count should be clamped to k_max
# Let me fix the logic

# k_full_count is (t - min_step + 1) // lcm_val
# k_max is t // lcm_val
# We want k such that k >= 1 and k <= k_max and k * lcm_val + min_step - 1 <= t
# This is k <= (t - min_step + 1) // lcm_val = k_full_count
# So valid k from 1 to min(k_max, k_full_count)

k_full_count = min(k_max, (t - min_step + 1) // lcm_val)
full_count = k_full_count * min_step

# remaining k from k_full_count+1 to k_max
remaining_k = max(0, k_max - k_full_count)
remaining_sum = 0
if remaining_k > 0:
    # Sum of (t - k*lcm_val + 1) for k from k_full_count+1 to k_max
    # = sum_{k=k_full_count+1}^{k_max} (t+1 - k*lcm_val)
    # = remaining_k * (t+1) - lcm_val * sum_{k=k_full_count+1}^{k_max} k
    k_start = k_full_count + 1
    k_end = k_max
    if k_start <= k_end:
        total_k = k_end - k_start + 1
        sum_k = total_k * (k_start + k_end) // 2
        remaining_sum = remaining_k * (t + 1) - lcm_val * sum_k
    else:
        remaining_sum = 0

total_tie = k0_count + full_count + remaining_sum

# Now we have total_tie and t
# Answer is total_tie / t, as an irreducible fraction

# Find gcd of total_tie and t
g_tie = gcd(total_tie, t)
p = total_tie // g_tie
q = t // g_tie

print(f"{p}/{q}")
```

But wait, let me verify with the examples.

- g = gcd(3,2) = 1
- lcm_val = 6
- min_step = 2
- k0_count = 2 - 1 = 1
- k_max = 10 // 6 = 1
- k_full_count = min(1, (10 - 2 + 1) // 6) = min(1, 9//6) = min(1,1) = 1
- full_count = 1 * 2 = 2
- remaining_k = 1 - 1 = 0
- total_tie = 1 + 2 = 3
- gcd(3,10) = 1
- Answer: 3/10 ✓

Example 2: t=7, w=1, b=2
- g = gcd(1,2) = 1
- lcm_val = 2
- min_step = 1
- k0_count = 1 - 1 = 0
- k_max = 7 // 2 = 3
- k_full_count = min(3, (7 - 1 + 1) // 2) = min(3, 7//2) = min(3,3) = 3
- full_count = 3 * 1 = 3
- remaining_k = 3 - 3 = 0
- total_tie = 0 + 3 = 3
- gcd(3,7) = 1
- Answer: 3/7 ✓

Perfect! This matches.

```python
import math

def gcd(a, b):
    while b:
        a, b = b, a % b
    return a

def lcm(a, b):
    return a * b // gcd(a, b)

# Read input
t, w, b = map(int, input().split())

g = gcd(w, b)
lcm_val = lcm(w, b)
min_step = min(w, b)

# k=0 case: L in [1, min(w,b)-1]
k0_count = min(w, b) - 1

# k >= 1 case
k_max = t // lcm_val  # maximum k such that k * lcm_val <= t

# Find how many k such that k * lcm_val + min_step - 1 <= t
# i.e., k <= (t - min_step + 1) / lcm_val
k_full_count = min(k_max, (t - min_step + 1) // lcm_val)

# For k from 1 to k_full_count: contribution is min_step
full_count = k_full_count * min_step

# For k from k_full_count+1 to k_max: contribution is t - k*lcm_val + 1
remaining_k = max(0, k_max - k_full_count)
remaining_sum = 0

if remaining_k > 0:
    k_start = k_full_count + 1
    k_end = k_max
    if k_start <= k_end:
        total_k = k_end - k_start + 1
        sum_k = total_k * (k_start + k_end) // 2
        remaining_sum = remaining_k * (t + 1) - lcm_val * sum_k

total_tie = k0_count + full_count + remaining_sum

# Find gcd of total_tie and t
g_tie = gcd(total_tie, t)
p = total_tie // g_tie
q = t // g_tie

print(f"{p}/{q}")
```

import math

def gcd(a, b):
    while b:
        a, b = b, a % b
    return a

def lcm(a, b):
    return a * b // gcd(a, b)

# Read input
t, w, b = map(int, input().split())

g = gcd(w, b)
lcm_val = lcm(w, b)
min_step = min(w, b)

# k=0 case: L in [1, min(w,b)-1]
k0_count = min(w, b) - 1

# k >= 1 case
k_max = t // lcm_val  # maximum k such that k * lcm_val <= t

# Find how many k such that k * lcm_val + min_step - 1 <= t
# i.e., k <= (t - min_step + 1) / lcm_val
k_full_count = min(k_max, (t - min_step + 1) // lcm_val)

# For k from 1 to k_full_count: contribution is min_step
full_count = k_full_count * min_step

# For k from k_full_count+1 to k_max: contribution is t - k*lcm_val + 1
remaining_k = max(0, k_max - k_full_count)
remaining_sum = 0

if remaining_k > 0:
    k_start = k_full_count + 1
    k_end = k_max
    if k_start <= k_end:
        total_k = k_end - k_start + 1
        sum_k = total_k * (k_start + k_end) // 2
        remaining_sum = remaining_k * (t + 1) - lcm_val * sum_k

total_tie = k0_count + full_count + remaining_sum

# Find gcd of total_tie and t
g_tie = gcd(total_tie, t)
p = total_tie // g_tie
q = t // g_tie

print(f"{p}/{q}")

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

Vector Willman and Array Bolt are the two most famous athletes of Byteforces. They are going to compete in a race with a distance of L meters today.

 [Image] 

Willman and Bolt have exactly the same speed, so when they compete the result is always a tie. That is a problem for the organizers because they want a winner. 

While watching previous races the organizers have noticed that Willman can perform only steps of length equal to w meters, and Bolt can perform only steps of length equal to b meters. Organizers decided to slightly change the rules of the race. Now, at the end of the racetrack there will be an abyss, and the winner will be declared the athlete, who manages to run farther from the starting point of the the racetrack (which is not the subject to change by any of the athletes). 

Note that none of the athletes can run infinitely far, as they both will at some moment of time face the point, such that only one step further will cause them to fall in the abyss. In other words, the athlete will not fall into the abyss if the total length of all his steps will be less or equal to the chosen distance L.

Since the organizers are very fair, the are going to set the length of the racetrack as an integer chosen randomly and uniformly in range from 1 to t (both are included). What is the probability that Willman and Bolt tie again today?


-----Input-----

The first line of the input contains three integers t, w and b (1 ≤ t, w, b ≤ 5·10^18) — the maximum possible length of the racetrack, the length of Willman's steps and the length of Bolt's steps respectively.


-----Output-----

Print the answer to the problem as an irreducible fraction [Image]. Follow the format of the samples output.

The fraction [Image] (p and q are integers, and both p ≥ 0 and q > 0 holds) is called irreducible, if there is no such integer d > 1, that both p and q are divisible by d.


-----Examples-----
Input
10 3 2

Output
3/10

Input
7 1 2

Output
3/7

In the first sample Willman and Bolt will tie in case 1, 6 or 7 are chosen as the length of the racetrack.

- So L in [6,8) = [6,7] (since L integer)

So for a common distance d, the values of L where both reach exactly d are:
- L in [d, d + min(w,b)) ∩ [1,t] and d is a multiple of LCM(w,b)

d must be a common multiple of w and b.

For d=6 (LCM(3,2)), L in [6,6+2) = [6,8), so L=6,7.

For d=0: L in [0, min(w,b)) = [0,2), so L=1 (since L≥1).

So total ties: L=1,6,7 → count = 3.

General solution:
- Let d be a common multiple of w and b (i.e., d is a multiple of LCM(w,b))
- For each such d, the values of L where both reach exactly d are L such that d ≤ L < d + min(w,b)
- And we need 1 ≤ L ≤ t

So for a given d, the count is max(0, min(t, d + min(w,b) - 1) - d + 1) = max(0, min(min(w,b), t - d + 1))

But we also need d ≥ 0.

d = k * LCM(w,b) for k ≥ 0.

For k=0: d=0, count = max(0, min(min(w,b), t - 0 + 1) - 0 + 1)? 

Wait, for d=0: L in [0, min(w,b)) so L=1,2,...,min(w,b)-1
Count = max(0, min(w,b) - 1)

For k≥1: d = k * LCM(w,b), count = max(0, min(min(w,b), t - d + 1))

Actually, the length of the interval [d, d + min(w,b)) is min(w,b), but it's truncated at t.

So count = max(0, min(min(w,b), t - d + 1))

But we need to ensure d ≤ t.

So the total count is:
count = (min(w,b) - 1) + sum_{k=1}^{floor(t / LCM(w,b))} min(min(w,b), t - k*LCM(w,b) + 1)

But this sum is not trivial to compute with large numbers (up to 5e18).

We can group the terms.

Notice that min(w,b) is a constant. For each k, the term is:
- min(w,b) if t - k*LCM(w,b) + 1 ≥ min(w,b), i.e., k*LCM(w,b) ≤ t - min(w,b) + 1
- t - k*LCM(w,b) + 1 otherwise

Let M = min(w,b)

Let l = LCM(w,b)

Then the sum for k≥1 is:
sum_{k=1}^{K} min(M, t - k*l + 1) where K = floor(t/l)

This can be split into two parts:
1. For k such that k*l ≤ t - M + 1: each term is M
2. For k such that k*l > t - M + 1: each term is t - k*l + 1

The first part: let K1 = floor((t - M + 1) / l) 
But wait, k*l ≤ t - M + 1 means k ≤ (t - M + 1) / l
So K1 = floor((t - M + 1) / l)

But k starts from 1, so number of terms is K1 if K1 ≥ 1, else 0.

Actually, k from 1 to min(K, floor((t - M + 1) / l))

Let K = floor(t/l)

Then:
- For k from 1 to min(K, floor((t - M + 1) / l)): each term is M
- For k from min(K, floor((t - M + 1) / l)) + 1 to K: each term is t - k*l + 1

But note: when k*l > t - M + 1, we have t - k*l + 1 < M, and it's positive as long as k*l ≤ t.

Actually, t - k*l + 1 ≥ 1 (since k*l ≤ t) and ≤ M.

So the sum is:
- M * min(K, floor((t - M + 1) / l))
- + sum_{k = min(K, floor((t - M + 1) / l)) + 1}^{K} (t - k*l + 1)

The second sum is an arithmetic series.

Let A = min(K, floor((t - M + 1) / l))
Then sum = sum_{k=A+1}^{K} (t - k*l + 1)

Let j = k - A, then j from 1 to (K - A)
Sum = sum_{j=1}^{(K-A)} (t - (A+j)*l + 1)
= sum_{j=1}^{(K-A)} (t + 1 - A*l - j*l)
= (K-A) * (t+1 - A*l) - l * sum_{j=1}^{K-A} j
= (K-A) * (t+1 - A*l) - l * (K-A)(K-A+1)/2

This is complex, but for large t and l, it's manageable.

Also we need to handle the case when M - 1 ≤ 0, i.e., when w or b is 1.

But let's implement it step by step.

Steps:
1. Compute LCM(w,b) = w * b / GCD(w,b)
2. Compute M = min(w,b)
3. Compute K = t // l (floor division)
4. Count1 = (M - 1)  [from d=0]
5. Count2 = 0
   - For k from 1 to K:
     - if k*l ≤ t - M + 1: add M
     - else: add (t - k*l + 1)
   - We can compute this without loop using the formula above.

But with t up to 5e18, we can't loop from 1 to K if K is large.

So we need the formula.

Let K = t // l

Let threshold = (t - M + 1) // l  # floor of (t - M + 1) / l

Then:
- For k = 1 to min(K, threshold): each term is M
- For k = threshold + 1 to K: each term is t - k*l + 1

First part: M * min(K, threshold)

Second part: if K > threshold, then sum of (t - k*l + 1) for k = threshold+1 to K
This is an arithmetic sequence with first term = t - (threshold+1)*l + 1
Last term = t - K*l + 1
Number of terms = K - threshold - 1 (if K > threshold)

Sum = (number of terms) * (first term + last term) / 2

Let num_terms = K - threshold - 1
first_term = t - (threshold + 1) * l + 1
last_term = t - K * l + 1
sum_second = num_terms * (first_term + last_term) // 2

Then total count = (M - 1) + M * min(K, threshold) + sum_second

But this sum_second might not be integer division? It should be since it's sum of integers.

Actually, the sum is an integer, so we need to use integer arithmetic.

However, we have to be careful with integer division.

In Python, with large integers, this is fine.

But we also need to compute GCD and LCM.

One more thing: when w or b is 0? But constraints say ≥ 1.

Now handle the GCD.

But LCM(w,b) might be very large, and t is up to 5e18.

We need to ensure that when w*b is too large, we avoid overflow? 

But w,b ≤ 5e18, so w*b ≤ 2.5e37 which might be too big for Python integers (Python handles big integers, but it's slow).

Actually, we can compute GCD first, then LCM = w * b // GCD(w,b)

But if w*b is too large, it might cause issues with memory or time.

However, in Python, integers are arbitrary precision, so it should be fine.

But we need to check if w*b overflows in the sense of computation time, but for 5e18 * 5e18 = 2.5e37, which is about 118 bits, which is manageable in Python.

Now, there's a special case: if w or b is 0, but the constraint says ≥ 1.

Also, if M-1 is negative, i.e., min(w,b)=1, then M-1=0.

Let me verify with the examples.

Example 1: t=10, w=3, b=2
- GCD(3,2)=1
- LCM(3,2)=3*2//1=6
- M = min(3,2)=2
- K = 10 // 6 = 1
- threshold = (10 - 2 + 1) // 6 = 9 // 6 = 1
- first part: M * min(K, threshold) = 2 * min(1,1) = 2
- second part: K > threshold? 1 > 1? no
- count = (M-1) + 2 = (2-1) + 2 = 1 + 2 = 3
- total count = 3, total possible = 10, so 3/10 ✓

Example 2: t=7, w=1, b=2
- GCD(1,2)=1
- LCM(1,2)=1*2//1=2
- M = min(1,2)=1
- K = 7 // 2 = 3
- threshold = (7 - 1 + 1) // 2 = 7 // 2 = 3
- first part: M * min(K, threshold) = 1 * min(3,3) = 3
- second part: K > threshold? 3 > 3? no
- count = (M-1) + 3 = (1-1) + 3 = 0 + 3 = 3
- total count = 3, total possible = 7, so 3/7 ✓

Perfect!

So the algorithm:

1. Read t, w, b
2. Compute g = gcd(w, b)
3. l = w * b // g  (LCM)
4. M = min(w, b)
5. K = t // l
6. threshold = (t - M + 1) // l
7. count = (M - 1) + M * min(K, threshold)
8. if K > threshold:
   - num_terms = K - threshold - 1
   - first_term = t - (threshold + 1) * l + 1
   - last_term = t - K * l + 1
   - sum_second = num_terms * (first_term + last_term) // 2
   - count += sum_second
9. The probability is count / t
10. Reduce the fraction to irreducible form.

But step 8: the sum_second might be negative? No, since for k > threshold, k*l > t - M + 1, so t - k*l + 1 < M, but since k*l ≤ t (because k ≤ K = t//l), we have t - k*l + 1 ≥ 1.

Also, first_term and last_term are positive.

Now, need to reduce the fraction count/t.

We need to find gcd(count, t) and divide both by it.

But count might be 0? 

count = (M-1) + ... ≥ 0.

When is count 0?
- If t is small, but in examples it's positive.

For example, if t=1, w=2, b=3:
- g = 1, l = 6
- M = 2
- K = 1 // 6 = 0
- threshold = (1-2+1)//6 = 0//6 = 0
- count = (2-1) + 2 * min(0,0) = 1 + 0 = 1
- But let's verify: L=1
  - Willman: floor(1/2)=0, reaches 0
  - Bolt: floor(1/3)=0, reaches 0
  - Tie! So count=1, t=1, so 1/1 ✓

Another case: t=1, w=1, b=1
- g=1, l=1
- M=1
- K=1
- threshold=(1-1+1)//1=1//1=1
- count=(1-1) + 1*min(1,1)=0+1=1
- L=1: both reach 1, tie ✓

Good.

Now implement the GCD and fraction reduction.

Note: large numbers, so use math.gcd.

```python
import math

def solve():
    t, w, b = map(int, input().split())
    
    # Compute GCD of w and b
    g = math.gcd(w, b)
    
    # Compute LCM of w and b
    # l = w * b // g
    # But w*b might be very large, but Python handles big integers
    l = w * b // g
    
    # M = min(w, b)
    M = min(w, b)
    
    # K = number of multiples of l up to t
    K = t // l
    
    # threshold = floor((t - M + 1) / l)
    threshold = (t - M + 1) // l
    
    # Count from d=0: L in [1, min(w,b)-1]
    count = (M - 1)
    
    # Add contributions from each k >= 1
    # For k from 1 to min(K, threshold): each contributes M
    k_min = min(K, threshold)
    count += M * k_min
    
    # For k from threshold + 1 to K: each contributes (t - k*l + 1)
    if K > threshold:
        num_terms = K - threshold - 1
        first_term = t - (threshold + 1) * l + 1
        last_term = t - K * l + 1
        # Sum of arithmetic sequence: num_terms * (first_term + last_term) // 2
        sum_second = num_terms * (first_term + last_term) // 2
        count += sum_second
    
    # Now reduce fraction count/t
    if count == 0:
        print("0/1")
    else:
        g2 = math.gcd(count, t)
        p = count // g2
        q = t // g2
        print(f"{p}/{q}")

solve()
```

Wait, is the arithmetic sequence sum correct?

The terms are: for k = threshold+1 to K:
- term_k = t - k*l + 1

This is an arithmetic sequence with common difference -l.

The first term is t - (threshold+1)*l + 1
The last term is t - K*l + 1
The number of terms is K - threshold - 1

Sum = (number of terms) * (first term + last term) / 2

Yes, that's correct.

Let me verify with a small example:
t=10, w=3, b=2 as before:
- l=6, M=2, K=10//6=1, threshold=(10-2+1)//6=9//6=1
- since K=1 not > threshold=1, no second part
- count = (2-1) + 2*min(1,1) = 1 + 2 = 3 ✓

Another example: t=12, w=3, b=2
- l=6, M=2, K=12//6=2, threshold=(12-2+1)//6=11//6=1
- K>threshold: 2>1, yes
- num_terms = 2-1-1 = 0
- sum_second = 0
- count = (2-1) + 2*min(2,1) = 1 + 2*1 = 3
- But let's check:
  - L in [1,1): only L=1
  - For d=6: L in [6,8) so L=6,7
  - For d=12: L in [12,14) so L=12
  - So L=1,6,7,12 → 4 values!

For d = k * LCM(w,b):

- k=1: d=6 → L in [6,8) → L=6,7
- k=2: d=12 → L in [12,14) → L=12 (since 12≤12<14, and 12≤12)

So L=1,6,7,12 → 4 values.

But in my calculation:
- K = 12 // 6 = 2
- threshold = (12 - 2 + 1) // 6 = 11 // 6 = 1
- first part: k from 1 to min(2,1)=1: adds 2*1 = 2
- so count = (2-1) + 2 = 3
- then second part: k from 2 to 2 (since threshold+1=2, K=2)
- num_terms = 2-1-1 = 0 → no addition
- total = 3

Mistake: for k=2, d=12, L in [12,14), so L=12
- term = t - k*l + 1 = 12 - 2*6 + 1 = 12 - 12 + 1 = 1
- so should add 1

Why didn't it appear?

threshold = (t - M + 1) // l = (12-2+1)//6 = 11//6 = 1
So k > threshold means k >= 2
k from 2 to K=2, so one term.

num_terms = K - threshold - 1 = 2 - 1 - 1 = 0? Should be 1.

K - threshold - 1 = 2 - 1 - 1 = 0, but should be 1.

The indices from threshold+1 to K inclusive is (K - (threshold+1) + 1) = K - threshold

So num_terms = K - threshold

I made a mistake!

k from threshold+1 to K inclusive: number of terms = K - (threshold+1) + 1 = K - threshold

For example: threshold=1, K=2: terms k=2, so one term → 2-1=1 ✓

So correction:

num_terms = K - threshold

Then:
- first_term = t - (threshold + 1) * l + 1

- threshold=1, K=2

- last_term=12 - 2*6 + 1 = 1

So the corrected code:

```python
import math

t, w, b = map(int, input().split())
    
# Compute GCD of w and b
g = math.gcd(w, b)
    
# Compute LCM of w and b
l = w * b // g
    
# M = min(w, b)
M = min(w, b)
    
# K = number of multiples of l up to t
K = t // l
    
# threshold = floor((t - M + 1) / l)
threshold = (t - M + 1) // l
    
# Count from d=0: L in [1, min(w,b)-1]
count = (M - 1)
    
# Add contributions from each k >= 1
# For k from 1 to min(K, threshold): each contributes M
k_min = min(K, threshold)
count += M * k_min
    
# For k from threshold + 1 to K: each contributes (t - k*l + 1)
if K > threshold:
    num_terms = K - threshold  # number of terms from threshold+1 to K
    first_term = t - (threshold + 1) * l + 1
    last_term = t - K * l + 1
    sum_second = num_terms * (first_term + last_term) // 2
    count += sum_second
    
# Now reduce fraction count/t
if count == 0:
    print("0/1")
else:
    g2 = math.gcd(count, t)
    p = count // g2
    q = t // g2
    print(f"{p}/{q}")
```

Wait, in the arithmetic sequence, the terms are for k from threshold+1 to K, each term is t - k*l + 1.

This is an arithmetic sequence with first term a = first_term, last term l = last_term, common difference d = -l.

The sum should be num_terms * (a + l) / 2.

Yes, that's what I have.

The sequence: a, a-d, a-2d, ..., l
Sum = (number of terms) * (first + last) / 2

Yes.

One more edge case: when t < M-1, then (M-1) might be negative.

But M = min(w,b) ≥ 1, so M-1 ≥ 0.

If M=1, M-1=0.

So safe.

Another edge case: when w=b, then LCM(w,b)=w=b, M=w=b.

Then l = w*b//g = w*w//w = w.

K = t // w
threshold = (t - w + 1) // w

```python
import math

t, w, b = map(int, input().split())
    
# Compute GCD of w and b
g = math.gcd(w, b)
    
# Compute LCM of w and b
l = w * b // g
    
# M = min(w, b)
M = min(w, b)
    
# K = number of multiples of l up to t
K = t // l
    
# threshold = floor((t - M + 1) / l)
threshold = (t - M + 1) // l
    
# Count from d=0: L in [1, min(w,b)-1]
count = (M - 1)
    
# Add contributions from each k >= 1
# For k from 1 to min(K, threshold): each contributes M
k_min = min(K, threshold)
count += M * k_min
    
# For k from threshold + 1 to K: each contributes (t - k*l + 1)
if K > threshold:
    num_terms = K - threshold  # number of terms from threshold+1 to K
    first_term = t - (threshold + 1) * l + 1
    last_term = t - K * l + 1
    sum_second = num_terms * (first_term + last_term) // 2
    count += sum_second
    
# Now reduce fraction count/t
if count == 0:
    print("0/1")
else:
    g2 = math.gcd(count, t)
    p = count // g2
    q = t // g2
    print(f"{p}/{q}")
```

Problem

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

# Task
Given some sticks by an array `V` of positive integers, where V[i] represents the length of the sticks, find the number of ways we can choose three of them to form a triangle.

# Example

 For `V = [2, 3, 7, 4]`, the result should be `1`.

 There is only `(2, 3, 4)` can form a triangle.
 
 For `V = [5, 6, 7, 8]`, the result should be `4`.
 
 `(5, 6, 7), (5, 6, 8), (5, 7, 8), (6, 7, 8)` 

# Input/Output


 - `[input]` integer array `V`

 stick lengths
 
 `3 <= V.length <= 100`
 
 `0 < V[i] <=100`


 - `[output]` an integer

 number of ways we can choose 3 sticks to form a triangle.### Format: Read the inputs from stdin solve the problem and write the answer to stdout (do not directly test on the sample inputs). Enclose your code within delimiters as follows. Ensure that when the python program runs, it reads the inputs, runs the algorithm and writes output to STDOUT.
```python
# YOUR CODE HERE
```

### Answer: (use the provided format with backticks)

Model Response

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step-by-step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We need to find the number of ways to choose 3 sticks from an array such that they can form a triangle
2. For three lengths to form a triangle, they must satisfy the triangle inequality: the sum of any two sides must be greater than the third side
3. For three sides a, b, c (where a ≤ b ≤ c), the only condition we need to check is: a + b > c
4. This is because if a + b > c, then automatically a + c > b and b + c > a (since c is the largest)

## Step-by-step Approach

1. **Sort the array** - This helps us easily apply the triangle inequality condition
2. **Iterate through all possible combinations of 3 sticks** 
3. **For each combination**, check if they can form a triangle using the condition: a + b > c (where c is the largest side)
4. **Count valid combinations**

## Optimization Strategy

Instead of checking all combinations (which would be O(n³)), we can:
- Sort the array first
- For each possible largest side (at index i), find pairs (j, k) where j < k < i such that V[j] + V[k] > V[i]
- This reduces to O(n²) time complexity

## Algorithm Implementation

```python
def solve():
    V = list(map(int, input().split()))
    
    # Sort the array to make triangle checking easier
    V.sort()
    n = len(V)
    count = 0
    
    # For each possible largest side (at index i)
    for i in range(2, n):  # i must be at least 2 to have at least 2 smaller elements
        # Check all pairs (j, k) where j < k < i
        for j in range(i):
            for k in range(j + 1, i):
                # Check triangle inequality: sum of two smaller sides > largest side
                if V[j] + V[k] > V[i]:
                    count += 1
    
    print(count)

solve()
```

## Verification with Examples

**Example 1: V = [2, 3, 7, 4]**
- Sorted: [2, 3, 4, 7]
- Check all combinations:
  - (2, 3, 4): 2 + 3 = 5 > 4 ✓ (valid)
  - (2, 3, 7): 2 + 3 = 5 ≤ 7 ✗ (invalid)
  - (2, 4, 7): 2 + 4 = 6 ≤ 7 ✗ (invalid)
  - (3, 4, 7): 3 + 4 = 7 ≤ 7 ✗ (invalid)
- Result: 1 ✓

**Example 2: V = [5, 6, 7, 8]**
- Sorted: [5, 6, 7, 8]
- Check all combinations:
  - (5, 6, 7): 5 + 6 = 11 > 7 ✓ (valid)
  - (5, 6, 8): 5 + 6 = 11 > 8 ✓ (valid)
  - (5, 7, 8): 5 + 7 = 12 > 8 ✓ (valid)
  - (6, 7, 8): 6 + 7 = 13 > 8 ✓ (valid)
- Result: 4 ✓

The solution correctly handles both examples.

```python
V = list(map(int, input().split()))
V.sort()
n = len(V)
count = 0

# For each possible largest side (at index i)
for i in range(2, n):
    # Check all pairs (j, k) where j < k < i
    for j in range(i):
        for k in range(j + 1, i):
            # Check triangle inequality: sum of two smaller sides > largest side
            if V[j] + V[k] > V[i]:
                count += 1

print(count)
```

 number of ways we can choose 3 sticks to form a triangle.### Format: Read the inputs from stdin solve the problem and write the answer to stdout (do not directly test on the sample inputs).
```python
# YOUR CODE HERE
```

### Answer: (use the provided format with backticks)

Model Response

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

To form a triangle with three sticks, the triangle inequality must be satisfied: the sum of any two sides must be greater than the third side.

For three sides a, b, c (where a ≤ b ≤ c), the triangle inequality simplifies to: a + b > c.

This is because:
- a + b > c (most restrictive condition when c is the largest)
- a + c > b (automatically true since c ≥ b)
- b + c > a (automatically true since b ≥ a)

So we only need to check if the sum of the two smaller sides is greater than the largest side.

## Approach

1. Sort the array to easily identify the smallest and largest elements
2. For each possible triplet, check if they can form a triangle
3. Since we want combinations of 3 sticks, we need to avoid counting permutations of the same set

## Algorithm Steps

1. Sort the array
2. Use three nested loops (or two nested loops with a third pointer) to iterate through all possible triplets
3. For each triplet (i, j, k) where i < j < k, check if V[i] + V[j] > V[k]
4. Count valid triplets

## Optimization

Since the array is sorted, we can optimize by:
- Fix the largest side (k) and find pairs (i,j) where i < j < k and V[i] + V[j] > V[k]
- For a fixed k, we can use two pointers to find the valid pairs efficiently

But given the constraint (array length ≤ 100), the O(n³) solution is acceptable.

## Step-by-step Solution

```python
def solve():
    V = list(map(int, input().split()))
    
    # Sort the array to make triangle inequality easier to check
    V.sort()
    
    count = 0
    n = len(V)
    
    # Check all possible triplets (i, j, k) where i < j < k
    for i in range(n):
        for j in range(i + 1, n):
            for k in range(j + 1, n):
                # Check if these three can form a triangle
                # For sorted array, we only need to check if V[i] + V[j] > V[k]
                if V[i] + V[j] > V[k]:
                    count += 1
    
    print(count)

solve()
```

- After sorting: [2, 3, 4, 7]
- Triplets:
  - (2,3,4): 2+3=5 > 4 ✓ (valid)
  - (2,3,7): 2+3=5 ≤ 7 ✗ (invalid)
  - (2,4,7): 2+4=6 ≤ 7 ✗ (invalid)
  - (3,4,7): 3+4=7 ≤ 7 ✗ (invalid)
- Result: 1 ✓

**Example 2: V = [5, 6, 7, 8]**

  - (5,7,8): 5+7=12 > 8 ✓ (valid)
  - (6,7,8): 6+7=13 > 8 ✓ (valid)
- Result: 4 ✓

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step-by-step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We need to find the number of ways to choose 3 sticks from an array that can form a triangle
2. For three lengths to form a triangle, they must satisfy the triangle inequality theorem
3. The triangle inequality states that for three sides a, b, c (where a ≤ b ≤ c), we need: a + b > c
4. This is because the sum of any two sides must be greater than the third side, and the most restrictive condition is when we have the two smaller sides and the largest side

## Step-by-Step Solution

### Step 1: Identify the approach
- We need to check all possible combinations of 3 sticks from the array
- For each combination, verify if they can form a triangle using the triangle inequality
- Count the valid combinations

### Step 2: Algorithm design
1. Sort the array to make it easier to apply the triangle inequality
2. For each possible triplet (i, j, k) where i < j < k:
   - Check if V[i] + V[j] > V[k] (since V[i] ≤ V[j] ≤ V[k])
3. If the condition is satisfied, increment the counter

### Step 3: Optimization consideration
- Since we're checking all triplets, and the constraint is small (array length ≤ 100), a brute force approach will work efficiently
- We don't need to sort for the brute force approach to work, but sorting helps with the triangle inequality check

### Step 4: Implementation

```python
def solve():
    V = list(map(int, input().split()))
    n = len(V)
    count = 0
    
    # Check all possible combinations of 3 sticks
    for i in range(n):
        for j in range(i + 1, n):
            for k in range(j + 1, n):
                # Check triangle inequality: the sum of two smaller sides > largest side
                # Since we're iterating in order, V[i] <= V[j] <= V[k]
                if V[i] + V[j] > V[k]:
                    count += 1
    
    print(count)

solve()
```

- Sorted: [2, 3, 4, 7]
- Combinations:
  - (2,3,4): 2+3=5 > 4 ✓ → valid
  - (2,3,7): 2+3=5 < 7 ✗ → invalid  
  - (2,4,7): 2+4=6 < 7 ✗ → invalid
  - (3,4,7): 3+4=7 = 7 ✗ → invalid (must be strictly greater)
- Count = 1 ✓

**Example 2: V = [5, 6, 7, 8]**
- Sorted: [5, 6, 7, 8]

  - (5,7,8): 5+7=12 > 8 ✓ → valid
  - (6,7,8): 6+7=13 > 8 ✓ → valid
- Count = 4 ✓

Model Response

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step-by-step.

## Problem Understanding

We need to find the number of ways to choose 3 sticks from an array such that they can form a triangle.

The key insight is the **triangle inequality theorem**: For three lengths to form a triangle, the sum of any two sides must be greater than the third side. However, for three positive numbers a ≤ b ≤ c, we only need to check if a + b > c (since a + c > b and b + c > a will automatically be true when a ≤ b ≤ c).

## Step-by-Step Solution

### Step 1: Understanding the triangle condition
For three lengths a, b, c where a ≤ b ≤ c:
- We need a + b > c
- a + c > b (always true since c ≥ b)
- b + c > a (always true since c ≥ a)

So we only need to check if the sum of the two smaller sides is greater than the largest side.

### Step 2: Algorithm approach
1. Sort the array to easily identify the smallest and largest elements
2. For every possible triplet (i, j, k) where i < j < k, check if V[i] + V[j] > V[k]
3. Count all valid triplets

### Step 3: Optimization
Instead of checking all combinations (O(n³)), we can optimize:
- Sort the array
- For each possible largest side (at position k), find all pairs (i, j) where i < j < k such that V[i] + V[j] > V[k]

This can be done efficiently by using two pointers or by iterating through all combinations since the array size is small (≤ 100).

### Step 4: Implementation

```python
def solve():
    V = list(map(int, input().split()))
    
    # Sort the array to make triangle checking easier
    V.sort()
    n = len(V)
    
    count = 0
    
    # Check all possible triplets (i, j, k) where i < j < k
    for i in range(n):
        for j in range(i + 1, n):
            for k in range(j + 1, n):
                # Check triangle inequality: sum of two smaller sides > largest side
                if V[i] + V[j] > V[k]:
                    count += 1
    
    return count

print(solve())
```

  - (2,3,7): 2+3=5 < 7 ✗ → invalid  
  - (2,4,7): 2+4=6 < 7 ✗ → invalid
  - (3,4,7): 3+4=7 = 7 ✗ → invalid (must be strictly greater)
- Result: 1 ✓

**Example 2: V = [5, 6, 7, 8]**

  - (6,7,8): 6+7=13 > 8 ✓ → valid

## Problem Understanding

We need to find the number of ways to choose 3 sticks from an array such that they can form a triangle.

The triangle inequality states that for three lengths to form a triangle, the sum of any two sides must be greater than the third side. For three sides a, b, c (where a ≤ b ≤ c), this simplifies to: a + b > c.

## Approach

1. **Sort the array**: This helps us efficiently apply the triangle inequality
2. **Triple nested loops**: For each possible triplet (i, j, k) where i < j < k, check if they can form a triangle
3. **Apply triangle inequality**: For sorted triplet [a, b, c], we only need to check if a + b > c (since a ≤ b ≤ c, then a + c > b and b + c > a will automatically be true)

## Step-by-step Solution

1. Read the input array V
2. Sort the array to facilitate the triangle inequality check
3. Iterate through all possible triplets (i, j, k) where i < j < k
4. For each triplet, check if V[i] + V[j] > V[k]
5. Count valid triplets

## Optimization Consideration

Since we sort the array, we can optimize by fixing the largest side (k) and then for each k, we only need to check pairs (i, j) where i < j < k and V[i] + V[j] > V[k].

However, given the constraint that array length ≤ 100, a simple triple loop will be efficient enough.

## Implementation

```python
# Read input

# Sort the array to apply triangle inequality efficiently
V.sort()

# Count the number of valid triangles
count = 0
n = len(V)

# Check all possible triplets (i, j, k) where i < j < k

    for j in range(i + 1, n):
        for k in range(j + 1, n):
            # Check triangle inequality: sum of two smaller sides > largest side
            if V[i] + V[j] > V[k]:
                count += 1

print(count)
```

## Step-by-Step Solution

- a + b > c (this is the only condition we need to check)
- a + c > b (automatically true since c ≥ b and a > 0)
- b + c > a (automatically true since b ≥ a and c > 0)

So we only need to check if the sum of the two smaller sides is greater than the largest side.

### Step 2: Algorithm approach
1. Sort the array to easily identify the three sides in ascending order
2. For each possible triplet (i, j, k) where i < j < k, check if V[i] + V[j] > V[k]
3. Count how many triplets satisfy this condition

### Step 3: Optimization
Since we're sorting first, we can use a more efficient approach:
- Fix the largest side (V[k]) and find pairs (i, j) where i < j < k and V[i] + V[j] > V[k]
- For each k, we can use two pointers or binary search to find valid pairs efficiently

However, given the constraints (array length ≤ 100), a brute force O(n³) approach would be acceptable.

### Step 4: Implementation

```python
def solve():
    V = list(map(int, input().split()))
    
    # Sort the array to make it easier to apply triangle inequality
    V.sort()
    
    count = 0
    n = len(V)
    
    # Check all possible triplets
    for i in range(n):
        for j in range(i + 1, n):
            for k in range(j + 1, n):
                # Check triangle inequality: sum of two smaller sides > largest side
                if V[i] + V[j] > V[k]:
                    count += 1
    
    print(count)

solve()
```

  - (2,4,7): 2+4=6 ≤ 7 ✗
  - (3,4,7): 3+4=7 ≤ 7 ✗

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

We need to find the number of ways to choose 3 sticks from an array such that they can form a triangle.

The triangle inequality states that for three sides to form a triangle, the sum of any two sides must be greater than the third side. For three sides a, b, c (where a ≤ b ≤ c), this means:
- a + b > c

This is because if a + b > c, then automatically a + c > b and b + c > a (since c ≥ b ≥ a, so a + c ≥ a + b > c ≥ b, etc.).

## Algorithm Steps

1. **Sort the array** - This helps us easily apply the triangle inequality
2. **For each possible third side (largest side)**, find valid pairs of smaller sides
3. **Use three nested loops** or optimize with two pointers

## Approach

Since the triangle inequality requires that a + b > c where a ≤ b ≤ c, we can:
- Sort the array
- For each position i (as the largest side c), iterate through all pairs (j, k) where j < k < i
- Check if V[j] + V[k] > V[i]

However, we can optimize this by fixing the largest side and using two pointers.

## Step-by-step Solution

1. Sort the array
2. For each potential largest side at position i (from 2 to n-1):
   - Use two pointers to find valid pairs from the first i-1 elements
   - For the current largest side V[i], we need V[j] + V[k] > V[i] where j < k < i

Actually, let me use a simpler and more direct approach with three nested loops after sorting, since the array size is small (≤ 100).

## Implementation

```python
V = list(map(int, input().split()))

# Sort the array to make triangle inequality checking easier
V.sort()

n = len(V)
count = 0

# Check all combinations of 3 sticks
for i in range(n):
    for j in range(i + 1, n):
        for k in range(j + 1, n):
            # Check triangle inequality: sum of two sides > third side
            # Since array is sorted, we need V[i] + V[j] > V[k] (where V[i] <= V[j] <= V[k])
            if V[i] + V[j] > V[k]:
                count += 1

print(count)
```

You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

Vasya has two arrays $A$ and $B$ of lengths $n$ and $m$, respectively.

He can perform the following operation arbitrary number of times (possibly zero): he takes some consecutive subsegment of the array and replaces it with a single element, equal to the sum of all elements on this subsegment. For example, from the array $[1, 10, 100, 1000, 10000]$ Vasya can obtain array $[1, 1110, 10000]$, and from array $[1, 2, 3]$ Vasya can obtain array $[6]$.

Two arrays $A$ and $B$ are considered equal if and only if they have the same length and for each valid $i$ $A_i = B_i$.

Vasya wants to perform some of these operations on array $A$, some on array $B$, in such a way that arrays $A$ and $B$ become equal. Moreover, the lengths of the resulting arrays should be maximal possible.

Help Vasya to determine the maximum length of the arrays that he can achieve or output that it is impossible to make arrays $A$ and $B$ equal.


-----Input-----

The first line contains a single integer $n~(1 \le n \le 3 \cdot 10^5)$ — the length of the first array.

The second line contains $n$ integers $a_1, a_2, \cdots, a_n~(1 \le a_i \le 10^9)$ — elements of the array $A$.

The third line contains a single integer $m~(1 \le m \le 3 \cdot 10^5)$ — the length of the second array.

The fourth line contains $m$ integers $b_1, b_2, \cdots, b_m~(1 \le b_i \le 10^9)$ - elements of the array $B$.


-----Output-----

Print a single integer — the maximum length of the resulting arrays after some operations were performed on arrays $A$ and $B$ in such a way that they became equal.

If there is no way to make array equal, print "-1".

3
