I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have an n×n grid with m banned cells
2. Chips must be placed on border cells (but not corners)
3. Each chip moves from its starting border position to the opposite edge over n-1 minutes
4. Chips move one cell per minute to adjacent cells (up/down/left/right)
5. Losing conditions:
   - A chip falls on a banned cell
   - Two chips end up on the same cell at any time
   - Two chips swap positions in a minute (this happens when two chips are on opposite border cells of a row/column and meet in the middle)

## Key Insights

1. **Border cells (non-corner)**: For an n×n grid, border cells that are not corners are:
   - Top row: positions (1,2), (1,3), ..., (1,n-1)
   - Bottom row: positions (n,2), (n,3), ..., (n,n-1)
   - Left column: positions (2,1), (3,1), ..., (n-1,1)
   - Right column: positions (2,n), (3,n), ..., (n-1,n)

2. **Chip movement pattern**: 
   - A chip at position (r,c) moves to (r',c') after k minutes
   - After k minutes, a chip at (r,c) will be at position determined by its direction
   - For row movement: if starting at row 1, it goes to row n in n-1 minutes
   - For column movement: if starting at column 1, it goes to column n in n-1 minutes

3. **Swapping condition**: Two chips swap if they start on opposite sides of a row or column and meet in the middle. This happens when they are symmetrically placed and meet at the center.

4. **Collision condition**: Two chips can't be at the same position at any time.

5. **Key observation**: For chips to not swap and not collide, they need to be placed such that their paths don't intersect and they don't meet at the same point.

## Critical Analysis

Let me think about when two chips can coexist:
- Chips can't start on symmetric positions in the same row/column that would cause them to meet in the middle
- Chips can't start such that they would land on the same cell at any time

Actually, let's reconsider: in a row of length n, if we place chips at positions (r,2) and (r,n-1), they will move toward each other. After k minutes, they would be at positions (r,2+k) and (r,n-1-k). When 2+k = n-1-k, we get k = (n-3)/2. This happens when n is odd and k = (n-3)/2, which means they meet in the middle.

So for even n: chips at (r,2) and (r,n-1) never meet since 2 + k = n-1 - k has no integer solution for k in [0, n-1]
For odd n: they meet at k = (n-3)/2

2 + k = n - 1 - k
2k = n - 3
k = (n-3)/2

This is valid when n ≥ 3 and (n-3) is even, i.e., n is odd.

So for odd n, chips placed symmetrically in a row/column will meet in the middle and cause a swap.

## Strategy

For any row or column, if we place chips at symmetric positions, they will meet in the middle (for odd n) or not meet (for even n).

But looking at the constraints, we need to avoid:
1. Landing on banned cells
2. Two chips at same position
3. Two chips swapping (meeting in the middle)

The key insight is that for any given row or column, we can place at most one chip per "half" path, and we need to avoid symmetric positions that would cause swaps.

Given the examples and the complexity, and since the output is always 0 or 1, I believe the solution is:

- Check if there exists any border non-corner cell that is not banned and whose path doesn't go through any banned cell.
- If yes, answer is 1.
- If no, answer is 0.

For a cell (r,c) on border (not corner), its path will go through cells (r, c+k) or (r, c-k) depending on direction, and similarly for row if it's in a column.

For a cell on top row (1,c), it moves down until it reaches row n.
It passes through (1,c), (2,c), (3,c), ..., (n,c)

Similarly for other borders.

So for a top row cell (1,c), it goes through all cells in column c from row 1 to n.

For a left column cell (r,1), it goes through all cells in row r from column 1 to n.

Therefore, if any cell in this path is banned, the chip cannot be placed.

So for each border non-corner cell, we need to check if the entire path in its movement direction contains any banned cell.

For a cell at (r,c):
- If on top or bottom row (r=1 or r=n), it moves vertically through column c from row 1 to n (for top) or row n to 1 (for bottom)
- If on left or right column (c=1 or c=n), it moves horizontally through row r from col 1 to n (for left) or col n to 1 (for right)

So for each such cell, we check if any cell on the path is banned.

If no such cell exists, it's a valid starting position.

The maximum number of chips is then the maximum number of such valid positions we can place without violating the other conditions.

The only exception might be when the valid positions are such that they don't interfere with each other.

Given the complexity and the examples, I think the intended solution is:

1. For each border non-corner cell, check if its entire path contains any banned cell.
2. If there is at least one such cell, answer is 1.
3. Otherwise, answer is 0.

This matches all examples:
- Example 1: all paths go through (2,2) which is banned, so 0
- Example 2: no banned cells, so for example (1,2): path goes through (1,2),(2,2),(3,2) - no banned cells, so valid. Answer 1
- Example 3: for (1,2): path goes through (1,2),(2,2),(3,2),(4,2) - (3,2) is banned, so invalid. For (1,3): (1,3)→(2,3)→(3,3)→(4,3) - (3,3) banned. For (4,2): (4,2)→(3,2)→(2,2)→(1,2) - (3,2) banned. For (4,3): (4,3)→(3,3)→(2,3)→(1,3) - (3,3) banned. For (2,1): path is row 2, columns 1 to 4: (2,1),(2,2),(2,3),(2,4) - no banned in row 2, so valid. Similarly (2,4) valid. So there are valid cells, answer should be 1.

So the answer is 1 in all cases where at least one valid cell exists.

Therefore, the algorithm is:

1. Read input
2. Store banned cells in a set
3. For each border non-corner cell (r,c):
   - If (r,c) is banned, skip
   - Determine the path it will take (vertical if on top/bottom row, horizontal if on left/right column)
   - Check if any cell in this path is banned
   - If no banned cell in path, this is a valid position
4. If at least one valid position exists, answer is 1, else 0.

Note: For a cell at (1,c), the path is all cells (i,c) for i from 1 to n.
For a cell at (n,c), the path is all cells (i,c) for i from n to 1.
For a cell at (r,1), the path is all cells (r,j) for j from 1 to n.
For a cell at (r,n), the path is all cells (r,j) for j from n to 1.

We can check if any cell in the path is banned by checking if any (i,c) or (r,j) is in the banned set.

Let me implement this.

```python
n, m = map(int, input().split())
banned = set()
for _ in range(m):
    x, y = map(int, input().split())
    banned.add((x, y))

# Check if any border non-corner cell has a path that avoids banned cells
valid_position_exists = False

# Top row: row 1, columns 2 to n-1
for c in range(2, n):
    if (1, c) in banned:
        continue
    # Check vertical path: column c, rows 1 to n
    path_valid = True
    for r in range(1, n+1):
        if (r, c) in banned:
            path_valid = False
            break
    if path_valid:
        valid_position_exists = True
        break

# Bottom row: row n, columns 2 to n-1
if not valid_position_exists:
    for c in range(2, n):
        if (n, c) in banned:
            continue
        # Check vertical path: column c, rows 1 to n
        path_valid = True
        for r in range(1, n+1):
            if (r, c) in banned:
                path_valid = False
                break
        if path_valid:
            valid_position_exists = True
            break

# Left column: column 1, rows 2 to n-1
if not valid_position_exists:
    for r in range(2, n):
        if (r, 1) in banned:
            continue
        # Check horizontal path: row r, columns 1 to n
        path_valid = True
        for c in range(1, n+1):
            if (r, c) in banned:
                path_valid = False
                break
        if path_valid:
            valid_position_exists = True
            break

# Right column: column n, rows 2 to n-1
if not valid_position_exists:
    for r in range(2, n):
        if (r, n) in banned:
            continue
        # Check horizontal path: row r, columns 1 to n
        path_valid = True
        for c in range(1, n+1):
            if (r, c) in banned:
                path_valid = False
                break
        if path_valid:
            valid_position_exists = True
            break

print(1 if valid_position_exists else 0)
```

This should solve the problem. The maximum points is 1 if there's at least one valid starting position, 0 otherwise.

Problem

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

Gerald plays the following game. He has a checkered field of size n × n cells, where m various cells are banned. Before the game, he has to put a few chips on some border (but not corner) board cells. Then for n - 1 minutes, Gerald every minute moves each chip into an adjacent cell. He moves each chip from its original edge to the opposite edge. Gerald loses in this game in each of the three cases:  At least one of the chips at least once fell to the banned cell.  At least once two chips were on the same cell.  At least once two chips swapped in a minute (for example, if you stand two chips on two opposite border cells of a row with even length, this situation happens in the middle of the row). 

In that case he loses and earns 0 points. When nothing like that happened, he wins and earns the number of points equal to the number of chips he managed to put on the board. Help Gerald earn the most points.


-----Input-----

The first line contains two space-separated integers n and m (2 ≤ n ≤ 1000, 0 ≤ m ≤ 10^5) — the size of the field and the number of banned cells. Next m lines each contain two space-separated integers. Specifically, the i-th of these lines contains numbers x_{i} and y_{i} (1 ≤ x_{i}, y_{i} ≤ n) — the coordinates of the i-th banned cell. All given cells are distinct.

Consider the field rows numbered from top to bottom from 1 to n, and the columns — from left to right from 1 to n.


-----Output-----

Print a single integer — the maximum points Gerald can earn in this game.


-----Examples-----
Input
3 1
2 2

Output
0

Input
3 0

Output
1

Input
4 3
3 1
3 2
3 3

Output
1



-----Note-----

In the first test the answer equals zero as we can't put chips into the corner cells.

In the second sample we can place one chip into either cell (1, 2), or cell (3, 2), or cell (2, 1), or cell (2, 3). We cannot place two chips.

In the third sample we can only place one chip into either cell (2, 1), or cell (2, 4).

The input will be stdin and you should print your solution to stdout

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have an n×n grid with m banned cells
2. We can place chips only on border cells that are NOT corners (so only edge cells that are not corners)
3. Each chip moves every minute to an adjacent cell, moving from one border to the opposite border
4. We lose if:
   - Any chip lands on a banned cell
   - Two chips occupy the same cell at any time
   - Two chips swap positions (they exchange places)
5. We want to maximize the number of chips we can place

## Key Insights

1. **Border cells (non-corner)**: For an n×n grid, the border cells that are not corners are:
   - Top edge: (1, 2) to (1, n-1)
   - Bottom edge: (n, 2) to (n, n-1) 
   - Left edge: (2, 1) to (n-1, 1)
   - Right edge: (2, n) to (n-1, n)

2. **Movement pattern**: Each chip starts on a border and moves toward the opposite border. After k minutes, a chip at position (x,y) will be at distance k from its starting position.

3. **Swap condition**: Two chips swap if they start at positions that are symmetric across the center of the grid and move toward each other. For example, if chip A starts at (1, j) and chip B starts at (n, j), they will swap after moving toward each other if they meet at the center.

4. **Collision condition**: Two chips occupy the same cell at some point if their paths intersect.

5. **Critical observation**: For any two chips to move without swapping or colliding, they must not be symmetric with respect to the center of the grid. If two chips start at symmetric positions (one on top edge at (1,j) and one on bottom edge at (n,j)), they will eventually swap positions when they meet at the middle, which violates the rules.

6. **Non-symmetric pairs**: Chips on the same row or column will collide if they move toward each other, so we need to ensure no two chips are on the same row/col in a way that causes collision or swap.

## Strategy

After analyzing the movement patterns:
- Chips on the same row from top to bottom (starting at different positions) will eventually collide
- Chips on the same column from left to right will eventually collide
- Symmetric chips (across center) will swap

Actually, let me reconsider. The key insight is that we cannot place two chips that are symmetric with respect to the center of the grid, because they will swap positions.

For any position (x,y) on the border (non-corner), its symmetric position is (n+1-x, n+1-y).

If we place a chip at (x,y) and another at (n+1-x, n+1-y), they will meet and swap at some point.

Therefore, we can only place chips on positions that don't have their symmetric counterpart also being available.

Additionally, we need to check if any path intersects with a banned cell.

Thus, the algorithm:
1. Identify all valid border cells (non-corner)
2. For each valid border cell, check if it's banned
3. If a cell is valid and not banned, mark it as available
4. For each available cell, mark its symmetric counterpart as unavailable (we can only choose one of each symmetric pair)
5. Count the maximum number of chips we can place

But there's one more constraint: we also need to ensure that no chip lands on a banned cell during its path.

So we need to check for each chip placement whether any position on its path (from start to opposite border) is banned.

For a chip at (x,y):
- If x = 1 (top edge), it moves down and will visit cells (1,y), (2,y), (3,y), ..., (n,y)
- If x = n (bottom edge), it moves up and will visit cells (n,y), (n-1,y), ..., (1,y)
- If y = 1 (left edge), it moves right and will visit cells (x,1), (x,2), (x,3), ..., (x,n)
- If y = n (right edge), it moves left and will visit cells (x,n), (x,n-1), ..., (x,1)

The path is essentially all cells in a straight line from start to opposite border.

## Algorithm

1. Read input
2. Create a set of banned cells
3. Identify all valid border cells (non-corner)
4. For each valid border cell, check if any cell on its path is banned
5. For each valid path, we can place at most one chip (because of the symmetric pairing - any two symmetric chips will swap)
6. We need to be careful about symmetric pairs - if we place a chip at (x,y), we cannot place at (n+1-x, n+1-y) because they swap

Actually, let me simplify. The key constraints are:
- Cannot place chips at banned cells
- Cannot place two chips at symmetric positions (they swap)
- Cannot place chips whose paths pass through banned cells

So the steps:
1. For each valid border position, check if its path contains any banned cells
2. For positions that don't have banned cells in their path, we can potentially place a chip
3. But we can only place one chip per symmetric pair

So:
1. Create banned set
2. Identify all valid border positions (non-corner)
3. For each valid border position, check if its path contains any banned cell
4. If yes, skip this position
5. If no, keep track of it
6. For all valid positions, group them by symmetric pairs: (x,y) and (n+1-x, n+1-y)
7. Count the number of symmetric pairs that have at least one valid position in the pair

Wait, actually we can place at most one chip per symmetric pair, and we can only place chips at positions whose paths don't contain banned cells.

So the algorithm:
- Create a set of banned cells
- Create a set of valid initial positions (border, non-corner, not banned)
- For each valid initial position, verify that its path doesn't contain any banned cells
- Group valid positions by their symmetric counterparts

```python
def solve():
    n, m = map(int, input().split())
    
    banned = set()
    for _ in range(m):
        x, y = map(int, input().split())
        banned.add((x, y))
    
    # Directions: top, bottom, left, right edges (non-corner)
    # For each edge, valid positions are from 2 to n-1
    valid_positions = []
    
    # Check each possible starting position
    for x in range(1, n + 1):
        for y in range(1, n + 1):
            # Must be on border and not corner
            if x == 1 or x == n or y == 1 or y == n:
                if (x == 1 or x == n) and (y == 1 or y == n):
                    continue  # corner cells are not allowed
                if (x == 1 or x == n) and (y == 1 or y == n):
                    continue
                
                # For a position on border, we need to check if its path contains banned cells
                path_has_banned = False
                
                if x == 1:  # top edge, moving down
                    for i in range(1, n + 1):
                        if (i, y) in banned:
                            path_has_banned = True
                            break
                elif x == n:  # bottom edge, moving up
                    for i in range(1, n + 1):
                        if (i, y) in banned:
                            path_has_banned = True
                            break
                elif y == 1:  # left edge, moving right
                    for i in range(1, n + 1):
                        if (x, i) in banned:
                            path_has_banned = True
                            break
                elif y == n:  # right edge, moving left
                    for i in range(1, n + 1):
                        if (x, i) in banned:
                            path_has_banned = True
                            break
                
                if not path_has_banned:
                    valid_positions.append((x, y))
    
    # Group by symmetric pairs
    # Symmetric of (x, y) is (n+1-x, n+1-y)
    seen = set()
    pairs = []
    
    for pos in valid_positions:
        x, y = pos
        sym_x, sym_y = n + 1 - x, n + 1 - y
        
        # Check if this symmetric pair is already processed
        sym_pos = (sym_x, sym_y)
        if sym_pos in seen:
            # This position is symmetric to one that's already been processed
            # We don't add it to pairs
            continue
            
        # Check if the symmetric position is also in valid_positions
        # If it is, we have a pair - but we only count pairs where at least one is valid
        if sym_pos in valid_positions:
            # We can place at most one chip in this pair
            pairs.append((pos, sym_pos))
            seen.add(pos)
            seen.add(sym_pos)
        else:
            # This position has no symmetric partner in valid_positions
            # So we can place a chip here
            seen.add(pos)
    
    # Actually, I need to reconsider. The symmetric relation is between (x,y) and (n+1-x, n+1-y)
    # If a position is valid and its symmetric counterpart is also valid, we can place only one chip
    # If a position is valid and its symmetric counterpart is invalid, we can place one chip
    
    # So count all valid positions, but group by symmetric pairs
    # For each pair of symmetric positions, we can have at most one chip
    # But the pair is defined by the relation (x,y) ~ (n+1-x, n+1-y)
    
    from collections import defaultdict
    pair_groups = defaultdict(int)
    
    for pos in valid_positions:
        x, y = pos
        sym_x, sym_y = n + 1 - x, n + 1 - y
        sym_pos = (sym_x, sym_y)
        
        # Use the lexicographically smaller one as key
        key = tuple(sorted([pos, sym_pos]))
        pair_groups[key] += 1
    
    # For each symmetric pair, we can have at most one chip
    # But actually, if a symmetric pair has two valid positions, we can choose one
    # If it has one valid position, we can choose that one
    # So count the number of distinct symmetric pairs
    result = 0
    for key in pair_groups:
        # Each symmetric pair contributes at most one chip
        result += 1
    
    print(result)

solve()
```

Let me re-read the problem: "at least once two chips were on the same cell" or "two chips swapped"

In my configuration with (2,1) and (2,4):
- Time 0: (2,1), (2,4)
- Time 1: (2,2), (2,3)
- Time 2: (2,3), (2,2) - wait, after 2 minutes: (2,2) → (2,3), (2,3) → (2,2) - so they swap positions at time 2!

Ah! This is the swap condition.

For chip at (2,1): after 1 minute at (2,2), after 2 minutes at (2,3)
For chip at (2,4): after 1 minute at (2,3), after 2 minutes at (2,2)

So at time 1: (2,2) and (2,3)
At time 2: (2,3) and (2,2) - they swap positions at time 2.

So they swap at minute 2, which violates the rule.

Therefore, we cannot place chips at (2,1) and (2,4).

In general, if two chips are on the same row or column, and are positioned such that they will meet at the center and exchange positions, then they swap.

For two chips on the same row, say (i,j) and (i,k), when will they swap?

Actually, if they are symmetric across the center of the row, they will swap.

For example, on row i, columns j and (n+1-j), then they will swap.

In our case, on row 2, columns 1 and 4 in a 4×4 grid:
- (2,1) and (2,4) are symmetric across the center of the row: (2,1) ↔ (2,4) because (4+1-1, 4+1-4) = (4,1) no.

For a position (x,y), the symmetric is (n+1-x, n+1-y)

For (2,1): (4-2+1, 4-1+1) = (3,4)
For (2,4): (4-2+1, 4-4+1) = (3,1)

So they are not symmetric to each other.

But in terms of the row, (2,1) and (2,4) are symmetric across the center of the row only if the center is at column 2.5.

Columns: 1,2,3,4. Center between 2 and 3.

(2,1) and (2,4) are not symmetric - they are at distance 1 and 3 from center.

(2,1) to center (2.5): distance 1.5
(2,4) to center: distance 1.5

Ah! They are symmetric across the center column.

For any row i, positions (i,j) and (i, n+1-j) are symmetric.

The symmetric of (i,j) under the grid symmetry is (n+1-i, n+1-j), not (i, n+1-j).

So (i,j) and (i, n+1-j) are not symmetric to each other unless i = n+1-i.

For example, i=2, n=4, i ≠ n+1-i = 3.

So they are not symmetric under the grid symmetry.

But do they swap? In the movement:

Chip at (2,1) on row 2: moves right: (2,1) → (2,2) → (2,3) → (2,4)
Chip at (2,4) on row 2: moves left: (2,4) → (2,3) → (2,2) → (2,1)

At time 1: (2,2) and (2,3)
At time 2: (2,3) and (2,2) - they swap at time 2.

So yes, they swap positions.

Therefore, any two chips on the same row or column that are symmetric across the row/column center will swap.

In general, if two chips are at (x,y) and (x, n+1-y), then when they move toward the center, they will swap at the center.

Similarly for columns.

Therefore, we cannot place two chips that are symmetric across the center of a row or column.

For a chip at (x, y), its "swap partner" on the same row is (x, n+1-y), and on the same column is (n+1-x, y).

Therefore, we need to ensure that for any valid position, we don't place a chip at its row-symmetric or column-symmetric position.

So the complete constraint is:
- Cannot place chips at banned cells or on their path
- Cannot place two chips at positions that are row-symmetric or column-symmetric (which would cause swap)

But row-symmetric positions are (x,y) and (x, n+1-y)
Column-symmetric positions are (x,y) and (n+1-x, y)

And the grid symmetry (x,y) ↔ (n+1-x, n+1-y) is more fundamental.

Actually, the swap condition includes both:
- Two chips on the same row that are symmetric across the row center
- Two chips on the same column that are symmetric across the column center

So in addition to the grid symmetry, we have row and column symmetry swaps.

Therefore, for each valid position, we need to exclude:
- Its row-symmetric partner: (x, n+1-y)
- Its column-symmetric partner: (n+1-x, y)
- Its grid-symmetric partner: (n+1-x, n+1-y)

And then we can place at most one chip per such group.

But actually, the row-symmetric and column-symmetric might not be present, and we need to group positions by the fundamental symmetry.

Perhaps the fundamental group is the orbit under the action of:
- (x,y) → (x, n+1-y)  // row symmetry
- (x,y) → (n+1-x, y)  // column symmetry
- (x,y) → (n+1-x, n+1-y) // grid symmetry

The group has order at most 4, and each group has 1, 2, or 4 positions.

For example, if x=2, y=1 in 4×4:
- (2,1)
- (2,4) = (2, 4+1-1) 
- (3,1) = (4+1-2,1)
- (3,4) = (4+1-2,4+1-1)

So group of 4.

If x=2, y=2 in 4×4:
- (2,2)
- (2,3) = (2,4+1-2)
- (3,2)
- (3,3)

Group of 4.

If on edge, might have fewer.

But in our earlier example with (2,1) and (2,4), they are in the same row-symmetric group, and will swap.

So we can only place one chip per such group.

Therefore, the algorithm is:

1. For each valid border non-corner position, check if its path contains any banned cell
2. Group valid positions into groups that are related by the symmetry transformations:
   - (x,y) → (x, n+1-y)
   - (x,y) → (n+1-x, y)
   - (x,y) → (n+1-x, n+1-y)
3. Each group can have at most one chip
4. Answer is the number of such groups

But we need to be careful about which positions are valid and in the groups.

Let me implement this:

```python
n, m = map(int, input().split())

banned = set()
for _ in range(m):
    x, y = map(int, input().split())
    banned.add((x, y))

# Function to get all symmetric positions of (x,y)
def get_symmetric_positions(x, y):
    positions = set()
    positions.add((x, y))
    # row symmetry: (x, n+1-y)
    positions.add((x, n+1-y))
    # column symmetry: (n+1-x, y)
    positions.add((n+1-x, y))
    # grid symmetry: (n+1-x, n+1-y)
    positions.add((n+1-x, n+1-y))
    return positions

valid_positions = []

# Check each border non-corner position
for x in range(1, n+1):
    for y in range(1, n+1):
        # Must be on border and not corner
        if (x == 1 or x == n or y == 1 or y == n) and not (x == 1 and y == 1) and not (x == 1 and y == n) and not (x == n and y == 1) and not (x == n and y == n):
            # Check if any cell on the path is banned
            path_has_banned = False
            if x == 1:  # top edge
                for i in range(1, n+1):
                    if (i, y) in banned:
                        path_has_banned = True
                        break
            elif x == n:  # bottom edge
                for i in range(1, n+1):
                    if (i, y) in banned:
                        path_has_banned = True
                        break
            elif y == 1:  # left edge
                for i in range(1, n+1):
                    if (x, i) in banned:
                        path_has_banned = True
                        break
            elif y == n:  # right edge
                for i in range(1, n+1):
                    if (x, i) in banned:
                        path_has_banned = True
                        break
            if not path_has_banned:
                valid_positions.append((x, y))

# Group valid positions into equivalence classes
seen = set()
groups = 0

for pos in valid_positions:
    x, y = pos
    if (x, y) in seen:
        continue
        
    # Get all symmetric positions
    group = get_symmetric_positions(x, y)
    
    # Check if any other valid position in this group is valid
    in_group = False
    for px, py in group:
        if (px, py) in valid_positions:
            in_group = True
            break
    
    if in_group:
        groups += 1
        # Mark all in this group as seen
        for px, py in group:
            seen.add((px, py))

print(groups)
```

Let me test with example 3: n=4, m=3, banned=(3,1),(3,2),(3,3)

Valid positions: (2,1), (2,4) (since (2,1) and (2,4) both have valid paths)

For (2,1):
- group: (2,1), (2,4), (3,1), (3,4)
- (3,1) is banned, so not in valid_positions
- (3,4): path (3,4) → (2,4) → (1,4) → (4,4) — (3,4) to (2,4) to (1,4) to (4,4)? Actually (3,4) on right edge, moving left: (3,4) → (3,3) → (3,2) → (3,1) — (3,3),(3,2),(3,1) are banned → invalid
- So only (2,1) and (2,4) in the group are valid? But (2,4) is in valid_positions

(2,4) is in valid_positions.

So the group has (2,1), (2,4), (3,1), (3,4) - (3,1) and (3,4) are invalid, so only (2,1) and (2,4) are valid.

Since at least one in group is valid, we increment groups by 1.

Then for (2,4): already in seen.

So groups = 1.

That matches expected output.

Example 2: n=3, m=0
Valid positions: (1,2), (3,2), (2,1), (2,3)

For (1,2): group: (1,2), (1,2) (n+1-1=2, n+1-2=1) → (2,1), (2,2), (3,2), (3,1)
- (2,1), (3,2) are valid
- So group has valid positions

For (3,2): symmetric to (1,2) and (2,1), (2,3) — might be in same group

So the group of (1,2) includes (1,2), (2,1), (2,3), (3,2) — all valid.

So when we process (1,2), we see it has valid positions in group, so groups += 1.

Then when we process (3,2) and (2,1), they are already in seen.

So groups = 1.

That matches expected output of 1.

Example 1: n=3, m=1, banned=(2,2)
Valid positions: only those whose path doesn't contain (2,2)

(1,2): path (1,2), (2,2), (3,2) — contains (2,2) → invalid
(3,2): path (3,2), (2,2), (1,2) — contains (2,2) → invalid
(2,1): path (2,1), (1,1), (2,2), (3,1) — contains (2,2) → invalid
(2,3): path (2,3), (1,3), (2,2), (3,3) — contains (2,2) → invalid

So valid_positions is empty → groups = 0.

That matches.

Therefore, the solution is correct.

```python
n, m = map(int, input().split())

banned = set()
for _ in range(m):
    x, y = map(int, input().split())
    banned.add((x, y))

# Function to get all symmetric positions of (x,y)
def get_symmetric_positions(x, y):
    positions = set()
    positions.add((x, y))
    positions.add((x, n+1-y))
    positions.add((n+1-x, y))
    positions.add((n+1-x, n+1-y))
    return positions

valid_positions = []

# Check each border non-corner position
for x in range(1, n+1):
    for y in range(1, n+1):
        # Must be on border and not corner
        if (x == 1 or x == n or y == 1 or y == n):
            # Skip corners
            if (x == 1 and y == 1) or (x == 1 and y == n) or (x == n and y == 1) or (x == n and y == n):
                continue
                
            # Check if any cell on the path is banned
            path_has_banned = False
            if x == 1:  # top edge
                for i in range(1, n+1):
                    if (i, y) in banned:
                        path_has_banned = True
                        break
            elif x == n:  # bottom edge
                for i in range(1, n+1):
                    if (i, y) in banned:
                        path_has_banned = True
                        break
            elif y == 1:  # left edge
                for i in range(1, n+1):
                    if (x, i) in banned:
                        path_has_banned = True
                        break
            elif y == n:  # right edge
                for i in range(1, n+1):
                    if (x, i) in banned:
                        path_has_banned = True
                        break
            if not path_has_banned:
                valid_positions.append((x, y))

# Group valid positions into equivalence classes
seen = set()
groups = 0

for pos in valid_positions:
    x, y = pos
    if (x, y) in seen:
        continue
        
    # Get all symmetric positions
    group = get_symmetric_positions(x, y)
    
    # Check if any position in this group is valid (in valid_positions)
    has_valid = any((px, py) in valid_positions for px, py in group)
    if has_valid:
        groups += 1
        # Mark all positions in this group as seen
        for px, py in group:
            seen.add((px, py))

print(groups)
```

n, m = map(int, input().split())

banned = set()
for _ in range(m):
    x, y = map(int, input().split())
    banned.add((x, y))

# Function to get all symmetric positions of (x,y)
def get_symmetric_positions(x, y):
    positions = set()
    positions.add((x, y))
    positions.add((x, n+1-y))
    positions.add((n+1-x, y))
    positions.add((n+1-x, n+1-y))
    return positions

valid_positions = []

# Check each border non-corner position
for x in range(1, n+1):
    for y in range(1, n+1):
        # Must be on border and not corner
        if (x == 1 or x == n or y == 1 or y == n):
            # Skip corners
            if (x == 1 and y == 1) or (x == 1 and y == n) or (x == n and y == 1) or (x == n and y == n):
                continue
                
            # Check if any cell on the path is banned
            path_has_banned = False
            if x == 1:  # top edge
                for i in range(1, n+1):
                    if (i, y) in banned:
                        path_has_banned = True
                        break
            elif x == n:  # bottom edge
                for i in range(1, n+1):
                    if (i, y) in banned:
                        path_has_banned = True
                        break
            elif y == 1:  # left edge
                for i in range(1, n+1):
                    if (x, i) in banned:
                        path_has_banned = True
                        break
            elif y == n:  # right edge
                for i in range(1, n+1):
                    if (x, i) in banned:
                        path_has_banned = True
                        break
            if not path_has_banned:
                valid_positions.append((x, y))

# Group valid positions into equivalence classes
seen = set()
groups = 0

for pos in valid_positions:
    x, y = pos
    if (x, y) in seen:
        continue
        
    # Get all symmetric positions
    group = get_symmetric_positions(x, y)
    
    # Check if any position in this group is valid (in valid_positions)
    has_valid = any((px, py) in valid_positions for px, py in group)
    if has_valid:
        groups += 1
        # Mark all positions in this group as seen
        for px, py in group:
            seen.add((px, py))

print(groups)

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

Gerald plays the following game. He has a checkered field of size n × n cells, where m various cells are banned. Before the game, he has to put a few chips on some border (but not corner) board cells. Then for n - 1 minutes, Gerald every minute moves each chip into an adjacent cell. He moves each chip from its original edge to the opposite edge. Gerald loses in this game in each of the three cases:  At least one of the chips at least once fell to the banned cell.  At least once two chips were on the same cell.  At least once two chips swapped in a minute (for example, if you stand two chips on two opposite border cells of a row with even length, this situation happens in the middle of the row). 

In that case he loses and earns 0 points. When nothing like that happened, he wins and earns the number of points equal to the number of chips he managed to put on the board. Help Gerald earn the most points.


-----Input-----

The first line contains two space-separated integers n and m (2 ≤ n ≤ 1000, 0 ≤ m ≤ 10^5) — the size of the field and the number of banned cells. Next m lines each contain two space-separated integers. Specifically, the i-th of these lines contains numbers x_{i} and y_{i} (1 ≤ x_{i}, y_{i} ≤ n) — the coordinates of the i-th banned cell. All given cells are distinct.

Consider the field rows numbered from top to bottom from 1 to n, and the columns — from left to right from 1 to n.


-----Output-----

Print a single integer — the maximum points Gerald can earn in this game.


-----Examples-----
Input
3 1
2 2

Output
0

Input
3 0

Output
1

Input
4 3
3 1
3 2
3 3

Output
1

In the third sample we can only place one chip into either cell (2, 1), or cell (2, 4).

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have an n×n grid with m banned cells
2. We can only place chips on border cells that are NOT corners (so only edge cells that are not at corners)
3. Each chip moves from its starting border position toward the opposite edge, one step per minute for n-1 minutes
4. We lose if:
   - Any chip lands on a banned cell
   - Two chips occupy the same cell at the same time
   - Two chips swap positions (they exchange places)

## Key Insights

1. **Border cells**: The border cells are on the edges (top, bottom, left, right). Corners are (1,1), (1,n), (n,1), (n,n). So valid border positions are:
   - Top edge: (1, 2) to (1, n-1)
   - Bottom edge: (n, 2) to (n, n-1)
   - Left edge: (2, 1) to (n-1, 1)
   - Right edge: (2, n) to (n-1, n)

2. **Movement pattern**: Each chip moves from its starting position toward the opposite edge. For example:
   - A chip at (1, j) moves down to (n, j)
   - A chip at (n, j) moves up to (1, j)
   - A chip at (i, 1) moves right to (i, n)
   - A chip at (i, n) moves left to (i, 1)

3. **When do chips swap?**: Two chips swap if they start at positions that are symmetric with respect to the center. For example, if we have chips at (1, j) and (n, j), they will meet at the middle row, and if n is odd, they meet at row (n+1)/2, and if they're at the same column, they will be at the same position, which causes a collision.

4. **When do chips meet?**: Two chips meet if they are on opposite edges and moving toward each other. For example:
   - Chip at (1, j) moving down and chip at (n, j) moving up will meet at row (n+1)/2 when n is odd
   - Chips moving horizontally will meet at column (1+n)/2 when n is odd

5. **Key constraint - no swapping**: Two chips swap if they start at symmetric positions. For example, chips at (1, j) and (n, j) will be at the same position at minute (n-1)/2 if n is odd.

6. **Key constraint - no collision**: Two chips cannot be at the same position at the same time.

## Analysis

For a chip to move from a border position to the opposite edge:
- A chip at (1, j) moves down to (n, j) - it passes through rows 2, 3, ..., n
- A chip at (n, j) moves up to (1, j) - it passes through rows n-1, n-2, ..., 1
- A chip at (i, 1) moves right to (i, n) - it passes through columns 2, 3, ..., n
- A chip at (i, n) moves left to (i, 1) - it passes through columns n-1, n-2, ..., 1

Two chips swap only if they start on opposite edges and at symmetric positions, and the midpoint is reachable.

For two chips to NOT swap or collide, we need to carefully choose starting positions.

## Critical Insight

- If n is odd: The middle row (or column) is row (n+1)//2. Chips moving from top to bottom will pass through the middle row at time (n-1)/2. Similarly for horizontal movement.
- Two chips can't be on the same cell at the same time.
- Two chips from opposite edges at the same column will meet exactly at the middle if n is odd, which means they will occupy the same cell at the same time - this violates the rule.

Therefore, we cannot place chips on symmetric positions.

## Strategy

The maximum number of chips we can place is limited by:
1. The number of available border cells (excluding corners)
2. The constraint that no two chips can meet or swap

For horizontal movement (top/bottom edges):
- We cannot place chips at positions that would cause them to meet in the middle
- For a chip at (1, j) and (n, j), they meet at row (n+1)//2
- So we can place at most one chip in any column on the top or bottom edge

For vertical movement (left/right edges):
- We cannot place chips at positions that would cause them to meet in the middle
- For a chip at (i, 1) and (i, n), they meet at column (n+1)//2
- So we can place at most one chip in any row on the left or right edge

Additionally, chips from different edges can potentially meet in the middle.

## Final Solution Approach

1. The maximum number of chips we can place is limited by the number of available border positions minus conflicts that would cause swaps or collisions.

3. The key insight is that if n is odd, then any two chips moving toward each other on the same row or column will meet at the center, which is forbidden.

4. Therefore, for any column, we can have at most one chip (either top or bottom, but not both).
5. For any row, we can have at most one chip (either left or right, but not both).

- If n is even: There's no central row or column where chips meet. Chips moving from opposite edges will never occupy the same cell at the same time.
- If n is odd: Chips will meet at the center row or column if they're on opposite edges in the same row/column.

- At time t, chip from top is at (1+t, j)
- At time t, chip from bottom is at (n-t, j)

At that exact time, they occupy the same cell, which violates the rule.

Actually, for odd n, if we have a chip at (1, j) and (n, j), they meet at row (n+1)/2 at time (n-1)/2, which means they occupy the same cell at the same time - violation.

Similarly for (i, 1) and (i, n) meeting at column (n+1)/2.

So for odd n:
- For each column j (2 ≤ j ≤ n-1), we can have at most one chip (on top or bottom)

Similarly for other directions.

So the algorithm:

1. For each possible border position, check if it's banned
2. For each column j from 2 to n-1:

   - Check if placing a chip at (n, j) is valid (no banned cells in column j from row n to 1)

   - Check if placing a chip at (i, n) is valid (no banned cells in row i from col n to 1)
   - We can choose at most one of these two

But wait, the problem says "two chips swapped" - this happens when they exchange positions. In our movement, a chip at (1, j) moves to (2, j) at time 1, and a chip at (n, j) moves to (n-1, j) at time 1. They don't swap - they just pass through different cells. 

The "swap" is when they happen to be at the same position at the same minute. For example, if at time t, chip A is at position P and chip B is at position P, and they were at different positions at time t-1 and t+1, then they "swap".

But in our movement, they're moving in opposite directions, so if they meet at the same position at the same time, they occupy the same cell - this is a collision, which is forbidden.

So if they meet at the same position at the same time, it's a violation.

This happens when:
- For a vertical pair in column j: they meet at row (1 + n - t) and (1 + t) for chip from top and bottom respectively. They meet when t = (n-1)/2, which requires (n-1) to be even, i.e., n is odd.

So only when n is odd, and they meet at the exact center.

Therefore:
- For odd n: we cannot have both a chip at (1, j) and (n, j) for any j
- For odd n: we cannot have both a chip at (i, 1) and (i, n) for any i
- For even n: chips never meet at the same position at the same time, so we can have multiple chips as long as they don't land on banned cells

So the solution:

1. Count total possible valid border positions (excluding corners, not banned)
2. For odd n, we need to remove conflicts:

   - For each row i from 2 to n-1, we can only use one of left or right (if both are available)

But actually, we can choose which one to use, so we want to maximize the count.

For each column j (2 ≤ j ≤ n-1), we can take at most one chip (either top or bottom) if that position is valid.
For each row i (2 ≤ i ≤ n-1), we can take at most one chip (either left or right) if that position is valid.

But there's overlap - the same position can't be used twice.

Actually, the positions are disjoint:
- Top edge: (1, j) for j = 2 to n-1
- Bottom edge: (n, j) for j = 2 to n-1  
- Left edge: (i, 1) for i = 2 to n-1
- Right edge: (i, n) for i = 2 to n-1

These are all different positions.

So the answer is:
- If n is even: total valid border positions (excluding corners, not banned)
- If n is odd: we can select at most one from each column (top/bottom) and at most one from each row (left/right), so we need to compute:
  - For each column j in [2, n-1]: count valid positions (1,j) or (n,j) that are not banned
  - For each row i in [2, n-1]: count valid positions (i,1) or (i,n) that are not banned
  - But we can only pick one from each column pair and one from each row pair

Actually, the maximum we can get is the sum of:
- For each column j, we can get 1 if at least one of (1,j) or (n,j) is valid
- For each row i, we can get 1 if at least one of (i,1) or (i,n) is valid

But we need to check if there are any conflicts with banned cells in the path.

For a chip at (1, j) to be valid: all cells in column j from row 1 to n must not be banned (actually, specifically, the path from (1,j) to (n,j) - all positions (r,j) for r in [1,n])
Similarly for (n,j) to be valid: all positions (r,j) for r in [1,n]

For (i,1): all positions (i,c) for c in [1,n] - specifically (i,1) to (i,n)
For (i,n): all positions (i,c) for c in [1,n]

So we need to check for any banned cell in the path.

But if a cell (r,j) is banned, then neither (1,j) nor (n,j) can be placed.

So the algorithm:

```python
n, m = map(int, input().split())
banned = set()
for _ in range(m):
    x, y = map(int, input().split())
    banned.add((x, y))

# If n is even, we can place a chip at any valid non-corner border cell
# If n is odd, we can place chips such that no two are in the same row or column pair

# For even n: just count valid border cells
# For odd n: we can have at most one per column pair and one per row pair

if n % 2 == 0:
    # Count valid non-corner border cells
    count = 0
    for j in range(2, n):  # columns 2 to n-1
        # top edge (1, j)
        if (1, j) not in banned:
            count += 1
        # bottom edge (n, j)
        if (n, j) not in banned:
            count += 1
    for i in range(2, n):  # rows 2 to n-1
        # left edge (i, 1)
        if (i, 1) not in banned:
            count += 1
        # right edge (i, n)
        if (i, n) not in banned:
            count += 1
    print(count)
else:
    # n is odd
    # We can place at most one per column (2 to n-1) and one per row (2 to n-1)
    # But we need to check if a column has a valid position in top/bottom or row has valid position in left/right
    
    # Count the maximum possible
    count = 0
    
    # For each column j from 2 to n-1, we can take at most one: either top or bottom
    # But we check if (1,j) or (n,j) is valid (and not banned)
    col_choices = 0
    for j in range(2, n):
        if (1, j) not in banned:
            col_choices += 1
        if (n, j) not in banned:
            col_choices += 1
    
    # Actually, we can take at most one per column, so it's the number of columns where at least one is valid
    # But we can't take both in a column
    valid_cols = 0
    for j in range(2, n):
        if (1, j) not in banned or (n, j) not in banned:
            valid_cols += 1
    
    # For each row i from 2 to n-1, we can take at most one: either left or right
    valid_rows = 0
    for i in range(2, n):
        if (i, 1) not in banned or (i, n) not in banned:
            valid_rows += 1
    
    # But we need to check if any cell in the path is banned
    # For a chip at (1,j), we need all cells in column j to be valid (no banned cells)
    # For a chip at (n,j), we need all cells in column j to be valid
    # For a chip at (i,1), we need all cells in row i to be valid
    # For a chip at (i,n), we need all cells in row i to be valid
    
    # So we need to check for each column j, if there's any banned cell in column j, then neither (1,j) nor (n,j) is valid
    # Similarly for rows
    
    # First, check if any column has banned cells
    col_has_banned = [False] * (n + 1)
    row_has_banned = [False] * (n + 1)
    
    for x, y in banned:
        if y >= 2 and y <= n-1:  # column j where 2 <= j <= n-1
            col_has_banned[y] = True
        if x >= 2 and x <= n-1:  # row i where 2 <= i <= n-1
            row_has_banned[x] = True
    
    # For a column j, if col_has_banned[j] is True, then we cannot place any chip in that column
    # For a row i, if row_has_banned[i] is True, then we cannot place any chip in that row
    
    valid_cols = 0
    for j in range(2, n):
        if not col_has_banned[j]:
            valid_cols += 1
    
    valid_rows = 0
    for i in range(2, n):
        if not row_has_banned[i]:
            valid_rows += 1
    
    # But we also need to ensure that if a cell is in the path and banned, it's not used
    # Actually, if any cell in column j is banned, then we cannot use column j at all
    
    # So the maximum is the sum of valid columns and valid rows
    count = valid_cols + valid_rows
    print(count)
```

Wait, I think I made a mistake. Let me reconsider the path checking.

For a chip to move from (1,j) to (n,j), it needs to pass through all cells in column j. If any cell (r,j) is banned for any r, then we cannot place a chip at (1,j) or (n,j) in that column.

Similarly, for a chip to move from (i,1) to (i,n), it needs to pass through all cells in row i. If any cell (i,c) is banned for any c, then we cannot place a chip at (i,1) or (i,n).

So the fix:

```python
n, m = map(int, input().split())
banned = set()
for _ in range(m):
    x, y = map(int, input().split())
    banned.add((x, y))

# For even n, we can place a chip at any valid non-corner border cell
# For odd n, we have the constraint that we can only place one per column (top/bottom) and one per row (left/right)

if n % 2 == 0:
    # Count valid non-corner border cells - just check if the cell itself is banned
    count = 0
    # Top edge: (1, j) for j from 2 to n-1
    for j in range(2, n):
        if (1, j) not in banned:
            count += 1
    # Bottom edge: (n, j) for j from 2 to n-1
    for j in range(2, n):
        if (n, j) not in banned:
            count += 1
    # Left edge: (i, 1) for i from 2 to n-1
    for i in range(2, n):
        if (i, 1) not in banned:
            count += 1
    # Right edge: (i, n) for i from 2 to n-1
    for i in range(2, n):
        if (i, n) not in banned:
            count += 1
    print(count)
else:
    # n is odd: we need to check if the entire column or row is banned
    # For column j (2 to n-1): a chip at (1,j) is valid only if no banned cell in column j
    # Similarly for (n,j)
    # For row i (2 to n-1): a chip at (i,1) is valid only if no banned cell in row i
    # Similarly for (i,n)
    
    # Check for each column j if any cell in column j is banned
    col_banned = [False] * (n + 1)
    row_banned = [False] * (n + 1)
    
    for x, y in banned:
        if y >= 2 and y <= n-1:
            col_banned[y] = True
        if x >= 2 and x <= n-1:
            row_banned[x] = True
    
    # For each column j from 2 to n-1, if col_banned[j] is False, we can place one chip (either top or bottom)
    count = 0
    for j in range(2, n):
        if not col_banned[j]:
            count += 1
    
    # For each row i from 2 to n-1, if row_banned[i] is False, we can place one chip (either left or right)
    for i in range(2, n):
        if not row_banned[i]:
            count += 1
    
    print(count)
```

At time 1: chip at (1,2) goes to (2,2), chip at (2,1) goes to (2,2) - they meet at (2,2) at time 1.

This is a violation.

So if we place chips in different columns and rows, they might still meet.

The only safe positions are positions that are at distance from the center such that they don't meet.

Actually, in a 3x3 grid, if we place two chips, they will meet at the center (2,2) if they are on opposite edges.

So the maximum is 1.

Therefore, the constraint is not just one per column or row, but we need to ensure no two chips meet at any time.

For any two chips, their paths should not intersect.

For example:
- A chip from (1,2) and (2,1): at time 1, both go to (2,2) - meet
- A chip from (1,2) and (3,2): at time 1, (1,2)->(2,2), (3,2)->(2,2) - meet at (2,2)
- A chip from (1,2) and (2,3): (1,2)->(2,2), (2,3)->(2,2) - meet at (2,2)

So in a 3x3 grid, any two chips will meet at the center at time 1.

Therefore, we can only have one chip.

If n is even, say n=4, then the center is not a single cell, so chips from (1,j) and (4,j) meet at (2.5, j) which doesn't exist. So they never occupy the same cell at the same time.

For even n, we can have multiple chips as long as they don't land on banned cells.

For example, in 4x4, a chip at (1,2) goes to (4,2), a chip at (1,3) goes to (4,3), they never occupy the same cell.

So the solution is:

- If n is even: we can place up to the number of valid non-corner border cells
- If n is odd: we can only place one chip (since any two will meet at the center)

Wait, is that true? What if we place a chip at (1,2) and a chip at (2,1)? They meet at (2,2) at time 1.

In a 3x3 grid, all pairs of non-corner border cells will meet at (2,2) at time 1.

So indeed, in odd n, we can only have one chip.

But what about in larger odd n? For example, n=5.

- A chip at (1,2) moves to (5,2): at time t, it's at (1+t, 2)
- A chip at (1,3) moves to (5,3): at time t, it's at (1+t, 3)
- They don't meet at the same cell at the same time.

- A chip at (1,2) and (5,2): at time t, (1+t,2) and (5-t,2). They meet when 1+t = 5-t, so t=2, at position (3,2). This is a meeting.

So they meet at time 2 at (3,2).

Similarly, any two chips on the same column will meet at the middle if n is odd.

Similarly, any two chips on the same row will meet at the middle.

But chips on different columns don't necessarily meet.

For example, chip at (1,2) and (1,3): both start on top edge, they move down, at time t they are at (1+t,2) and (1+t,3) - never the same cell.

So only symmetric pairs on the same column or row meet.

Therefore, in odd n:
- We can have at most one chip per column (top/bottom) and one per row (left/right)
- But we also need to ensure that for any two chips, their paths don't intersect.

Actually, two chips from different columns and different rows might still intersect.

For example, chip at (1,2) and (3,1): 
- (1,2) at time t: (1+t, 2)
- (3,1) at time t: (3, 1+t)
- They meet when 1+t = 3 and 2 = 1+t, so t=2 and t=1 - impossible.

So no intersection.

But chip at (1,2) and (5,2) will meet at (3,2) at time 2.

So if we have a chip on both top and bottom of the same column, they meet in the middle.

Therefore, in odd n:
- We cannot have both top and bottom in the same column
- We cannot have both left and right in the same row
- But we can have chips in different columns and different rows, as long as they don't meet.

Do any two chips from different columns and different rows meet?

In odd n, for a chip at (1,j) and (i,1), at time t:
- (1+t, j) and (i, 1+t)
- They meet when 1+t = i and j = 1+t, so t = i-1 and t = j-1, so i-1 = j-1, i = j.

So only if i = j do they meet.

When i = j, at time t = j-1, they are at (1+(j-1), j) = (j, j) and (j, 1+(j-1)) = (j, j).

So they meet at (j,j) at time j-1.

Therefore, if we have a chip at (1,j) and (j,1), they meet at (j,j).

Similarly, (1,j) and (i,n) meet when 1+t = i and j = n-t, so t = i-1 and t = n-j, so i-1 = n-j, i = n-j+1.

So they meet if i = n-j+1.

Similarly, (n,j) and (i,1) meet at (n-t, j) and (i, 1+t), when n-t = i and j = 1+t, so t = j-1 and t = n-i, so j-1 = n-i, i = n-j+1.

And (n,j) and (i,n) meet when n-t = i and j = n-t, so t = n-i and t = n-j, so i = j.

Therefore, chips from different edges will meet if:
- Same column (top/bottom) - meet at center
- Same row (left/right) - meet at center
- (1,j) and (i,1) - meet at (j,j) if i = j
- (1,j) and (i,n) - meet at (n-j+1, j) if i = n-j+1
- (n,j) and (i,1) - meet at (n-j+1, j) if i = n-j+1
- (n,j) and (i,n) - meet at (j,j) if i = j

This is very complex. Actually, for the maximum number of chips, a known result from competitive programming is that in odd n you can only place one chip, and in even n you can place up to the number of valid border positions.

But let me check the third example:
Input: 4 3
3 1
3 2
3 3
Output: 1

n=4 (even), so according to my even rule, we should be able to place more than 1.

Border cells (non-corner):
- Top: (1,2), (1,3)
- Bottom: (4,2), (4,3)
- Left: (2,1), (3,1)
- Right: (2,4), (3,4)

Banned: (3,1), (3,2), (3,3)

So valid positions:
- (1,2): not banned ✓
- (1,3): not banned ✓
- (4,2): not banned ✓
- (4,3): not banned ✓
- (2,1): not banned ✓
- (3,1): banned ✗
- (2,4): not banned ✓
- (3,4): not banned ✓ (3,4) is not banned)

So valid positions: (1,2), (1,3), (4,2), (4,3), (2,1), (2,4), (3,4)

That's 7 valid positions. But output is 1.

Why? Because of the swap rule.

The problem says: "At least once two chips were on the same cell" or "two chips swapped"

In this case, if we place two chips, say at (1,2) and (4,2), they are in the same column. They will meet at t=1.5 when n=4? At time 1: (2,2) and (3,2). At time 2: (3,2) and (2,2). They swap positions.

The problem says "at least once two chips swapped" - this happens when they exchange positions.

In this case, at time 1: chip from top at (2,2), chip from bottom at (3,2)
At time 2: chip from top at (3,2), chip from bottom at (2,2)

They have swapped. So this is a violation.

For even n, when two chips are in the same column, they will swap at time (n-1)/2. For n=4, (n-1)/2 = 1.5, which is not an integer.

At time 1: (2,2) and (3,2)
At time 2: (3,2) and (2,2)

So at time 2, they are at the positions they were at time 1.

Do they swap? The positions are exchanged.

In minute 1, chip A is at (2,2), chip B is at (3,2)
In minute 2, chip A is at (3,2), chip B is at (2,2)

So they have swapped. This is a violation of the "swap" rule.

Therefore, for any two chips in the same column or same row, they will swap (since for even n, the meeting time is not integer, but they pass through each other).

For two chips in the same column:
- At time t, chip from top is at (1+t, j)
- At time t, chip from bottom is at (n-t, j)
- They swap when they meet at the same position during the minute, but in even n, they are at different positions at integer times.

However, in the example, at time 1: (2,2) and (3,2) are different positions
At time 2: (3,2) and (2,2) are the same positions as at time 1, but swapped.

The rule says "at least once two chips swapped in a minute" - this might mean that during a minute, they exchange positions.

In minute 2, chip from top goes from (3,2) to (4,2), but in our movement, it's at (3,2) at time 1, then at (4,2) at time 2.

I think I have a misunderstanding of the movement.

The problem says: "every minute moves each chip into an adjacent cell"

So from (1,2) to (2,2) at time 1
From (4,2) to (3,2) at time 1
At time 2: (2,2) to (3,2), (3,2) to (2,2)

So at time 1: positions (2,2) and (3,2)
At time 2: positions (3,2) and (2,2)

So the positions are swapped. This is a swap.

Therefore, any two chips on the same column with even n will swap at time (n-1)//2? For n=4, at time 1 and 2, they are at different positions, and at time 2 they swap to the previous positions.

In minute 2, they exchange positions. This is a swap.

Therefore, for even n, we cannot have two chips in the same column or same row.

So the solution is:

- If n is even: we can have at most one chip per column and one per row, but specifically, we can have at most one chip, because if we have two in different places, they might still swap or meet.

Actually, for even n, two chips in the same row or column will swap.

For two chips in the same row: both move in the row, say at (i,1) and (i,3), they move right and left respectively. At time t, (i,1+t) and (i,n-t). They swap when 1+t = n-t and t = n-t, so 2t = n-1, which is not integer if n is even.

For example, n=4: at time 1: (i,2) and (i,3)
 at time 2: (i,3) and (i,2)

So they swap at time 2. This is a violation.

Therefore, for even n, we cannot have two chips in the same column or same row.

Therefore, we can have at most one chip.

For odd n, as we saw, any two chips in the same column or row will meet at the center or swap, so we can have at most one chip.

Therefore, in all cases, the maximum number of chips is 1, unless there is no valid border cell.

But wait, in example 2: n=3, m=0, output is 1.

In example 3: n=4, m=3, output is 1.

In example 1: n=3, m=1, output is 0.

So when is it 0? When there are no valid border cells.

Example 1: n=3, banned at (2,2)

- (1,2): not banned, so valid

- (1,2), (1,3) - (1,3) is corner? No, (1,3) is corner.

Another possibility: "border (but not corner)" might mean only on the border, and specifically in the examples, for n=3, the only non-corner border cells are (1,2), (3,2), (2,1), (2,3) - and if any of these are banned, we cannot place a chip there.

In example 1, (2,2) is banned, and it's not on the border? (2,2) is not on the border - it's in the middle.

Border cells are only on edges.

(2,2) is not on the border, so it doesn't affect the path of any chip from a border cell.

For a chip at (1,2): path is (1,2), (2,2), (3,2) - so if (2,2) is banned, then the chip cannot move through it.

Therefore, for a chip to be placed at (1,2), the path from (1,2) to (3,2) must not have any banned cells.

Similarly for (3,2): path includes (3,2), (2,2), (1,2) - so (2,2) is banned, so cannot place at (3,2).

For (2,1): path from (2,1) to (2,3): (2,1), (2,2), (2,3) - (2,2) is banned, so cannot place.

For (2,3): path from (2,3) to (2,1): (2,3), (2,2), (2,1) - (2,2) is banned, so cannot place.

Therefore, no valid chip positions.

So the rule is: for a chip to be placed at a starting position, the entire path must not contain any banned cells.

For a chip at (1,j): the path is all (r,j) for r from 1 to n
For a chip at (n,j): the path is all (r,j) for r from 1 to n
For a chip at (i,1): the path is all (i,c) for c from 1 to n
For a chip at (i,n): the path is all (i,c) for c from 1 to n

So the algorithm is:

1. For each valid non-corner border cell, check if any cell in its path is banned
2. If not, then that position is valid
3. Then we need to select a subset of valid positions such that:
   - No two chips are on the same cell at the same time (avoid collisions)
   - No two chips swap (avoid swaps)

For even n: two chips in the same column or row will swap at some point, so we can have at most one chip per column and one per row, but actually they will swap, so we can have only one chip in the entire grid.

For odd n: two chips in the same column or row will meet at the center, so again, only one chip.

Therefore, in all cases, we can have at most one chip.

And we can have one chip if there exists at least one valid non-corner border cell (where the path has no banned cells).

Otherwise, 0.

So the solution is:

- For each non-corner border cell, check if the entire path (all positions in the column or row) has no banned cells
- If there is at least one such cell, answer is 1
- Otherwise, answer is 0

Let me verify with the examples:

Example 1: n=3, m=1, banned=(2,2)
- (1,2): path includes (1,2), (2,2), (3,2) - (2,2) is banned, so invalid
- (3,2): path includes (3,2), (2,2), (1,2) - (2,2) banned, invalid
- (2,1): path includes (2,1), (2,2), (2,3) - (2,2) banned, invalid
- (2,3): path includes (2,3), (2,2), (2,1) - (2,2) banned, invalid
- None valid, so output 0 ✓

Example 2: n=3, m=0
- (1,2): path (1,2), (2,2), (3,2) - no banned, valid
- So we can place one chip, output 1 ✓

Example 3: n=4, m=3, banned=(3,1), (3,2), (3,3)
- (1,2): path (1,2), (2,2), (3,2), (4,2) - (3,2) is banned, invalid
- (1,3): path (1,3), (2,3), (3,3), (4,3) - (3,3) banned, invalid
- (4,2): path (4,2), (3,2), (2,2), (1,2) - (3,2) banned, invalid
- (4,3): path (4,3), (3,3), (2,3), (1,3) - (3,3) banned, invalid
- (2,1): path (2,1), (3,1), (4,1), (3,1) is banned, invalid
- (3,1): banned, not in non-corner
- (2,4): path (2,4), (3,4), (4,4), (3,4) - no banned? (3,4) not banned, (3,4) is not in banned list, so path is (2,4), (3,4), (4,4) - no banned, valid
- (3,4): path (3,4), (2,4), (1,4), (4,4) - (3,4) is not banned, so path is all (3,c) for c in [1,4] - (3,1), (3,2), (3,3) are banned, so (3,4) is not banned, but the path includes (3,4), (3,3) - (3,3) is banned, so invalid

For (2,4): path is (2,4), (3,4), (4,4), (1,4) - no, for a chip at (2,4), it moves to (3,4) at time 1, then (4,4) at time 2, then (4,4) at time 3? 

It moves from (2,4) to (3,4) to (4,4) - only to (4,4) at time 2.

The path is only the cells it visits: (2,4) at time 0, (3,4) at time 1, (4,4) at time 2.

So only positions (2,4), (3,4), (4,4)

Banned cells: (3,1), (3,2), (3,3) - (3,4) is not banned, so path is valid.

Therefore, (2,4) is valid.

Similarly, (3,4) is at (3,4) at time 0, then (2,4) at time 1, then (1,4) at time 2.

Path: (3,4), (2,4), (1,4)

(3,4) is not banned, so valid.

But (3,4) is in the path of (3,4) at time 0, and (2,4) at time 1, and (1,4) at time 2.

No banned cells in the path? (3,4) is not banned, (2,4) not banned, (1,4) not banned.

So (3,4) is valid.

For (3,4): path is (3,4), (2,4), (1,4) - no banned cells, so valid.

So both (2,4) and (3,4) are valid.

But the output is 1, not 2.

Why? Because if we place two chips, they will swap.

For example, chip at (2,4) and (3,4):
- At time 0: (2,4), (3,4)
- At time 1: (3,4), (2,4) - they swap
- So they swap in minute 1 - violation

Therefore, we cannot have two chips.

In fact, for any two chips, if they are on the same row or column, they will eventually swap or meet.

For even n, any two chips in the same row or column will swap at some point.

Therefore, we can have at most one chip.

So the answer is 1 if there is at least one valid non-corner border cell, otherwise 0.

Therefore, the final solution is:

```python
n, m = map(int, input().split())
banned = set()
for _ in range(m):
    x, y = map(int, input().split())
    banned.add((x, y))

# Check if any valid non-corner border cell has a path with no banned cells
has_valid_position = False

# Top edge: (1, j) for j from 2 to n-1
for j in range(2, n):
    # Path: all (r, j) for r from 1 to n
    valid = True
    for r in range(1, n+1):
        if (r, j) in banned:
            valid = False
            break
    if valid:
        has_valid_position = True
        break

# Bottom edge: (n, j) for j from 2 to n-1
if not has_valid_position:
    for j in range(2, n):
        valid = True
        for r in range(1, n+1):
            if (r, j) in banned:
                valid = False
                break
        if valid:
            has_valid_position = True
            break

# Left edge: (i, 1) for i from 2 to n-1
if not has_valid_position:
    for i in range(2, n):
        valid = True
        for c in range(1, n+1):
            if (i, c) in banned:
                valid = False
                break
        if valid:
            has_valid_position = True
            break

# Right edge: (i, n) for i from 2 to n-1
if not has_valid_position:
    for i in range(2, n):
        valid = True
        for c in range(1, n+1):
            if (i, c) in banned:
                valid = False
                break
        if valid:
            has_valid_position = True
            break

print(1 if has_valid_position else 0)
```

This will output 1 if there's at least one valid non-corner border cell with a clear path, 0 otherwise.

Let me verify with the examples:

Example 1: banned={ (2,2) }
- (1,2): path includes (2,2) - banned, so invalid
- (3,2): path includes (2,2) - banned, invalid
- (2,1): path includes (2,2) - banned, invalid
- (2,3): path includes (2,2) - banned, invalid
- has_valid_position = False -> output 0 ✓

Example 2: banned = {}
- (1,2): path (1,2), (2,2), (3,2) - no banned, valid -> output 1 ✓

Example 3: banned = {(3,1), (3,2), (3,3)}
- (1,2): path (1,2), (2,2), (3,2), (4,2) - (3,2) is banned, invalid
- (1,3): path (1,3), (2,3), (3,3), (4,3) - (3,3) banned, invalid
- (4,2): path (4,2), (3,2), (2,2), (1,2) - (3,2) banned, invalid
- (4,3): path (4,3), (3,3), (2,3), (1,3) - (3,3) banned, invalid
- (2,1): path (2,1), (3,1), (4,1), (3,1) banned, invalid
- (3,1): is corner, not considered
- (2,4): path (2,4), (3,4), (4,4) - (3,4) is not banned, so valid -> has_valid_position = True -> output 1 ✓

Perfect.

Therefore, the final code is:

```python
n, m = map(int, input().split())
banned = set()
for _ in range(m):
    x, y = map(int, input().split())
    banned.add((x, y))

has_valid_position = False

# Check each valid non-corner border cell
# Top edge: (1, j) for j in [2, n-1]
for j in range(2, n):
    valid_path = True
    for r in range(1, n+1):
        if (r, j) in banned:
            valid_path = False
            break
    if valid_path:
        has_valid_position = True
        break

# Bottom edge: (n, j) for j in [2, n-1]
if not has_valid_position:
    for j in range(2, n):
        valid_path = True
        for r in range(1, n+1):
            if (r, j) in banned:
                valid_path = False
                break
        if valid_path:
            has_valid_position = True
            break

# Left edge: (i, 1) for i in [2, n-1]
if not has_valid_position:
    for i in range(2, n):
        valid_path = True
        for c in range(1, n+1):
            if (i, c) in banned:
                valid_path = False
                break
        if valid_path:
            has_valid_position = True
            break

# Right edge: (i, n) for i in [2, n-1]
if not has_valid_position:
    for i in range(2, n):
        valid_path = True
        for c in range(1, n+1):
            if (i, c) in banned:
                valid_path = False
                break
        if valid_path:
            has_valid_position = True
            break

print(1 if has_valid_position else 0)
```

You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

Gerald plays the following game. He has a checkered field of size n × n cells, where m various cells are banned. Before the game, he has to put a few chips on some border (but not corner) board cells. Then for n - 1 minutes, Gerald every minute moves each chip into an adjacent cell. He moves each chip from its original edge to the opposite edge. Gerald loses in this game in each of the three cases:  At least one of the chips at least once fell to the banned cell.  At least once two chips were on the same cell.  At least once two chips swapped in a minute (for example, if you stand two chips on two opposite border cells of a row with even length, this situation happens in the middle of the row). 

In that case he loses and earns 0 points. When nothing like that happened, he wins and earns the number of points equal to the number of chips he managed to put on the board. Help Gerald earn the most points.


-----Input-----

The first line contains two space-separated integers n and m (2 ≤ n ≤ 1000, 0 ≤ m ≤ 10^5) — the size of the field and the number of banned cells. Next m lines each contain two space-separated integers. Specifically, the i-th of these lines contains numbers x_{i} and y_{i} (1 ≤ x_{i}, y_{i} ≤ n) — the coordinates of the i-th banned cell. All given cells are distinct.

Consider the field rows numbered from top to bottom from 1 to n, and the columns — from left to right from 1 to n.


-----Output-----

Print a single integer — the maximum points Gerald can earn in this game.


-----Examples-----
Input
3 1
2 2

Output
0

Input
3 0

Output
1

Input
4 3
3 1
3 2
3 3

Output
1

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have an n×n chessboard with m banned cells
2. We can place chips only on border cells that are NOT corners (so only edge cells excluding corners)
3. Each chip moves from its starting position toward the opposite edge, one cell per minute
4. We lose if:
   - Any chip lands on a banned cell
   - Two chips occupy the same cell at any time
   - Two chips swap positions (they pass through each other)
5. We want to maximize the number of chips we can place

## Key Insights

1. **Border non-corner cells**: For an n×n grid, the border cells that are not corners are:
   - Top edge: rows=1, columns=2 to n-1
   - Bottom edge: rows=n, columns=2 to n-1  
   - Left edge: columns=1, rows=2 to n-1
   - Right edge: columns=n, rows=2 to n-1

2. **Movement pattern**: Each chip starts at a border cell and moves toward the opposite edge. For example:
   - A chip at (1, j) moves down to (n, j)
   - A chip at (n, j) moves up to (1, j)
   - A chip at (i, 1) moves right to (i, n)
   - A chip at (i, n) moves left to (i, 1)

3. **Swapping prevention**: Two chips swap if they start on opposite edges and move toward each other. This happens when:
   - One chip starts at (1, j) and moves down
   - Another chip starts at (n, k) and moves up
   - They meet in the middle at some time
   - This is a swap if they cross paths

4. **Collision prevention**: Two chips can't occupy the same cell at the same time.

5. **Key observation**: For chips moving in opposite directions (e.g., top-bottom or left-right), they will meet at some point. To avoid swaps, we need to be careful about which chips we place.

6. **Critical insight**: The chips on opposite edges can potentially interfere with each other. For chips on the top and bottom edges, they move vertically and will meet at some time. Similarly, left and right edge chips will meet. However, chips on the same edge moving in the same direction won't interfere with each other.

7. **Alternative insight**: Actually, the problem becomes simpler. We can place at most one chip per "path" that doesn't conflict. But more importantly, we need to check if any chip path hits a banned cell.

8. **Simpler approach**: We can try to place chips greedily. The constraint that doesn't allow two chips to swap suggests that we can't place chips on both sides of a path. 

9. **Key realization**: For any given direction (horizontal or vertical), chips on opposite edges don't directly conflict with each other in terms of swapping, but they do meet at some point. However, the problem states that when two chips swap, it's a loss. 

10. **Final insight**: Actually, looking at the examples:
    - In example 2 (3×3 with no bans), answer is 1
    - In example 3 (4×4 with 3 bans), answer is 1
    
    This suggests that we can often place at most 1 chip, unless there's some special configuration.

11. **Critical observation**: For any position on the border (excluding corners), when we place a chip there, it will move in a straight line toward the opposite edge. For a chip to avoid constraints:
    - It must not pass through any banned cell
    - No other chip can occupy the same cell at any time
    - No two chips can swap
    
    The main constraint is the banned cells. For a chip to be placed at position (r, c), we need to check if its entire path is clear.

12. **Conclusion**: We can place a chip at position (r, c) if and only if:
    - The position is on the border and not a corner
    - The entire path from start to opposite edge doesn't contain any banned cells
    - We can add this chip without creating conflicts with previously placed chips

13. **Conflict analysis**: Two chips on the same row (left-right) won't interfere because they're moving in the same direction and won't meet. Two chips on the same column (top-bottom) won't meet because they're moving in the same direction. However, a chip on top edge and a chip on bottom edge will meet at some point. Similarly for left and right edges.

14. **Final solution approach**: 
    - We can place chips independently as long as their paths don't contain banned cells
    - But we need to check for swaps: if two chips cross paths and swap, that's invalid
    - Actually, if two chips are on opposite edges (top-bottom or left-right), they will pass through each other, which is a swap
    
    This means we cannot place chips on both top and bottom edges, or on both left and right edges.

    Therefore, we have two main sets:
    - Top edge (1, 2) to (1, n-1)
    - Bottom edge (n, 2) to (n, n-1)
    - Left edge (2, 1) to (n-1, 1)
    - Right edge (2, n) to (n-1, n)

    We can choose at most one of the two opposite pairs (top-bottom or left-right).

    So the maximum is: max(available_top_bottom_chips, available_left_right_chips)

15. **Algorithm**:
    - For each available border non-corner position, check if its path is clear of banned cells
    - For top-bottom pairs, check if any chip path goes through a banned cell
    - Count how many chips we can place in each direction
    - Take the maximum of these counts
